783. THOMAS WINTER , GEORGE TURNER, alias George Body, and WILLIAM CLARKE , were indicted for stealing on the 12th of January, 31/2lbs. of pork, value 2 s. 4 d. the goods of Thomas Johnson; and that Clarke had been previously convicted of felony.
CHARLES TURNER . I am in the service of Thomas Johnson, a butcher, in Hackney-road. On Saturday, the 12th of January, about nine o'clock in the evening, Kemp the officer came to the shop, and in consequence of what he said, I looked outside the shop, and missed two pieces of pork, which I had seen safe two minutes before.
GEORGE KEMP ( police-constable N 82.) I was on duty in the Hackney-road, on the evening of the 12th of January, and a little before nine o'clock the three prisoners passed me—I knew them before, and watched them for about twenty minutes—I saw them all three pass Johnson's shop—Turner turned back and took two pieces of pork out of a dish in front of the shop, and gave it to Clarke, who gave it to Winter—I crossed the road directly and took Winter—as I crossed the road he said to Clarke, "D—your eyes, go back and get some more"—I took Winter to the station-house with the pork—I took him on Monday to the office—I saw Clarke and Turner waiting outside there, and secured them.
Turner's Defence. I was at home and in bed at the time he says he
saw me—I was standing about Spitalfields market, where I get my living by portering, on the Monday, and followed the mob to the office, seeing two lads whose faces I knew—I just went in to look at them, and was taken for this robbery, of which I know nothing.
Clarke's Defence. On the Monday after the robbery I was passing through Worship-street, when the policeman took hold of me, and charged me with this robbery—I know nothing of it—I was at home and at work at the time.
DENNIS POWER ( police-constable H 18.) I was a witness against a person named George Clarke, who was tried here in April last year—the prisoner Clarke is that person—I am sure of it—I have a certificate of his conviction, which I got from Mr. Clark's office—( read.)
Clarke. It must be a mistake, I was never here before. Witness. I am sure he is the person—I apprehended him myself at his mother's door—it was for stealing five pairs of clogs—he was tried in the New Court, and got six months' imprisonment—I heard him sentenced—I have not the smallest doubt he is the person—his mother is now outside, and she has been talking to me about it this morning.
WINTER *— GUILTY . Aged 15. TURNER *— GUILTY . Aged 18.
Confined Three Months. CLARKE *— GUILTY . Aged 16.— Transported for Seven Years.

784. WILLIAM ABRAY was indicted for stealing, on the 7th of february, 2 gallons of gin, value 1 l. 1 s., and 1 pint of rum, value 2 s. 6 d., the goods of Richard Moss, his master.
RICHARD MOSS . I keep the Adam and Eve, in St. John-street-road—the prisoner lived with me about nine days. On the 7th of February, after I was gone to bed, I heard a noise in the house—I went down, and called out "Hollo"—he made no answer—at last he said, "It's me"—I said, "Who is me?"—he said, "William"—he said he was ill—he was in the scullery—I kicked my foot against a bottle of gin, and asked what it was—he said water—I asked what he was going to do with it—he said he did not know—I found both the windows of the bar unfastened, which I had fastened before I went to bed—he had got in at one window just under a door, and opened the door—I brought the gin away, called in a policeman, and he shewed us the tub he took the gin out of—I found a bottle of rum in the kitchen, and a funnel wrapped up in one of the children's dresses—he had broken the window by falling down, and that disturbed me.
FREDERICK CHAPPELL ( police-constable G 221.) I apprehended the prisoner, and have the gin. (The prisoner received a good character.)
GUILTY . Aged 17.— Confined Three Months.

EDWARD DE LIMA WOODS . I am a pawnbroker, and live in King-street, Hammersmith. On the 30th of January, I hung this shawl up in my shop, but did not miss it till the 31st—the prisoner was a customer at my shop.
HENRY BRITTAIN . I am shopman to Mr. Watts, a pawnbroker, at
Hammersmith. I have a shawl pawned on the 30th of January by the prisoner, for 2 s., in the name of Eliza Williams, No. 4, King-street—I knew her before—she had been a customer a long time.

BENJAMIN LINO . I am shopman to William Nathan, a pawnbroker, in Three Colt-street, Limehouse. I missed a pair of trowsers from the door, and went to Ratcliff-highway—I saw the prisoner in company with two other persons—the prisoner separated from them—I followed him till I met an officer, and then gave him in charge—he was searched at the station-house, and the trowsers found secreted under his coat, round his body—he said he bought them in Rosemary-lane—I had seen them sale at two o'clock.
LOWTHER JACKSON . I am a tailor, and live in Three Colt-street On the 7th of February, about six o'clock, I saw the prisoner with two others pass my door—the prisoner looked into my shop—I went to the door, and they got to Nathan's, which is two doors from me—I went in-doors, and sent my son to inform Mr. Nathan that suspicious characters were at his door, and when I got back to the door they were gone—they were followed, and the prisoner was taken,
THOMAS SQUIRE . I am a policeman. I took him into custody and found the trowsers concealed under his jacket.
( Property produced and sworn to.) GUILTY .— Confined One Month.

789. JAMES HURLEY was indicted for forging a request for the delivery of 5000 of twenty-penny clasp nails, and 6000 ten-penny clasp nails, with intent to defraud Samuel Bradney.
RICHARD BODDINOTON . I am shopman to Samuel Bradney, an ironmonger. About half-past three o'clock on Friday, the 1st of February, Mr. Bradney brought this order to me in the warehouse, and in consequence of what he said to me, I went into the shop and saw the prisoner—I had the order in my hand—I asked him if he wanted to take the nails with him—he said he would—this is the order—( read)—"Please let the bearer have 5000 twenty-penny clasp nails, and 5000 ten-penny ditto, put them in a bag. For William Brooks, builder, No. 1, Fourawl-court; Rosemary-lane"—Mr. Brooks deals with Mr. Bradney—I did not give the
prisoner the nails—we suspected the order was not correct, and I went up to Mr. Brooks and asked him if it was his order—I asked the prisoner where he was to take the nails to—he said to a public-house (I forget the name of it) in Osborne-street—I asked if they were to be used there—he did not answer me directly, but after a bit he said they were not to be used there, but there was a man waiting there for them.
Cross-examined by MR. DOANE. Q. Did he not tell you he was to take them to the Three Compasses, in Brick-lane? A. Yes, he said the man would be there who had sent him with the order.
WILLIAM BROOKS . I am a builder, and live in Ables-buildings, Rosemary-lane. I deal with Mr. Bradney—I do not know the prisoner to my knowledge—I may have seen him—this order is not my writing—it is a forgery.
Cross-examined. Q. Is it the handwriting of any body in your establishment? A. No, nobody wrote it by my direction.
WILLIAM BROOKS, JUN . I am a son of the last witness. This order is not my writing—I did not authorize the prisoner to take it—I know nothing about it.
SAMUEL BRADNEY . On Friday afternoon, about half-past three o'clock, the prisoner came to my shop and presented this order to me—I made inquiry, and afterwards took him into custody.
JURY. Q. On his stating he was to carry these nails to a public-house, did you go there to make inquiry? A. I did not.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18390204-790]
790. SAMUEL HARVEY was indicted for stealing, on the 2nd of February, I dead fowl, value 2 s. 6 d., the goods of James Morris.
JAMES MORRIS . I am a poulterer and cheesemonger, and live in Church-street, Hackney. On the 2nd of February, in consequence of information from the witness Ogan, I looked at the poultry that was exposed for sale, and missed a fowl—I went with Ogan to the Old Mermaid—he there pointed out the prisoner to me, and told me his name, and said, "That is the person that stole the fowl"—I said, "Come, Mr. Harvey, give me the fowl, let us have no bother about it, all I want is my fowl"—he denied all knowledge of it—I saw a fowl afterwards taken out from between some water-butts.
Prisoner. Q. Will you swear it was your fowl? A. I will not swear to it, but I believe it to be mine.
WILLIAM HENRY OGAN . I am a shoemaker, and live in Church-street, Hackney. I was standing at my father's door on Saturday evening, the 2nd of February, directly opposite the prosecutor's—I saw the prisoner pass with three others, and as he passed, he took a fowl from the prosecutor's window, passed it along to the other three, and turned up the old Mermaid-yard, which is close by—I immediately ran and informed the prosecutor, told him the prisoner's name, and went with him into the Mermaid public-house, where I found the prisoner, with others, in the parlour—the water-butts, where the for was taken from, are up the yard, a little distance from the door—the prisoner would have an opportunity of putting it there before he went into the house.
THOMAS OAKES . I am a policeman. I took the prisoner into custody at the Mermaid, and found the fowl between two water-butts in the back yard.
Prisoner's Defence. I had been at work for two days, at Isle worth, and had been with three others to take our money on this day—we went into the Mermaid to meet my wife, and directly we sat down, the policeman came and took me—the prosecutor said if he only had his fowl, he would say nothing more about it—if I had been guilty, I should have told him of it without being locked up.
GUILTY . Aged 26.— Confined Six Months.
[trialAccount t18390204-791]

[trialAccount t18390204-796]
796. FREDERICK LAWSON and JOHN SPENCER HIBBS were indicted for burglariously breaking and entering the dwelling-house of Lewis Anwell (since deceased,) about two o'clock in the night of the 14th of January, at St. Mary Matfelon, alias Whitechapel, and stealing therein 5 watches, value 10 l.;17 spoons, value 15 s.; 2 brooches, value 15 s.; 1 watch-key, value 2 s. 6 d.;2 pairs of ear-rings, value 10 s.; 4 handkerchiefs, value 10 s.; 72 pence, and 100 halfpence; the goods and monies of the said Lewis Anwell.
MR. PHILLIPS conducted the Prosecution,
HARRIET ANWELL . I am the widow of Lewis Anwell. He was alive at the time of this robbery, but very ill indeed—my house is the Halfmoon and Punchbowl, in Buckle-street, in the parish of St. Mary Matfelon Whitechapel—I know the prisoners—they, with two others, named Nathan and Gardener, were at my house on the 14th of January—Lawson was employed in the house as pot-boy—at one o'clock, on the morning of the 14th of January, he helped me to put the shutters up—I heard some noise in the course of the night, which alarmed me—I suppose it was about ten minutes before three o'clock—it was as if something heavy had fallen into an empty room—such a noise as a man would make in falling from a window—I came down, about eleven o'clock in the morning, and went into the bar—I found my cash-box broken open, and missed from it a gold watch, four silver watches, a quantity of spoons, a trinket-box, with rings and different things in it—the value of all I lost is about 13 l.—I have since seen one of the watches, but none of the other property, except what was in the possession of Lawson's brother—I had taken nearly 200 l. up stairs with me the night before, and that I had with me up stairs—I went into the long room, where there is a sky-light—I found the pulley of the sky-light drawn up, and the birch-broom put up, to keep it up—that window opens by a pulley inside—by pulling a rope, the window would go up—I had left that window shut, when I went to bed, and all the house shut up—I had done it myself—Lawson went round with me—I had left that room safe at half-past one o'clock—Lawson did not sleep in the house—there is a wall and a water-butt outside the house.
HENRY BROWN . I am the prosecutrix's father. On the night before the robbery, I saw Gardener, Lawson, and Hibbs in my daughter's house—I saw Lawson and Gardener in the long room, trying to pull up the sky-light by the rope—Hibbs was standing by them, and he said they should pull the other rope, and that would take the sky-light up—I saw them again on the Monday night all together, Lawson, Gardener, Nathan, and Hibbs—that was the night as the robbery was discovered in the morning—I went into the long room in the morning, and found the sky-light up, the broom under it, and the door open.
NAOMI ANWELL . I am the prosecutrix's daughter. I was the first person that opened the bar on the morning of the 15th of January—I did so at eight o'clock—I found it shut—I unlocked it, and unscrewed the shutters.
WILLIAM NORMOYLE ( police-sergeant H 15.) I was called in by the prosecutrix to examine the house, on the Tuesday morning, the 15th—I found the sky-light, in the long room, open, and the birch-broom under it, to keep it so—I also saw a small door, which leads from the long room into the bar, forced open, and a drawer in the bar forced open—it appeared to me that the entrance had been made through the sky-light, because the dust on the frame of it was rubbed off, as if a person had dropped from there into the room—it is a few yards from the floor—a person falling, or dropping from it to the floor, would necessarily make some noise—I took Hibbs into custody—he said he was innocent of it.
ALLEN PIPE ( police-constable H 51.) On the night of the 17th of January I went to a house, in Craig's-court, Whitechapel, and took a young
woman into custody, and found the duplicate of a watch in the coal-hole, under the stairs—in consequence of information, I went to the lodging of George Lawson, in Kingsland-road, and got a ticket—I also found a watch-key there.
GEORGE LAWSON . I am the prisoner's brother, and lodge in Orange-street, Bethnal-green-road, and work for a floor-cloth manufacturer. One day in January, my brother produced a watch to me—I exchanged mine for it, on trial—I afterwards pawned it at Mr. Cotton's, in Hackney-road.
SAMUEL WALKER . I am in the employ of Mr. Cotton, a pawnbroker, in Hackney-road. I have a watch, which was pawned by George Lawson, on the 17th of January.
CORNELIUS FOAY ( police-constable H 98.) I took Lawson into custody. I asked him if he knew Gardener—he said he did not—I said, "He was in your company at Mr. Anwell's, on the morning of the robbery"—he said he did not know him.
MART WRIGHT . I am the wife of John Wright, and lire in the same house as the prisoner Lawson. On Tuesday morning, the 15th of January, I saw Lawson and Gardener together, in Lawson's room—Gardener sat in the room by the fire side—I did not hear any conversation between them.
JOHN BRIGHTWELL . I am a shoemaker, and live in Buckle-square, Buckle-street. I know Nathan, Lawson, and Hibbs—I have seen them together at the prosecutrix's house—on Tuesday morning, the 15th of January, I was awoke by the top of the water-butt falling off—I looked out of window, it was rather a dark morning—I cannot tell what hour it was—(I was very poorly—I had been taking medicine, and went to bed very early that evening)—I saw three or four men—I saw three, and heard another one—I thought I heard voices, which I knew; one was Nathan's, and another Lawson's—I could not say exactly whose voices the others were—I could see three men, and heard a fourth voice—I saw Nathan, and I am almost sure I heard Lawson's voice—I saw Nathan and Hibbs on the wall—on the Saturday night before the robbery, I was in the long room, and saw Lawson, Hibbs, Nathan, and another, there—I saw Hibbs put the broom under the sky-light, to open it—I saw Hibbs take and pull a rope, and say, "This is the way"—he said so to Nathan—I had frequently seen the prisoners there before—I never saw them in my passage.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. Do you know a person who goes by the name of Mad Jack? A. Not to ray knowledge—I never heard any one call me so—I do not very often go to this long room.
MRS. ANWELL re-examined. This watch is one of those which was taken out of my bar—it is mine.
Lawson. I have witnesses to prove that this man Brightwell has false-sworn himself.
LAWSON — GUILTY . Aged 27.— Transported for Fifteen Years.
HIBBS — NOT GUILTY .

CHARLES WOODHAM . I am in the service of Mr. Boyce, pawnbroker, in Great Charles-street, New Cut. These spectacles were taken in by a young man who has left us, and this is the duplicate of them—( looking at one.)

800. JOSHUA HARWOOD was indicted for stealing, on the 11th of January, 2561bs. weight of tallow, value 7 l. 10 s., the goods of Morris Parry and others, his masters: and JOHN MARCH , for feloniously receiving the same, well knowing it to have been stolen; against the Statute, &c.
MORRIS PARRY . I am one of the firm of Catherine Parry and Sons. We are tallow-chandlers in Fleur-de-lis-court, Spitalfields—Harwood was our errand-boy for about three months—on Friday morning, the 11th of January, our man found a sack in the cellar, which he brought up to me—it had the appearance of having had tallow in it—I desired him to put it where he found it—about five o'clock, Harwood left to go to tea—I followed, with our man—he went into the shop of the prisoner March, in Lamb-street—it is an oil-shop—I went in, and saw Harwood and March there, and the bag which I had seen before, was on the weighing-machine—Harwood was taking the money off the counter as I went in, and March was behind the counter—the bag contained thirty-six pounds of Russia tallow—I asked Harwood what he did there—he said he came to buy soap and soda—I pointed to the bag—he then immediately began to cry, and begged I would forgive him, as his father had died that morning, and he did it to buy a coffin—I told March I was surprised he should buy tallow of such a boy
as that—he made me no answer—he was serving a customer at the time—he asked me to walk into his parlour—I did so, and he told me he bought it in consequence of the boy representing that his father was a pastry-cook, and this was his perquisite, that he said it was dripping, and he bought it for dripping—I said I thought that was impossible, for I remembered him coming to our shop to buy candles, and putting them to his nose to try the quality—I thought he could not make that mistake—I left the shop, and gave Harwood into custody—I went again and saw March—I asked him how much he had of it—he shewed me two casks which had been in his possession—I asked if that was the whole he had got—he said he believed that was the whole—I said he must know if he had any more—he said, no, he believed he had not—Harwood was not there then—Mrs. March came, and asked me to look over the place—I went into the passage, and found another cask—I said "You forgot this"—he said "Yes, I had forgotten that"—I asked him what he had given the boy for it—he said 21/2 d. per pound, as dripping—I said that was a very inferior price, as we were giving, for common kitchen-stuff, 36 s. and 38 s. per cwt—I told him it cost us 60 s. per cwt., which would be about 61/2 d. per pound—I then took March into custody—there was about 21/2 cwt. altogether in the three casks—I believe it to be my tallow.
Cross-examined by MR. BODKIN. Q. Did he not say "I had no idea it was tallow?" A. Yes—I searched a warehouse of his, at a distance from the shop, at his wife's request, but found nothing there—the two casks I saw were in the shop—one was under the counter, the other, I think, had a cover on, but I am not sure—they were old butter-firkins, such as are used to put kitchen-stuff in—the third cask was in the passage leading to the warehouse—I had to pass it to go to the warehouse—21/2 d. per pound is what is given in shops for kitchen-stuff.
COURT. Q. Is Russia tallow like kitchen-stuff? A. Not at all to me, either in tight or smell, but I am a tallow-chandler.
EDWIN RICHARD . I am in the prosecutor's service. I found this bag in the cellar—I showed it to my master, and replaced it where I found it, by his desire—the prisoner went down into the cellar about dinner time—I went down soon after, and missed the bag—I afterwards saw the prisoner come out with the bag on his shoulder, and watched him to March's shop.
GEORGE BUCKHAM . I am in the prosecutor's service. I saw Harwood take away the bag, and followed him into March's shop—I stood outside, and looked through the window—I saw Harwood put the bag on the machine, and March weighed it—it was then placed against a salt-bin—March came to the counter, and paid Harwood—I cannot say how much, it was silver—Harwood took a reckoner from his pocket, looked at it, and said that was right—the bag was not opened at all—I then called my master—I heard Harwood say he came there to buy soap and soda—he afterwards admitted that he took it to buy his father a coffin.
Cross-examined. Q. I believe his father is dead? A. Yes—he had died that morning between seven and eight o'clock.
WILLIAM SHAW ( police-constable H 112.) I was sent for and took March into custody—I was there some time—he said he was ill—I was ordered to stay by him till the surgeon was sent for—he came and examined him, and he was taken to the station-house—he said it was a bad job.
MR. BODKIN called
THOMAS CODD . I am an oil-man, and live in Chiswell-street. I have known March seventeen or eighteen years—he has borne a most excellent character—the shop in Lamb-street was taken by his son about three months ago to carry on the business of an Italian warehouseman which his employers are—the prisoner was only there to take care of the goods for his son—I valued the stock when the shop was taken—I do not think the prisoner knew the name of one article in the shop—he came and asked me several times to tell him what different things were—the shop was advertised to be let shortly after, and the prisoner continued to have charge of it, till a tenant was found—I deal in kitchen-stuff and dripping—it is sold at 2 d. a lb. in retail shops—not one person in twenty out of the trade would know the difference between dripping and Russia tallow—children generally bring such things.
— MILLER. I am an oil-man. I occupied the shop before it was let to the prisoner's son—the prisoner expressed his utter ignorance of the quality and name of most of the things—I was often called in to tell the name of things, and I expressed my opinion that he was very unfit to remain there—I deal in kitchen-stuff and tallow—there is a great deal of difference in kitchen-stuff in a melted state—a man might mistake tallow for it.
— PEACOCK. I have been in the melting trade, and have had large dealings with tallow-chandlers—I think it very difficult to distinguish tallow from kitchen-stuff—I have bought kitchen-stuff as good as any tallow—I knew the prisoner as a grocer, and believe he was not at all acquainted with these things.
JOHN JENKINS . I am a trunk-maker, and live in Liverpool-street, Bishopsgate-street. I recommended the prisoner's son to this shop.
THOMAS WILLIAM MARCH . I am the prisoner's son, and am in the employ of Kennett and Eaton. I took the shop in Lamb-street some months ago, to carry on the pickle and foreign fruit trade—I altered my intention, and advertised to let it—my father had no interest in the trade whatever—he was only there till I could let the shop—he was not at all acquainted with tallow or kitchen-stuff—he never saw any till he came there.
CHARLES EATON . I am one of the firm of Kennett and Eaton. The prisoner's son was in our employ—he took this shop in Lamb-street, and the father was merely there till his son could let it again—the father was more than twelve months in our employ—a more honest man never lived, but he had no knowledge of the business.
(Elizabeth Hammond, the wife of a sawyer, and Daniel Forbes, a journeyman weaver, gave Harwood a good character.)
HARWOOD — GUILTY . Aged 13.— Transported for Seven Years to the Isle of Wight.
MARCH — NOT GUILTY .

803. WILLIAM LONG was indicted for stealing, on the 20th of January, 901bs. weight of lead, value 1 l. 1 s., the goods of John Little; and fixed to a building.

ELIZABETH DARBY . I lent the prisoner's wife a bag on the 20th of January, not knowing what it was for.
MAURICE CALNAN . I am a policeman. On the evening of the 20th of January I was on duty in Hackney-road, near Weymouth-terrace, and saw the prisoner with something bulky on his shoulder—I followed, and asked what he had got—he said some lead—I said, was it sheet lead or pipe—he said he did not know—I said, "Where did you get it?"—he said, "From a man of the name of Palmer"—I took him to the station-house, and found it was new sheet-lead recently cut—I found a knife in his pocket, the blade of which was scratched as if it had cut some hard substance—the owner of the lead came the next day—I went to the building in Weymouth-terrace, and found the lead corresponded exactly with the gutter.
Prisoner. I had it given me by Mr. Palmer.
GUILTY . Aged 25.— Transported for Seven Years.

804. WILLIAM THOMSON was indicted for stealing, on the 30th of January, 1 spoon, value 3 s., the goods of John Lucena Kettle.

in old plate, and I heard Emanuel, the man of the shop, say he would have nothing to do with it—the prisoner then went to Lazarus, a dealer in old clothes, and offered the spoon—he came out—I followed him, and asked him where he got it from—he said from his brother's—I asked him where he lived—he told me a place which I found to be false.

805. CORNELIUS NEALE was indicted for stealing, on the 27th of January, 1 half-sovereign, 4 half-crowns, 7 shillings, and 1 sixpence, the monies of Thomas Henley, from his person.
THOMAS HENLEY . I work in a factory. On the 27th of January I went to a wake —the prisoner and Catharine Bryant were there—I had not known either of them before—I had a half-sovereign, four half-crowns, and 7 s. 6 d. in silver loose in my waistcoat pocket—I sat close by Bryant when I went in, and the prisoner sat at the back of us—I had taken out my money, for I had the "hiccups," and Bryant said to me, "Young man, you owe me a shilling"—I said, "My good woman, I never saw you before, but if I owed you a shilling I could pay you," and I took out my money to show that I had money—after that I felt a hand at my pocket, but I fell asleep from half-past two o'clock till half-past five o'clock in the morning, and when I awoke the prisoner and Bryant were both gone—I got up, and felt that all my money was gone, and my pocket had been cut—I suspected Bryant, and had her taken—I then had the prisoner taken, and 4 s. 6 d. was found on him, also 51/2 d., and a knife.
Prisoner. Q. Did you look at your pocket when you felt something at it? A. No—I had not the least thought that there would be a thief in such a house.
CATHERINE BRYANT . I saw the prosecutor at the wake —I said he owed me a shilling, (which is the custom of our country people to do,) to frighten | him out of the hiccups—he then showed his money—the prisoner was sitting behind, and he was looking behind the prosecutor when he showed me his money—he then came, and sat alongside the prosecutor—then the prosecutor fell asleep across the prisoner—the prisoner was groping about the prosecutor's pockets—I was afraid to mention this at the time, there being so many people there.
Prisoner. Q. Why did not you tell the man when I did it? A. Because you had a great many friends there.
WILLIAM SLADDEN ( police-constable K 50.) I took the prisoner about seven o'clock in the morning, on the 27th of January—I found on him 4 s. 6 d. in silver, 51/2 d. in copper, and a knife.
GUILTY .* Aged 28.— Transported for Ten Years.

808. MARY DAWS was indicted for stealing, on the 29th of January, 1 pair of slippers, value 1 s., the goods of John Clarke; and that she had been before convicted of felony.
JOHN CLARKE . I am a shoe-maker, and live in Shoreditch. On the 29th of January I received information that some person had taken a pair of slippers from the door—I sent my boy after the prisoner—he came back with her and the slippers—she said, if I would let her go she would pay for them, and buy four pairs more—there was no policeman near, and I let her go—about an hour afterwards I found her at the station-house—these are the slippers—she raved and went on so I thought she was mad.
JAMES GAINER . I live with the prosecutor—he sent me after the prisoner—she had got about 100 yards from the shop, and was carrying the slippers in her muff—I told her she had taken a pair of slippers from my master's—she said, "I will pay for them, and buy four pairs more, if you will let me go"—she did not buy the four pairs.
MARTIN ROOTS ( police-constable H 170.) I took the prisoner on the 29th of January, on suspicion of having stolen property in her possession.
Prisoner. They were shirts that I had been making for my brother.

(The prisoner received a good character.)

809. JOSEPH CHITTENDEN was indicted for embezzlement.
MR. PRENDERGAST conducted the Prosecution.
JAMES GRIFFITHS . I am a brewer, and live at Islington. The prisoner was engaged by me as a collecting clerk, at 1 l. a week, and 1 s. for each new customer he brought—these terms were afterwards changed to 30 s. a week—he was to receive the money for the ale and beer delivered to the customers, and to pay me that money on the same night, when he returned, and was to enter in a book what he had received in the day—I have that book here, in his own hand-writing—on the 15th of November here is no sum entered by him as received of Mr. Leach, of Broad-street; nor did he on that day pay me 5 s. as so received; nor has he at any time paid it me—he entered other sums as received that day, and paid them to me, but not that—it was his duty to take out, for the purpose of giving receipts to the customers, a little book, containing blank forms of receipts—he ought not to receive money without giving one of these forms, or a bill with a printed head—he is not my partner.
Cross-examined by MR. DOANE. Q. How long had he been in your service? A. About three months—he received 5 l. or 6 l. or 8 l. or 10 l. a day—there are several entries by him on the 15th of November—I think he left me on the 24th of January—I am not aware that any customers left me when he did—his brother came to me shortly after he left—I think I found out about this 5 s. at out three weeks after he left—his brother did not complain that I had been spreading reports against the prisoner's honesty—he said, if there was any thing wrong he would make it right—another brother of the prisoner told me where I could find him—when his brother called on me I knew of one charge, but not of this.
ROBERT LEACH . I am a plumber, and live in Broad-street. On the 15th of November I paid the prisoner five shillings on account of his master, Mr. Griffiths—I have his receipt here—I saw him write it—I knew it was the custom to give printed receipts—I looked at this receipt when he gave it me—I forget the words I made use of, but I know the prisoner said, "I have given you this on a blank piece of paper, as I have not any printed receipts with me," and he said, "I am a partner with Mr. Griffiths."
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18390204-810]
810. JOHN SINFIELD was indicted for stealing, on the 5th of february, 1 metal cap, value 3 s., the goods of the London Dock Company.
MR. BALLANTINE conducted the Prosecution.
HUGH SMITH . I am assistant-foreman at the London Docks. On the 5th of February I was going towards the North-quay, and saw the prisoner, who was a stranger—I made some inquiries of him, and he walked away—I followed him, and asked what he wanted—he said, "Nothing"—he had a blue coat on, over a smock frock—I asked what he had got under his coat—he said, "Nothing"—I put my hand under it, and found this piece of brass—I saw a belt under his frock, his frock was turned in, and that supported the piece of metal—I asked where he got it—he said he picked it up, and pointed to a place where it came from—he then began to cry, and begged my pardon.
DANIEL RUDKINS . I am a constable of the London Docks. On the 5th of February I took the prisoner from the witness, with this metal cap—the prisoner said he picked it up—I went to examine the jiggers and crabs, and I found a deficiency, and this piece fitted exactly—the nuts and staples were left in the crab, and this had been taken away—he was not in the employ of the Docks, and had no right there.
JAMES BARLOW . I am foreman-millwright at the London Docks. I consider this cap belongs to the Docks—I had missed two caps the day before, and that day I missed one.
GUILTY . Aged 21,— Confined Three Months.
