811. JOHN JONES , WILLIAM WESTCOMB , and ABRAHAM WAFFINGTON , were indicted for stealing, on the 5th of February, 601bs. weight of rope, value 3 s., the goods of the London Dock Company.
MR. BALLANTINE conducted the Prosecution.
TIMOTHY CRAWLEY . I live in Angel-garden, Shadwell, and am a labourer in the London Docks occasionally. On the 5th of February I saw Waffington, who I had known five or six years, on the Eas-quay—he asked me to go and get some old junk, pointing to a shed close by—he said it would fetch 8 s. a cwt., and he would make a pass himself to get it out—I said I would not do such a thing for him, or any body else, as I had once been in trouble for beating a policeman—he said I was not game —I then left him, and stood behind a crane, and watched—in about two hours I saw him come out of the shed, which he had pointed out to me—he had a piece of rope with him, which he put behind a pile of wood—he then went back, and got some more rope—the other two prisoners then came out, with each of them some rope, and chucked it down in the same place, behind the wood—I saw them all talking together—I told the dock-officers of it—it was between half-past twelve and one o'clock.
Waffington. Q. How often have you been in the House of Correction? A. Only once—I worked at the Docks last Monday week.
GEORGE DIX . I am an officer of the London Docks. Between one and two o'clock, on the 5th of February, Crawley made a communication to me—I went to the East-quay, and he pointed out Jones and Westcomb—I took them into custody—they said they knew nothing about it—I went to the wood, and found 60lbs. weight of rope.
CHARLES SLATER . I am an officer. I went in search of Waffington, and found him coming out of a cheque-clerk's box—I told him he was charged with stealing rope—he said he knew nothing about it.
WILLIAM LANCASTER . I am foreman of the spirit quay. I examined the pile of foreign junk in the shed, and il looked as if the corner had been pulled down—this is part of it.
Westcomb. I went to see a cousin at the Docks, and was taken.
Waffington. I went there for work, and was taken.
NOT GUILTY .
OLD COURT.— Thursday, February 14 th, 1830.
Fourth Jury, before Mr. Recorder.
[trialAccount t18390204-812]
812. RICHARD VINCENT was indicted for stealing, on the 4th of December, at St. Mary Matfelon, alias Whitechapel, 1820lbs. weight of butter, value 100 l., and 20 casks, value 1 l. the goods of Joseph Barber.—Another COUNT, stating it to be the goods of John Rice; and JAMES NORTHCOTE , GEORGE HOPKINS . FRANCIS TOY , WILLIAM CHRISTALL, alias Griesback, and JAMES GREGSON , for feloniously receiving the same, collectively, well knowing the same to have been stolen; against the Statute, &c.—Another set of COUNTS, charging each with receiving part of the said goods separately.—Another set of COUNTS, charging each with a substantive felony, in receiving part of said goods.—Another COUNT, charging them, collectively, with inciting, &c. the said Richard Vincent to commit the said felony.—Another set of COUNTS, charging each separately with inciting the said Richard Vincent to commit the said felony.—Another COUNT against them, collectively, for harbouring the said Richard Vincent, knowing him to have committed the said first-mentioned felony.—Another set of COUNTS, charging each, separately, with harbouring, &c. the said Richard Vincent, knowing him to have committed the said felony.
MESSRS. CLARKSON, BODKIN, and CHAMBERS conducted the Prosecution.
MICHAEL SULLIVAN . In December last I was in the service of Mr. Hill, a town-carman, and drove one of his carts—I went out daily, and took a stand among other carts, to be hired—I was standing in Thames-street, on the 4th of December, and saw the prisoner Vincent in Thames-street—I had known him before—it was about eleven o'clock, as near as I can guess—I had been doing a little job for a gentleman in Thames-street, and had some beer with five or six others, at the Lion and Key—Vincent was there—I saw him again about half an hour after—he said he had got some friends coming down for twenty casks of butter, and as I had nothing to do would I do it—I asked him whether it was Priestley's work (Priestley is a master carman, who generally does the butter work)—he said, "No," it was not Priestley's—he went away then for about a quarter of an hour, then returned, and gave me a note—he said there was twenty tubs of butter all ready to put into the cart, in the new warehouse, and they knowed about them—he did not mention Barber's quay.
Q. Look at this, is it the order ho gave you? A. I cannot say, because I never opened it—it was doubled up like this—I can read writing a little—when we had this conversation we were close to one part of Mr. Barber's warehouses—the warehouse I received the butter at is called the New warehouse, because it has been lately built—I went there—I did not take my cart at first—I gave the order to a young man there—he went and showed it to Mr. Murphy, in the warehouse—Murphy told him to go to the counting-house and show it, which was done—the young man came back, and said I might have the twenty tubs, I am pretty sure he said twenty tubs—I went and fetched my cart.
Q. Where was Vincent when you went with your horse and cart? A. He was in Thames-street at the time, he was ten or twelve yards from the warehouse.
Q. From you? A. Yes—when I brought the horse and cart to the warehouse, he stood about the same distance—the twenty tubs were put into the cart—I saw Vincent while the cart was loading—he stood close against the tail of the cart—I had some mats over the horse and on the copse of the cart—when the butter was put in, Vincent got to the off-side of the cart, and moved the mats more back into the cart, which would partly cover the tubs, but not all—my master then came up to me, and asked where I was going—I told him—I then proceeded with the cart over Tower-hill—Vincent ordered me to take the road to Whitechapel—he kept on the near side of the way—he walked on the path, and I rode on the near side of the cart—I went on till I got near Whitechapel-church—I saw Vincent most all the way, and I found him there when I got there—he asked, by Whitechapel-church, if I had got money to pay the toll—I said
"No"he said he would get some—he then left me—he kept on, and spoke to some man going along—I cannot say who it was, as his back was towards me—I lost sight of him for some time—I went on to Mile-end-gate, and stopped at Mr. Man's, a little brewhouse, and called for halt a pint of beer—Vincent came up and said, "Make it a pint of beer"—he gave me 6 d. to pay the toll, and told me to go to the King's Arms, down the road—I went—he kept on walking—when I got to the King's Arms Vincent went in—he came out again, and said the parties were not there—we had some beer there, and dined—we staid there an hour and a half, or from that to two hours—while there a man named Roberts came up and joined Vincent—Roberts lived in North-street, Whitechapel, and kept a Tom and Jerry-shop, a beer-shop and chandler's-shop—I said that I was stopping there too long, and told Vincent I was squandering my time, I wanted to get back again—Vincent came out again—Roberts was outside and spoke to him—we turned round, and went down a lane at the side of this public-house—it might be a quarter of a mile up that lane—we stopped at a public-house there—Vincent and Roberts were there—I saw two more men there—I knew one of them by sight—it was neither of the prisoners—I saw a van near that public-house, with one horse in it—nothing was done with the butter there—the van drove away—Vincent and they all went away with the van and left me there—and in a quarter of an hour or twenty minutes Vincent came back, and told me to go on—he got on the cart itself, and drove it down another turning out of the lane, and there I found a van—I cannot say whether it was the same—Vincent and Roberts moved the butter out of my cart into the van, and drove on.
Q. Did the turning they went down take them out of sight of the public-house? A. Yes—there was one horse in the van, I think—I did not assist to move the butter into the van—I did the ropes up—Roberts and the two men went off with the van—it was about one hundred yards from the public-house—Vincent remained with me—I spoke about the money just before the van went, and Roberts told Vincent to pay me—after the van was gone, Vincent told me, as we came along, if they asked me where the butter went, to say it went in a van with four horses—he returned with me in my cart—we did not stop any where till we got to Roberts's house, in North-street—I went in there, and called for a pint of beer, and Vincent went into the chandler's-shop side—he came outside after that, and paid me a sovereign—the proper charge for the cart was 15 s.—he said I was to give my master the 15 s., and the 5 s. was for myself—I gave my master 15 s., and kept 5 s.—we came into Whitechapel-road afterwards, and had a quartern and a half of rum—I kept 3 s. 3 d., had the rum, and gave Vincent 1 s.—I told my matter the butter was put into a van with four horses.
Q. Did you stop at the King's Arms at Bow as you return? A. We did not come that way.
Cross-examined by MR. ADOLPHUS. Q. What is Vincent? A. He is a carman occasionally, and does any labouring work—he had no cart with him that day—he spoke to me at the regular cart stand—there were plenty of other carmen there—he was not a particular friend of mine—I knew him as a carman—he staid outside Barber's warehouse and I went in—he gave me the paper—I said nothing to the young man I delivered it to, no farther than saying, "I come for twenty casks of butter"—I mentioned no name, nor said any thing about who sent me—I had not taken butter from the new
warehouse before—I had been to Barber's concern, and removed butter for Priestley, the town carman, when he was busy, four or five times—I am well known there.
Q. Did Vincent tell you nothing about the butter more than to go and take it away by virtue of that note? A. No, nobody heard him give me the order that I know of—there was no other carman by, I know—I went into the warehouse out of Vincent's sight, and there got the butter, which I brought out, and put in the cart—he was about ten or twelve yards from me in the street when the butter was being delivered—Mr. Barber's people put it into the cart, I did not—I did not point out Vincent to them as having any thing to do with it—they did not ask any thing about it—I gave them the note, and they gave me the butter—they saw nothing of Vincent that I know of—when I drove off he walked on the off side of the cart on to Tower-hill—he then got on the pavement and walked on—I did not see him speak to any body there—I had often drank with him—It was about a mile from the warehouse that we had been when he said, "Make it a pint"—there were plenty of people there, but nobody that I knew—I did not ask where the butter was going to—Vincent did not tell me—I stated that it was a four-horse van.
Q. How long after the transaction did you hear of it again? A. About an hour after—my master said Mr. Barber wanted to see me—I went to him and told him I took it down Whitechapel-road, and put it into a van with four horses—I did not then tell him who employed me—I did afterwards—I did not then know why he was inquiring about it, or that it was stolen not till he said it was a forged order—he said so then—I did not then tell him Vincent had employed me—they took me up, and put me in the Compter—I was taken before the Lord Mayor two or three days after—I was in custody during that time, but nobody came to me that I could mention about Vincent to—I did not tell the officer who took me to the Mansion-house, of Vincent, nor the Lord Mayor, because the Lord Mayor asked me no questions—I was remanded—I was at the Compter from Friday till Monday, and then went to Lambeth-street—I then told about Vincent.
Q. Had you heard in the meantime that any body else had been taken up? A. Yes—I was committed as a witness—I was never in prison before on any charge of felony, only for a row —I was in the service of Mr. Thomas Bailey, as carman—I left'on account of a row with the foreman, and he accused me of stealing some silk—Mr. Bailey did not discharge me, it was the foreman—I was not discharged from any other service in the same manner—Mr. Hill pays me weekly, unless I have money in the course of the week—I have frequently kept money back in the course of the week, which is taken out of my wages on Saturday.
Q. Were your wages under stoppage at any time? A. Yes, at the time I buried my wife—he lent me some money, I took a little more, and it was stopped at so much a week—I gave Vincent the shilling for giving me the job, it is always usual—we always get something, and if any body is with us we always give them something—it did not occur to me that this was an irregular transaction at first, till Mr. Barber mentioned it—I thought it very queer when Vincent mentioned about the four horses, but not till then—I did not mention about it till eight days afterwards—I told my master it was a van and four horses that took it away—I described the person who employed me as a stout man—I did not give his name—I did not give a
correct description of Vincent's dress—I did not say he was dressed in black—I think I said it was a fustian coat or something—that was not the way he was dressed—I gave the true dress of Roberts and the other men—neither of them had employed me, but they were with the van—I was asked the dress of the party who employed me, and gave the dress of somebody else as the dress of Vincent.
JURY. Q. What became of the mats? A. They were left in the cart—it did not rain—I did not notice whether the butter was covered over in the van—we seldom cover goods when it does not rain, unless they are particular goods.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. Is the young man here to whom you delivered the order? A. Yes.
Cross-examined by MR. PRENDERGAST. Q. You did the same here with the order, as you have done on other occasion when you got butter from the quay? A. Yes.
MR. BODKIN. Q. Why, when asked by your matter about the person who employed you, did you not give his name and a description of him? A. I had no particular reason at first—he had been a companion of mine in Thames-street—I did not know whether it was right or not—he had been a man out of work, and when I had a job by which I could get a shilling, he went with me.
Q. He had befriended you before? A. Yes, I had known him for years—it is many years ago that I lived with Bailey—I have been between seven and eight years with Hill, and was with Bailey some years before—I was not taken before any Magistrate about the silk—I might have gone to work for Bailey afterwards if I had thought proper.—when my wife died I had some money coming from my club, and when these thing happen they collect a trifle round—I kept a little money back till I got that—master stopped that out of my wages—I told him how it happened.
MR. PRENDERGAST. Q. You knew the other men at well at Vincent? A. Yes, I had seen them often.
JAMES BATES . I am warehouseman to Joseph Barber, who is the sole owner of the wharf called Brewer's Quay. In the latter part of November, there was a consignment of butter to his care—forty-six of the easke were marked "Prime Kiel P E" and there was a brand mark on the head and side, "I M R."
COURT. Q. DO you know what documents were transmitted with the butter? A. No.
MR. BODKIN. Q. Were they in the prosecutor's warehouse and care? A. Yes, they were discharged from; the ship Regina —I know this document—( looking at it)—it is the bill of lading referring, to the butter I am speaking of—I saw it at the time the butter was delivered—I do not know whether it was delivered with the butter—I checked the receipt of the butter by this bill of lading; at the time it came in, and entered it in this book at the time in my own Handwriting—( producing a book.)
Q. Look at that to refresh your memory, and tell the Whether this it the bill of lading referring to the butter in question? A. It is—the forty-six tubs marked I M R were piled separately from the others—it is usual to put chalk marks on the front casks of the pile, and these were chalked I M R—I saw that mark on some of the forty-six—on the 30th of November I delivered ten of those casks—I had no order to deliver them—Mr. Rice's carman came for them—I went to the principal clerk's, who said I
might deliver them—I am not the warehouseman who delivered the twenty casks in question.
JURY. Q. Did Mr. Rice write an order for the ten casks? A. I had no order.
Cross-examined by MR. ADOLPHUS. Q. DO you know Vincent? A. No—I never saw him doing work about the warehouse to my knowledge—I know Sullivan—I believe he has done work about our warehouse, but I have been there but a short time—I know nothing of the delivery of the twenty casks—I am not a principal warehouseman.
Cross-examined by MR. PRENDERGAST. Q. Did you see these casks marked? A. I did, all of them—I both saw them being marked, and after they were marked—they were marked "Prime Kiel, P E," and I M R was branded on the top and likewise on the side of the cask—I saw that that was done on all of them, we are obliged to examine them as they come in—I did not see it done—it is done abroad—we put the chalk mark—the dairy mark, which is I M R—that is branded, but we chalk it on the outside casks as well.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. When did you see the bill of lading first? A. At the time I was taking in the butters—the butters were landed from the Regina —I got the bill from the counting-house—it is the original—I first saw it in the office at the time of the delivery—I had to go into the counting-house to see it.
THOMAS MURPHY . I am warehouseman to Mr. Barber, of Brewer's-quay. On Tuesday, the 4th of December, I delivered twenty casks of butter to Sullivan, marked P E, and I M R branded—it was about twelve o'clock in the day, I should say—I received this order from Sullivan's hand—I heard him speak to a person named Dowsett, who is in our employ—there was a conversation between them, and Dowsett pointed to me—I went up immediately, and asked Sullivan what he wanted—he said, "Twenty casks of butter,"—I said, "For whom?"—he said, "For Rice," and with that produced the order—I gave the order to Dowsett to go to the principal warehouseman—after he returned I delivered the twenty casks of butter to Sullivan, and gave him a cart note for Rice and Son.
Cross-examined by MR. ADOLPHUS. Q. Was this note delivered to you open as it is now, or folded up? A. I cannot remember—I am quite sure that Sullivan asked for twenty casks of butter for Rice.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. You are the warehouseman? A. Yes, occasionally—the delivery was made in the due course of my duty.
JAMES DOWSETT . I was, at the time in question, a labourer in Mr. Barber's employ—I assisted in stowing away the butter that came out of the vessel Regina —there were forty-six casks marked I M R—I helped to pile them up—Nathaniel Brown is in our service—he chalked several of them—I remember Sullivan coming on the 4th of December—he asked me where the foreman was—I pointed to Murphy—he went up to him—Murphy asked what he came for—he said, "For butter"—he asked him, "For whom?"—he said, "For Rice"—I received the order from Murphy to make inquiry about it of the warehouse-keeper—I returned with instructions to deliver them.
JURY. Q. Who gave the instructions to you? A. Mr. Pittock, the warehouse-keeper, on the other side of the way.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. Did you on that deliver them to Sullivan? A. I
did—three or more of those I delivered to him had the chalk mark on them, and I M R was branded on the tops and sides of them all—I saw them deposited in the cart, which stood about two yards from the warehouse door, the width of the pavement, in Thames-street.
Cross-examined by MR. ADOLPHUS. Q. DO you know Vincent at all? A. I never remember seeing him before he was at the office—I never saw Sullivan before—he said he came for butter—he did not say twenty casks—he did not mention the quantity in my hearing—I assisted in the delivery into the cart—it was a common town cart—it was not covered with any thing when I saw it—there was a mat or two on the copse, which is very common—I think there was not sufficient to cover the whole—I saw nobody about, but the carman who took them in—nobody assisting him at all—nobody drawing mats from the copse over the butter.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. I believe you only remained while the casks were loading? A. Certainly not—there is a very considerable thoroughfare in Thames-street—my attention was confined to the cart.
SAMUEL CHITTLE . I live at No. 3, Cumberland-place, North-street, Whitechapel. On the 4th of December I was at Bow—on coming home I called at the King's Arms public-house—I think it was between three and four o'clock—I saw Sullivan and Vincent there, drinking a pot of beer together—I saw Hill's town cart standing almost close by the door—there was nothing in it—I did not go up to look into it—I was there about half an hour—they left before me—to the best of my knowledge they went out of the house together—I did not see which way they went—they took the cart with them to the best of my knowledge, but I do not know—I did not come out with them—when I came out it was gone—I came away perhaps a quarter of an hour after them.
Cross-examined by MR. ADOLPHUS. Q. There was but one cart, I presume? A. I saw but one, that was Hill's—it might be a few yards from the door—there was nothing in it—there could not be twenty casks of butter in it without my seeing it—I cannot say whether Sullivan and Vincent met there accidentally or not—it is common for two carmen to drink together.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. Did you look into the cart to see whether there was any thing in it? A. I did as I came up, but I saw nothing in it—it was empty—I am certain there was nothing in it.
JAMES HARRINGTON . I am a publican, and live in Tower-street. I know the prisoner Gregson—I saw him at my house, I think it was on Thursday, the 6th or 7th of December, the day before he was taken up—he asked if I wanted to buy any butter—I said I did not know that I did—he asked what I was paying—I said, "A shilling a pound"—I asked if he had got any—he said he had not himself, but he knew somebody who had—I asked him the price—he asked me 80 s. per cwt. I think, and said I was to give him something for himself provided I bought it—I said it would not do for me unless it was very good, and I should not buy it unless I saw it—he said they should bring me one in the afternoon, about three or four o'clock, to see if I liked it—as he was going away I said, "There is some butter stolen from Mr. Barber's quay, it is none of that, is it?"—I do not exactly know what he said, but it was "Oh no," or something to that purpose—he said nothing about the quality—I did not ask him about it—I did not see him again that day—I went out, and left word at home if any body called with any thing, to leave it, but nothing came—I saw him about eleven or twelve o'clock
next day—he was then very tipsy indeed—I think he asked me, "Sir, will you have any of that butter?"—I said, "You have not sent me any to look at yet; you were to bring me a tub to look at"—(he had not mentioned a tub)—he said he would bring me a sample in a cloth—I said that would not do, I would not have any butter in a cloth—he said, "Very well, it don't make any difference to me whether you have any or not"—about twenty minutes after I went into the tap-room, and he was then sitting down asleep, with some porter before him—about five minutes before one o'clock the prisoner Northcote, and another person who I do not see here, came—I did not hear them ask for any body—shortly after they came, Gregson came, and Northcote began to swear at him—I cannot say the words exactly—it was, "You are a d—pretty fellow," or "a d—old humbug; you promised to meet me this morning at a quarter before ten o'clock, and now it is one o'clock"—I did not hear Gregson make him any answer—the prisoner Christall was there at the time, and was within a yard or two of him—he had come in about the time Gregson did—I think Christall heard what was laid, but he did not take any notice of what was going on—after that, Northcote, the other man who was with him, Gregson, and Christall, went out—when they were going out, I think Gregson said to the man who was with Northcote, that he would talk to him about it, or something to that effect—he was a good distance off me—he did not say what he would talk about—Northcote: said to Gregson, "You are a d—old fool, do you think I will allow you to do business with that man while I am present, with my property?—the property is mine"—I suppose he alluded to the man who was with Northcote.
COURT. Q. Not a word was mentioned about what property he meant? A. No—butter was not mentioned from the beginning to the end.
Cross-examined by MR. PRENDERGAST. Q. Nothing was said in the presence of Northcote and the other about butter? A. No—I never saw Northcote before, I did not take any particular notice of him—I will not swear it was him—if I had seen him in the street I should not have known him.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. DO you know Gregson is a man who acts as agent for people when he can get it? A. I have bought things from him before, and paid other people for them—I have bought fresh butter of him, and poultry, and eggs, which he had to sell for other persons, merely telling me he had it to sell—that was understood—he would say he had a dozen lumps of butter to sell, if I would buy it—I have bought butter of Mr. Willoughby, a neighbour of mine, through him—Mr. Willoughby is a respectable man—I cannot tell whether he received a commission on it—I have bought different sorts of property of respectable people through him.
MR. PRENDERGAST. Q. Have you seen Christall before? A. Yes, but know nothing of him.
MR. CHAMBERS. Q. When you bought things of Gregson, did you know who they belonged to? A. No—I cannot say whose property they were—he represented that they belonged to other people—I have not bought any large quantities of butter of him; perhaps eight or ten lumps, and I have sent my boy to pay the person who he said it belonged to; not to pay Gregson at all.
EDWIN SUTCLIFFE . I am a cheesemonger, in High-street, Whitechapel. I succeeded Mr. Robinson in the business, and have carried it on for two years on my own account—I had a partner four years—before that I managed
the business for Mr. Robinson—on Friday, the 7th of December, the prisoner Christall came to my house between four and fire o'clock in the afternoon—I did not know him before—he said he had some butter to sell, and that his name was Hancock—I asked him if he was brother to Mr. Hancock of Great St. Helen's, as I knew Mr. Robinson had dealt with Mr. Hancock—he said no, but he knew Mr. Robinson, and had had dealings with him—I told him I had no recollection of him—he said yes, he had sold him a 10 s. of butter some time ago—I have no recollection of any dealings whatever with him—Mr. Robinson is since dead—he said he lived in Great Alie-street, next door to the Black Horse—he asked me to buy, and brought a sample in his pocket in paper—it was not left with me—he said it was Kiel butter, and 84 s. was named as the price—there is a large quantity of that butter in the market from time to time—I looked at the sample, and tasted it—it was not very good—it appeared Kiel butter—after some conversation, he said he had got a cask outside if I would allow him to bring it in—I gave him leave—(a man named Tripp had been with me two days before, and I was aware of the robbery at Brewer's Quay)—the butter was brought in, by the prisoner Hopkins, in a cask covered with a sack—I told Hopkins to put it down at the end of my counter, but Christall told him to take it into the back warehouse, which he did, and I and Christall then went into the back warehouse—mine is a wholesale and retail shop—there are folding doors between the back and front shop—the sack was taken off in the back warehouse, and I found the head and top hoops had been taken off the cask—it contained Kiel butter—I tried the quality, and it was very good Kiel butter, worth about 104 s. in the market—I asked how long it had been landed—he said from thirty-six to forty hours—I asked where the head of the cask was—he said it was at home—I asked him what ship they came out of—he said, "You must not be too particular"—he gave no reason for that—I then asked if I could see the remainder of the butter, the bulk of the goods—he said I could—he had said he could supply me with ten casks a week, but he had only got five by him at present—I wished to see the other four—he said, if I would go home with him, I could see them—I went with him—the cask of butter was left at my warehouse—he took me to Toy's house, the Angel and Crown, Wellclose-square—I asked him, as we went along, what ship they came out of, and he said, "You must not be too particular, not ask so many questions"—he did not give me any account of the butter—I did not see any thing of Hopkins—I suppose he walked behind, as I saw him at Toy's house, in the tap-room—when we got to Toy's, Christall asked if I would take any thing to drink in front of the bar—I said no—he asked me to walk into the parlour, which is a back room behind the tap-room—I went towards there, and found that Christall had turned back, and gone into the tap-room—I turned back, and followed him unperceived by him—he went up to one of the boxes in the tap-room, and joined some person there, but who it was I cannot say—Toy was not there then—he came directly almost.
Q. Did you observe him speak to those persons? A. He was speaking, but I could not hear what he said—I was near the tap-room door—I stood on one side—Northcote then came up to me out of the taproom with a pint-pot in his hand—he was one of the persons to whom Christall had spoken—he asked me if I was the gentleman come to look at the butters—I said "Yes"—he said, "Well, walk this way towards the parlour, and they shall be brought to you"—Christall was close alongside,
and heard what passed—I said I could not stop, I expected to find the butters there—Christall wished me particularly to walk into the parlour and stop there—I said, no, I could not wait—Gregson then came up from the tap-room, and said to Christall, "Oh, if the gentleman makes any obstacle, I will give you the money for the butters"—Northcote was there, and could hear that—they were all together, Northcote, Christall, and Gregson—during this conversation Hopkins had returned, and was sitting in front of the bar—I do not know whether he could have heard the conversation—Christall (I think) said it was not convenient to take me to the place where the butters were—I am not positive it was him—it was said in the hearing of the others—they said they did not like to point out the houses of their friends, as they were engaged in these smuggling transactions—I did not consent to wait—I said I would have nothing further to do with it, and left the house—I had got about half-way across Wellclose-square, when Christall came after me, and said it would be doing him a service if I would buy the butters, as he should only get his commission upon it—I said no, I would have nothing further to do with it—I called on Tripp at his lodgings within five minutes, on my way home, and communicated to him what had passed—in consequence of that, Shelswell and Taylor, the officers, were sent for from Lambeth-street—I went home, and Tripp came to my house soon after, and Shelswell and Taylor were placed outside my door—I and Tripp looked more particularly at the cask, and noticed the brand marks had been cut out—Christall then came to my house, and asked me to buy the one cask—I said no, I would not have any thing more to do with it—he said he had made a mistake, it was not Kiel butter, as he had told me, but it was Friezland butter—I told him no, it was not Friezland butter, it was Kiel, but I refused to buy it—he beckoned to Hopkins to come and take it away, and Hopkins came and put it on a truck in front of my door—I do not think there was any name on the truck, but I did not look particularly—I did not notice whether they went away together—I saw it put into the truck, Hopkins being with it, and Christall left my house—I did not notice whether he covered it over.
Cross-examined by MR. PRENDERGAST. Q. Christall and you went together from your house to this place in Wellclose-square? A. Yes—we had some talk together on the road—he did not propose to have any other dealings with me, to my knowledge—he said I might have 100 gallons of brandy if I liked.
Q. Did he not profess his readiness to let you have any article in the smuggling line—not calling it so, but intimating it? A. I recollect he mentioned brandy.
Q. That sort of article on which the duty was not paid? A. Yes—I had asked for the head of the cask before we left my shop—I do not recollect Christall inquiring for it when we got to Wellclose-square—he might have done so, but I did not hear him—I said nothing about the head there—he said it would be of service if I would buy the butters, as he should get his commission—Hopkins acted merely as a porter, bringing the goods with the truck, and afterwards following to Wellclose-square with the truck—one cask being left at my house, there would be good reason for a porter to suppose he would have to bring more—he did not assist in any way but as a porter.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Is there not a duty on foreign
butter of 20 s. a cwt.? A. Yes—I never heard that there was a considerable penalty for having possession of smuggled butter—Christall did not say, when I wished to see the casks, "No, I can't, I do not like to show the place, because it is smuggled"—he promised to take me down and show me the other four casks—I told him. I wished to see them—I do not recollect his saying, "No, I can't, I do not like to show the place, because it is smuggled"—I asked him where they were—he said, "Close by," and he would take me to the place—he said at Toy's that he did not like to show his friends, but before I went to Toy's house I do not recollect his saying any thing of the kind—I should not like to swear he did not, but I do not recollect it—I should not like to swear he did, or did not—it was in reference to this butter that he spoke afterwards of the things being smuggled.
JOHN TRIPP . I am warehouseman to the prosecutor. On Friday, the 7th of December, Sutcliffe made a communication to me, and I procured Shelswell and Taylor—I went in to Sutcliffe's while Shelswell and Taylor remained outside—I went into the warehouse at the back of the shop, and saw a cask of butter covered over with a sack—there was no head on it, nor any top hoops—I saw the place where the dairy mark ought to have been was cut out—while I was there Christall came in and spoke to Sutcliffe—Hopkins also came in, took the cask in his arms, took it out, and put it into the truck—that was while Christall was talking to Mr. Sutcliffe—Hopkins had drawn up with the truck as Christall came in—I came out, and gave notice to Shelswell, and he and Taylor went down Red Lion-street, following Hopkins—I afterwards went to Toy's, after borrowing a great coat of Mr. Sutcliffe, that I might not be known—Toy was not at home—he came in in seven or eight minutes with a butter-knife in his hand—it was a regular knife for drawing samples of butter—I asked him what he wanted of a butter-knife—he said he had borrowed it of Mr. Jarvis—I knew Toy before, and that he had one child—I asked him if he was aware my employers had been defrauded of twenty casks of butter, and asked him if he knew any thing respecting these butters—he said he knew nothing at all about them—I then told him, I had received information from Mr. Sutcliffe that there were some more casks in or near his premises—he said he knew nothing at all about them—I told him there had been two men stopped, offering a cask of butter for sale, and if he did know any thing I begged of him to tell me—he then said he did know, but he would not tell me, for if he did he should get other people into trouble—I never said, "Never mind other people, look to yourself, you have got a wife and family"—he then said he did know, but he would not tell me—I told him he must take his chance then.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Did you not say to him, "For God's sake tell me all you know aboutit?" A. Yes—I said, "If you tell me what you know about it I will make it all right"—I do not know that there is a heavy penalty for having possession of smuggled butter—there is a penalty—I do not know the amount of it.
THOMAS SHELSWELL . I am an officer of Lambeth-street office. Tripp applied to me on the 7th of December, and I went with Taylor within a few doors of Sutcliffe's—I did not see any thing brought out of the shop—I saw a cask of butter in a truck, covered with a sack—the sack was thrown carelessly over the top of the cask—we followed the truck to opposite the door of Potter, a green-grocer, in Rosemary-lane—Hopkins was drawing it—Christall
was with it all the way, in communication with Hopkins—Christall went into a public-house near there, and came out again—the truck then stood opposite Potter's door—he went up to Hopkins and Hopkins then put the tub of butter into Potter's shop—I then stepped up to Christall, and asked him what he had got there—he said a cask of butter—I asked who it belonged to—he said in a very hurried manner, "Why to me, to be sure"—I then told him I suspected it was a cask, one out of 20, which had been obtained from Brewer's Quay, by a forged order—he said, "Oh, if that is the case, here is the man I bought it of," pointing to Gregson, who was standing against a potato-bin, in the shop—that was said loud enough for Gregson to hear—I said to Gregson, "How do you account for it?"—Gregson said, "I know nothing at all about it"—Christall and Gregson were then taken into custody—I took Christall, and desired Hopkins to bring the butter away in the truck—Hopkins was quite near enough to hear the conversation I have spoken of—I went to Lambeth-street, locked up the butter, and sent Taylor with Gregson to the station-house, and took Hopkins and Christall myself—I asked Hopkins where he brought the butter from—he said from Whitechapel—I said I was aware of that—he said he would show me where he brought it from, I went with him and Christall, to the Angel and Crown, Toy's house—he took me there—Toy was in the bar—it was about half-past six o'clock—on going in, Christall said to Toy, "You have brought me into a nice mess with your butter"—I told Christall to hold his tongue, and then told Toy that I had stopped these two men with a truck, and a cask of butter, which I suspected to be one of 20, obtained by a forged order from Brewer's Quay, and that from what information I had received, I expected there was some more of it there, or that he knew of it—he said he knew nothing about the butter, he had seen no butter, he knew nothing about it, it had never been there—I then called Hopkins forward, and asked him in Toy's presence, where he took the butter from—he immediately turned and shoved the tap-room door open, pointed to the table in the right-hand corner, and said, "There is where I took it from"—I said to Toy, "What do you think of this?"—Toy told Hopkinsit was a lie, it had never been there—Taylor then returned—I left Hopkins and Christall in his custody, and took Toy into the passage—he went with me into the passage—I told him that he being a publican, I knew him to be a respectable man, and it was much more to his credit, if he knew any thing of the butters to tell me, than otherwise—he said he knew nothing of them—they had never been there, and after a minute or so he turned round, and said directly, "I have heard of some butters in the neighbourhood, but from what I can understand they have been removed about three quarters of an hour—I think they were taken away in a cart like a gravel cart, but if you will come down to me to-morrow morning, about half-past nine o'clock, there is no doubt you may hear something about them, and I will put you on the men and the butters too"—I did not take him into custody that night—on that representation I left the house—on the Sunday after this I accompanied Childs and Taylor to Cumberland-place, Waterloo-town, and took Northcote into custody there—we stopped at a public-house, coming from there, with Northcote in custody—I saw Childs take a pocket-book from Northcote in that public-house, which he has—I looked at it, and saw some writing in it.
Cross-examined by MR. PRENDERGAST. Q. When you saw Christall and
Hopkins in Rosemary-lane, Christall said something to somebody in the shop, and then Hopkins carried the butter in just as a porter would act? A. Yes: Gregson was in Potter's shop—I understood Christall to mean when he said "The butter belongs to me," that he had the direction of it—he afterwards said, in Gregson's presence, "That is the man I had it of"—Hopkins and Christall showed me every facility in finding out where the butter came from.
Q. When you mentioned about Brewer's Quay, did not Christall say, "Why, Mr. Barber, why I know him, I am sure I know nothing about it?" A. I have no recollection of that—I did not observe any particular effect being produced on Christall on my saying it was stolen.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. What day was it you had the conversation with Toy? A. On the 7th of December—the first hearing at Lambeth-street was on the 8th—I heard that Toy came of his own accord to the office—he was taken into custody in the office—there were seven examinations.
COURT. Q. What day was he taken into custody? A. On the 8th of December, about three o'clock—he was not examined.
CHARLES JARVIS . I am a cheesemonger, and live in Ratcliff-highway. I know Toy—on Friday evening, the 7th of December, between half-past five and six o'clock, he came to my house and said, "Jarvis, a friend of mine has some butter to sell, will you buy it?"—I asked what sort it was—he said it was very good—I asked what sort it was—he said he thought it was Kiel butter, and produced some samples on half-a-sheet of paper—that is a very unusual mode of producing samples—he produced three or four samples—they appeared to be all of one sort—could not judge whether they came from different casks—he named the price as 54 s. the cask—I asked him what he meant, whether he meant by the cask or cwt.—he again said, "54 s. a cask"—a cask generally weighs 96 or 100 lbs. net—I told him I thought it was very cheap indeed—he asked if I did not think it was cheap—I said I did—I had heard that Mr. Barber had lost some butter from his warehouse, and was induced to ask him about it, as it struck me it had some connexion with that—I said it was very unusual to purchase butter by so small a sample, could he not get me a larger sample—I asked him if I could not see the bulk—he said, "No"—I asked him where they were—he said at a friend's house of his, not far off—I asked him what marks the butters had on them—he told me, "None at all"—I then brought forward the knife we generally take samples with, and drew a cask before him, and said, "That is the way we draw samples"—I knew him very well—I asked him whether he could not get me a sample like that, that I might judge better of the quality—he had told me two or three times that I could not see them—he left me with the intention, I thought, of bringing me a sample, taking my knife with him—I did not offer to go with him, as he had objected to my seeing the bulk—I asked him what size the casks were—he said he thought they were what they called "hundreds"—the sample was Kiel butter, and very good—he returned again the same evening, soon after eight o'clock—he took me to the back part of the shop, and told me he had like to have got into a nice mess about this butter, for since he had left me he had found out it was stolen—I said, "Indeed"—he said he thought the butter had been smuggled, as he knew the old chap that brought it to him never dealt in any thing but what was smuggled—he did not tell me who the old chap was—I told him
I thought it was a very good job that the butters were not found at his place—he told me it was a good job, for it might have been 300 l. out of his pocket if it had been found there—he then gave me a different statement—he told me two men had brought a cask of butter to his house in a truck, set it down on the threshold, or inside his door, I cannot say which, called for something to drink, and asked him whether he would buy the butter, or not—he did not tell me who the two men were—he said he told the men it was no use to him, as his consumption was not more than two or three pounds a week, but he would go to his cheesemonger and see whether he would buy it or not—I was his cheesemonger—he said that when he had returned from my house, the men had just left his house—they had not left long before the officers came, and he had ascertained from the officers that it was stolen—he said it was removed from there, they had just got it away in time, and added, that it was far enough off by this time—when he first came to my shop he did not say a word about a cask having been brought to his house by two men.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. You had known Toy a good while? A. I had, since Midsummer-day, when I took my house.
SAMUEL TAYLOR . I am a street-keeper of Whitechapel. I went to Sutcliffe's shop with Shelswell—I saw the truck standing opposite the shop—I followed it to Potter's—I heard Shelswell ask Christall whose property it was—I afterwards went and apprehended Northcote, and took him to a public-house—he sat down before the fire, and I saw him with a pocket-book and a letter in his hand—Childs snatched the pocket-book out of his hand—on the 10th of December I was looking out for Vincent—Sullivan was with me—we met Vincent in Whitechapel-road—he put out his hand to shake hands with Sullivan, but Sullivan drew his hand back and said to me, "Here is Vincent, this is the man that gave me the order"—I took hold of him by the arm, and told him I wanted him for giving Sullivan a forged order, by which he obtained the butter from Barber's wharf—Vincent turned round and looked at Sullivan and said, "Oh, the vagabond has come that, has he?"—he stated something in a muttering manner, which I could not understand, and I took him away to the station-house—after he had a walked a few yards with me, he said, "I know nothing about it."
Cross-examined by MR. ADOLPHUS. Q. You are quite sure he said nothing more, not what he muttered, but did not he say, "I know nothing about it then?" A. I cannot tell, it might be that—Vincent had been in prison a week at that time on this charge.
Cross-examined by MR. PRENDERGAST. Q. When Christall was asked in the presence of Gregson who the property belonged to, he said Gregson, did he not? A. He said he bought it of Gregson—he did not say it belonged to Gregson.
Q. If any body has sworn that the expression used was, that the butter belonged to Gregson, that is not true, is it? A. I cannot say—I was not there all the time—there was some conversation after I was out of the shop—I followed out with Hopkins—I went to Toy's after putting Gregson into the station-house.
WILLIAM CHILDS. I am a beadle of Tower-hill. On Friday, the 7th of December, I went to Toy's house, at eleven o'clock—he was not at home—he came in in about a quarter of an hour—he did not tell me where he had been—when he came in I said, "Do you know any thing about any butters?"—he said, "No"—I said, "I have just gained information that
you have some butter by you somewhere, you have been offering some for sale?"—he said, "No, I have not"—I said, "Do you know a person named Jarvis in Ratcliff-highway?"—he said, "Yes, I do"—I said, "Have you offered him any butters for sale?"—he said, "No"—I said, "You have not offered any butters to Mr. Jarvis at 50 s. a cask?"—he said, "Yes, I did, I have offered them for a friend of mine to Mr. Jarvis"—I said, "Well then, you must go along with me"—he said, "No, I will be d—if I do"—he said, "You know where I live, and I will not go out of my bar to-night for no one"—two or three persons came up to the bar, and said, "He is always to be found at any time, he will not run away"—I knew one of those persons—I said, "If you won't go with me will you allow me to search your house?"—he said, "No, I will be d—if any man shall search my house without a warrant"—I went into the bar to him after that, and told him, "It is a very serious job, you had better give the butters up if you have got them by you, and the men if you know them"—he said, "Well, if you will come to me to-morrow morning, at half-past eight o'clock, I will put you on the men and the butters too—I had some butters in the house, but they are gone away"—I then left him.
COURT. Q. Are you sure he said in the house? A. In the house—it was at or in the house.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. Did he say why you were to come at half-past eight? A. Yes, he said, "I know you Childs, and I should rather you had the job than any one else"—he said, "I told Shelswell to come at half-past nine o'clock, but if you come at half-past eight o'clock I will put you on them first"—I went next morning at half-past eight o'clock, and saw Toy—he said, "Well, it is all over"—I said, "What is all over?"—he said, "I have given the butters up to Mr. Norman, the Inspector of the H division"—he said he had given him four casks—on the following Sunday I went with Shelswell and Taylor to take Northcote—we found him at George Pementer's in Waterloo-town, sitting up in a bed undressed—it was between six and seven o'clock in the morning—I searched him, but found nothing—as we were bringing him to the office, we stopped at a public-house—I was about to search him again, and saw him take his hat off, pull his handkerchief out of his hat, and taw a pocket-book in his hand—I pulled it out of his hand and gave it to Shelswell, who pointed out to me what was in it—I produce the book, after that I took a letter out of his hand—the book is now in the same state as when I took it from him—here is written in the pocket-book, "I M R, 1. 0. 6."—I handed it to Shelswell in the state I found it—Northcote was in Toy's tap-room when I was there on Friday night, at eleven o'clock.
Cross-examined by MR. PRENDERGAST. Q. You and Shelswell made pretty work with the pocket book—you snatched it out of the man's hand and gave it to Shelswell, and he returned it to you after finding something in it, telling you to mind it? A. Yes—he did not have it above two minutes—I saw the mark—I am quite sure I did not rub out any part of it—God forbid I should do so—Northcote took the pocket-book in his hand in the presence of myself and Shelswell, and sat down by the fire with it in his hand—his thumb was inside it—it was not long in his hand before I snatched it out—it might be a minute and a half—I had searched his clothes where I took him before that, and found nothing—he had the book covered with his handkerchief all but a little bit—every body could see
that bit of the book—I do not know whether Taylor had it in his hand, he looked at it.
Q. Do you mean to swear that that pocket-book has never been out of your possession from the time Shelswell handed it to you to the present time? A. I cannot swear to that, because it was given in at Lambeth-street Office—I gave it into the hands of one of the officers myself there for the Magistrate to see—I cannot say whether it was Shelswell or Lea, or who—it was at the last examination but one or two—they gave it to me as soon as I went in—it was out of my possession perhaps half an hour—it has not been out of my possession since—it has not been in the hands of Mr. Barber since the examination—he had it at the police-office—I have had it in my pocket several times when I have been to Mr. Barber's counting-house—I might show it to Mr. Barber there, but I will swear it has not been out of my sight—I will swear I did not leave it there, and receive it again.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. I believe you know that Toy went to the police-office, not in custody? A. Yes—I had desired him to go there, and he did.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. DO you know Gregson? A. Yes—I do not know that he acts as an agent for the disposal of property.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. At the police-office were the witnesses examined apart? A. Yes—the pocket-book was called for to be shown to a witness—Shelswell called my attention to something in it—it is in the same state as to the letters and figures, as when he called my attention to it—it has never been out of my sight except at the police-office.
JAMES FOGG . I am an officer of the Thames Police. I know the King's Head public-house, on Tower-street. On the 4th of December, from ten to twelve o'clock in the morning I was there, and saw Northcote and Gregson there, in company with each other—it was between ten and eleven o'clock, or about half-past ten o'clock—I saw them several times that morning, from Thames-street to Tower-street together—Gregson lives in Barking-alley, a short distance from Brewer's Quay—I know all the prisoners—on the Friday after, I saw Christall go to Gregson's house—I afterwards saw him at the Czar's Head, in Tower-street—he asked the landlady if Jerry Gregson was there—the landlady said "No"—he said, "I know he is here"—she said, "He has not been here for some time, and I don't care if I never see him nor you again."
Cross-examined by MR. PRENDERGAST. Q. Have you not very often seen Northcote about there? A. Yes.
WILLIAM HILL . I live in St. Dunstan's-hill, I know Vincent. On the 4th of December, about a quarter before ten o'clock, I saw him in Lower Thames-street, about one hundred yards from Mr. Barber's—he was alone, but he had been speaking to two men—one was tall and the other short—but I do not know them—he was standing about a yard from the carts.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. He was in the habit of waiting about there for jobs, was he not? A. Yes, I have frequently seen him there—I do not know whether he was waiting for jobs—I have never said so—he drives a cart now and then.
MR. BODKIN. Q. Are you Sullivan's master? A. Yes, he has been with me eight years and a half—he paid me 15 s., on account of carrying this butter.
CLARENCE BARBER . I am nephew to the prosecutor, and am in his employ. I produce two orders, marked "A" and "B"—I received them at our counting-house.
Cross-examined by MR. PRENDERGAST. Q. The order produced by the witness, is it not a common order for the delivery of butter? A. Yes, when we know the party.
COURT. Q. It that in a shape in which the proprietor of any butter would send for it? A. It is generally the case that we have this sort.
JURY. Q. Would a delivery clerk deliver butter on an order of that kind? A. Yes.
COURT. Q. What, without any request to deliver, but merely having the marks, and the name of the firm? A. Yes—if we knew the butters marked so belong to the firm, we should deliver them—when we know the parties are in the habit of sending their carts down for goods—it is not in my department to deliver the goods—I am not aware whether Mr. Barber has made good the deficiency in the butter.
THOMAS NESBITT . I am in partnership with John Nesbitt. We were the consignees of a cargo of butter by the Regina —this bill of lading reached me by post—in consequence of that I made an arrangement to land a portion of the cargo at Brewer's Quay, at Mr. Barber's warehouse—that portion did not include what was marked I M R—I sold some of that portion to Samuel Rice and Son, and ordered the bill of lading to be endorsed to Rice—I ordered them to endorse an assignment of the portion of the cargo named in the bill of lading—that included the forty-six casks marked I M R.
(The bill of lading produced consigned the butters in question, with others, by the ship Regina, to Messrs. John and Thomas Nesbitt," or to their assigns"—upon the back was endorsed, "Deliver to the order of Messrs. Samuel Rice and Son, I C M 46, I M R 46, say 92 casks of the within butter for J. and T. Nesbitt. WM. GEO. LEGG.")
WILLIAM GEORGE LEGG . The indorsement on this bill of lading is in my hand-writing—I did it by direction of my employers, Messrs. Nesbitt—it was left at Mr. Barber's wharf.
JOHN RICE . I am in the butter business. I have no partner—I purchased of Messrs. Nesbitt some casks of butter, including forty-six marked I M R—they were landed at Brewer's Quay to our order, and remained there, subject to my order.
COURT. Q. Did you draw any of them from there yourself? A. Yes;—this paper is not written by myself, or any of my servants—I never authorised it.
JURY. Q. IS that the usual form of sending an order? A. Yes; we merely put the marks, and the ship the casks come by—( The paper was here read as follows:—"I M R, Regina, 1/4 20 Cks. RICE & Co.")
Cross-examined by MR. PRENDERGAST. Q. Have not you seen other casks of butter marked in the same way? A. I never saw the mark before on any cask—I have no partner—my father died twelve months ago—since that, I have carried on the business alone.
CHARLES PITTOCK . I am principal warehouseman to Mr. Joseph Barber, in the butter department. I have been fifteen years in his service—I ordered this butter to be delivered—I have authority to deliver goods to nobody but the person to whom they are consigned
or an order from them—I ascertain the identity of the party and the property, previous to delivery.
Cross-examined by MR. ADOLPHUS. Q. Had Vincent any means of knowing your way of doing business? A. I should say not, and Sullivan had no more than him.
Cross-examined by MR. PRENDERGAST. Q. Is it your business, when an order comes with a proper name and mark on it, to deliver the property? A. Yes.
JURY. Q. Would not you examine the books to see if the property stood in the name of the party? A. Yes, I did so in this case—if the marks on the paper and on the book correspond, it is my duty to deliver it—I delivered more of the butter, and should say the order for that was similar.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. Would you deliver the goods without believing it was a genuine order? A. Certainly not—I had no authority to deliver to any thing but what I believed to be a genuine order.
COURT. Q. Then you gave credit to that paper, believing it to be genuine? A. Yes—Rice's cart came for the other goods.
HENRY PALMER . I am carman to Mr. Rice. On the 30th of November I brought from Brewers's Quay ten casks of Kiel butter, marked "I M R." to my master's warehouse, and conveyed them the same day to Spooner's, a cheesemonger in the Strand.
JOHN BOWRING . I am carman to Mr. Rice. On the 4th of December I brought away from Brewer's Quay sixteen casks of Kiel butter, marked "I M R," to my master's warehouse, and the same day I took ten of them to Mr. Hollingdale's, a cheesemonger, and next day took one to Topley of Greenwich.
JOHN SPOONER, JUN . I am a cheesemonger, and live in the Strand. On the 30th of November I received from Mr. Rice ten casks, marked "I M R"—on the 13th of December I had all those casks in my possession.
GEORGE HOLLINGDALE . I am a cheesemonger, and live in Wormwood-street. On the 4th of December I received ten casks, marked "I M R," from Messrs. Rice, and on the 12th of December I had them all in my possession.
WILLIAM TOPLEY . I am a cheesemonger, and live at Greenwich. On the 5th of December I received a cask of Kiel butter, marked "I M R," from Messrs. Rice and Son, and have it in my possession now.
JOHN SMITH . I am warehouseman to Mr. Rice. On the 4th of December I delivered five casks of Kiel butter, marked "I M R," to West, the Richmond carrier—they came from Mr. Barber's premises.
JOHN WEST . I am carter in the employ of Mr. Pope, the Richmond carrier. I received five casks of butter from Smith, and delivered them to J. Spooner, of Richmond.
JOHN SPOONER, SEN . I live at Richmond, and am a cheesemonger. On the 5th of December I received five casks of Kiel butter from Pope, marked "I M R" from Mr. Rice, and had them in my possession on the 13th of December.
WILLIAM NORMAN . I am Inspector of the H division of police. On the morning of the 8th of December, at half-past six o'clock, Toy came to the station-house, and told me that he had four tubs of butter at his house, and wished me to go with him to fetch them away, as he thought
it was part of the property stolen from Brewer's Wharf—I went with him—he took me to a back place, a skittle-ground in the back-yard, where there were four tubs of butter—when he fetched me, he said he wished me to go with him to the house of the person who brought them—he said the person's name was Roberts—he took me to North-street, Whitechapel—it was a kind of chandler's-shop, and beer shop besides, a double house, with shop-windows on each side of the door—the shop was closed—I got in after Toy had called the people up—I did not find Roberts.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Did not you find Thomas Roberts's name over the shop door? A. Yes—Toy left me after that—I told him to come to Lambeth-street Office at eleven o'clock—he did so—he said he was willing to give me all the information in his power—when he said the butter was at his house, he said it was brought there overnight—I did not ask him what they were brought there for.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. Did he tell you at what time overnight? A. No—he did not say he had appointed for two officers to come at a later hour.
NATHANIEL BROWN . I am a labourer in the employ of Mr. Barber. I was there when the casks marked "I M R" came in—I chalked the letters "I M R" on several of them—here is part of my chalk-marks on one of the four produced—there is sufficient to enable me to say it is one of the casks I marked with chalk.
CHARLES PITTOCK re-examined. The casks weighed from 100lbs. to 112lbs. each—here is the place on the side of the casks where the brand mark "IM R" has been taken out—the dairy mark has been erased.
COURT. Q. IS there enough to satisfy you the "IM R" has been there? A. There is no trace of the brand mark—
( The letter found on Northcote was here read as follows:—)
"London, Sept. 22, 1838.
DEAR JEM —I much regret the receipt of yours of this morning, not because you have called on me to send you some blunt, which I am pleased to do; I therefore enclose you 10 s. You must be in a confounded rum state indeed, on a desolate island without a shot in the locker; however, this will serve to get you off a lee-shore and put you under weigh. Jones is home, and he found times so bad that he was forced to be sent home by the parish. However, I hope things will be better with you; yet I must be candid enough to say that we have all had a jolly laugh at your expenes. Mr. Jones, begs to be very kindly remembered to you, and at the same time he hopes that you will not be so far forgetful as to leave his shirts in the Isle of Wight; don't forget to take your regular dose, for Jones is very uneasy about your welfare. Wishing that you may receive this safe, and that it may be of service, I am yours truly, F. TOY."
"MR. JAMES NORTHCOTE."
(Barnard Jewell, tailor, Ship-alley, Wellclose-square; Lieut. William L. Gregg, R. N.; Charles Skinner, surgeon, Woburn-place; William Farrow, clerk to Barclay and Co.; Henry William Ward, wine-merchant, Seething-lane; George-Trail, master mariner and ship-owner, No. 31, Gloster-terrace, Commercial-road; James Rowden, engineer, No.7, Ratcliff-highway; John Savory, plumber, Ratcliff-highway; Charles Mason, wine and spirit-merchant, Well-street; Edward Gladwin, engraver and printer, Ship-alley, Wellclose-square; Thomas Haines, boot-maker,
Ship-alley; Edwin Boys, commercial traveller, Ship-alley; and John Sladden, auctioneer and appraiser, Grove's-alley, Wellclose-square; deposed to Toy's good character: and John Hubbard, Lime-street, Fen-church-street; and William Taylor, commission agent, Denmark-street, Camberwell, to that of Christall: and John Penn, pewterer and engineer, Gray's-court; James Butler, carpenter, Little Prescott-street; Thomas Johnson, tailor, Alie-street, Goodman's-fields; John Marshall, bootmaker, Ann Howard and Job Newman, boot-maker, to that of Gregson.
VINCENT — GUILTY . Aged.— Judgment Respited.
NORTHCOTE — GUILTY . Aged.
TOY — GUILTY . Aged.
CHRISTALL — GUILTY . Aged.
GREGSON — GUILTY . Aged.
HOPKINS — NOT GUILTY .
Of Receiving. Judgment Respited.
(Toy was recommended to mercy by the Jury.)

813. ELIZABETH BROWN was indicted for stealing, on the 7th of February, 1 pan, value 1 s., the goods of Edmund Butterick.
THOMAS ABBOTT . I live with my father, in Clerkenwell. On the 7th of February, between seven and eight o'clock in the evening, I saw the prisoner take a pan from the prosecutor's shop, put it under her apron, and walk away—I ran in, and told Mr. Butterick—he came out, but could not find her—I know she is the person.
SAMUEL PERKINS . I saw a crowd on the 7th of February—I went up, and the prosecutor had got hold of a woman, but not the prisoner—I was afterwards going across St. John's-square, and met the prisoner with this pan.
EDMUND BUTTERICK . I keep an earthenware shop in Jerusalem-passage. This is my pan—I do not know the prisoner.
Prisoner's Defence. I was in great distress.
GUILTY .* Aged 58.— Confined One Year.
[trialAccount t18390204-814]
814. JOHN HUTCHINS was indicted for stealing, on the 28th of January, 5lbs. weight of bacon, value 4 s.; 41bs. weight of beef, value 2 s.; and 4lbs. weight of mutton, value 2 s.; the goods of Elizabeth Morton.
JAMES GILLINGS ( police-constable E 159.) I was in North-crescent, Tottenham Court-road, at one o'clock in the night, on the 28th of January, and saw the prisoner come up the prosecutrix's area, with a bag containing the articles named—I asked what he had been doing—he never spoke—I saw him get over the railing with this property—I found no money upon him, but a knife and a rope.
SARAH BUTCHER . I am servant to Mrs. Elizabeth Morton, of Northcrescent. The prisoner had nothing to do with the house—these are my mistress's property, and it had been in the safe in the area.
Prisoner's Defence. I was in great distress, and had no home.
GUILTY . Aged 18.— Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18390204-815]
815. CATHERINE BANKS was indicted for stealing, on the 1st of February, I shawl, value 5 s., the goods of Michael Hawdon, from the person of Ruth Hawdon.
RUTH HAWDON . I am the wife of Michael Hawdon—we live in Salisbury-street near Portman-market. On the 1st of February, at half-past ten, or near eleven o'clock at night, I was passing from Broad-street to Russell-street, St. Giles's—the prisoner came and asked me if I had any money—I told her I had not—she watched her opportunity, snatched my shawl off me, and ran away—I watched her as far as I could, and called "Police"—the officers took her about ten minutes afterwards—I know her to be the same person—this is my shawl—( looking at it.) CHRISTIAN BURNETT. I saw the prosecutrix walking along, and saw the prisoner take the shawl from her shoulders, and run away with it in her hand—she came towards my door, and then ran into a court, where the policeman took her—I am sure the prisoner is the person.
JAMES BEDMAN ( police-constable E 56.) I found the prisoner in Buckridge-street, coming out of a cellar—I did not find the shawl.
GEORGE HOBBY ( police-constable E 129.) I found this shawl the next morning, at No. 13, Buckridge-street.
GUILTY .* Aged 18.— Transported for Ten Years.
[trialAccount t18390204-816]
816. JAMES MACDONALD was indicted for stealing, on the 23rd of January, 1 purse, value 6 d.;1 handkerchief, value 6 d.;1 hat, value 8 s.; 9 sovereigns, 1 twopenny-piece, and 1 penny; the goods and monies of John Pullen, from his person.
JOHN PULLEN . I was a waiter at the King's Head tavern, Islington, but have left. At a little after five o'clock in the morning, on the 23rd of January, I had been walking about all night—I had to call at a house that morning, and I went into the Three Hats, at Islington, to wait till I could go there—I was sober, but was fatigued, and I fell asleep on the form after I had been there an hour—there were some cab men and some women there, but I do not remember seeing the prisoner—when I went to sleep I had nine sovereigns, a silver twopenny-piece, and one penny-piese, in my purse, in my trowsers pocket—I had some copper in my waistcoat pocket, but I do not know how much—I had a good hat on—this is it—( looking at it)—when I awoke, about eight o'clock, my hat and money were all gone, except a few coppers, and an old hat was left in place of mine—the prisoner was taken the same evening—I know nothing of him.
JAMES FROST . I was pot-boy at the Three Hats. The prosecutor was there—he went to sleep—I saw the prisoner come in while he was asleep, and go away before he awoke—the prosecutor then complained of his loss, and I found this old hat there.
WILLIAM PAYNE . I live at No. 19, Lower-street, Islington. I work at a plasterer's, and have known the prisoner for four years—he lived near me—on the 23rd of January he called me out of doors, and asked whether I would go and pawn a hat for him—I said I did not mind—I went in doors and came out again, and then he asked if I would go and sell it in Petticoat-lane—I went there with him, and he sold it—after he had sold it he told me he had stolen that hat from a gentleman at the Three Hats, at Islington, and had left his old hat there—he said he took 9 d. out of the gentleman's pocket, and that he got his hand under three
sovereigns, but he could not get them out—when he was taken I told all this.
DAVID BELASCO . I bought this hat of the prisoner for 4 s., in Cutler-street, Houndsditch, near Petticoat-lane—the officer came to me afterwards—I had then sold it, but I knew who I had sold it to, and we got it.
WILLIAM CHINO ( police-constable N 61.) I took the prisoner, and found the hat—the money has never been found.
GUILTY .* Aged 22.— Transported for Fifteen Years.
[trialAccount t18390204-817]
817. JOHN SMITH was indicted for stealing, on the 3rd of February, 1 half-sovereign, 1 half-crown, 4 shillings, 1 sixpence, and 1 purse, value 1 d.; the goods and monies of Mary Ann Roberts, from her person.
MARY ANN ROBEETS . I live in Norfolk-place, Waterloo-road. I was in Brydges-street, Strand, on the 3rd of February, about five o'clock in the morning—I had been out all night, and had taken a little drink, but I was quite sober, and had all my senses about me—I met a female whom I knew—she asked me if I had any money—I said I had—she said she had had nothing to eat all day—I took her into Mr. Thompson's, and gave her some bread and cheese and some gin—she then asked me to g ive her 1 s., which I did not do—I came out—I have every reason to believe the prisoner followed me out of the house, but I did not see him there—he came up to me in Brydges-street, and put his hand on my shoulder—I requested him to take it off—he then put his hand in my bosom, and took out my purse—(I had changed a sovereign and taken the change, after giving the woman what I have spoken of)—I called out "Stop thief," and the officer took him—he said, "You may search me, I have got nothing"—the officer said "No, you have thrown it away"—another officer took it up—this is my purse, and the money in it.
Prisoner. I had been with her an hour and a half before then, walking arm in arm up Brydges-street. Witness. He was not with me at all.
WILLIAM CHAD WICK ( police-constable F 71.) I was on duty, and heard the alarm—I saw the prisoner run away, pursued by this witness—I ran after him—he took something out of his pocket, and threw it down—I then took him, and he said, "I have nothing about me, search me"—I said, "It is no use searching you, you have thrown it away"—I held him till another officer came up with a lantern—he looked, and found this purse directly, with the money in it.
(Joseph Stone, a bootmaker, Horseshoe-court, Clement's-lane, and Hugh Smith, a tailor, gave the prisoner a good character.)
GUILTY . Aged 20.— Recommended to mercy by the Prosecutrix.
Transported for Ten Years.

818. LOUISA KAY was indicted for stealing, on the 24th of January, 5 napkins, value 3 s.; and 1 petticoat, value 2 s.; the goods of Joseph Dowden.

We live in Market-street, Fitzroy-square—the prisoner lived near me with her mother—I left this petticoat and napkin hanging in my room, up one pair of stairs, about six o'clock in the morning, on the 24th of January—I returned at eight o'clock at night—they were then gone—these are them.
LOUISA DOWDEN . I am the prosecutrix's daughter. I was minding the door, on the 24th of January—I had the key of the room door in my hand—the prisoner came and took it from me.

I apprehended the prisoner on the 25th—she said she knew nothing about it—she then told me she did steal the things, but did not pledge them—I asked who did—she would not tell me the girl's name, but said they were pledged at Mr. Franklin's, in Tottenham-court-road.
JAMES GOLDEN . I live with Mr. Franklin, in Tottenham Court-road—I took in these things of a woman, but not the prisoner.

820. HENRY COOK was indicted for a misdemeanor.
RICHARD HOLLAND . I am a painter and glazier, and live in Tottenham Court-road. I had known the prisoner for four or five years—on the 17th of November I saw him at the Roebuck public-house—after we had been there a few minutes he said he had in his pocket a ticket to admit two persons to the Adelphi theatre any Tuesday or Thursday during the season, and if I liked to give him a trifle I might have it—I knew he had been a stage-manager at the Clarence theatre for three yean—I gave him 4 d. for the ticket—I had no more money about me, or I might have given more—I went to the theatre on the Tuesday, and they would not let me in—the officer took the ticket from me—I believe this is it—( looking at a ticket.) Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. Did you know he was in distress? A. I believe he was, and his family also.
JOHN SHAW . I am constable of the Adelphi theatre. I was on duty, and was called up to the Pit to take this ticket—it is a forgery—the name on it is Baker—we have nobody in the house of that name—I have burnt upwards of 100 in the same hand-writing—one cheque taker took two in one night, and he had to pay for them.
FREDERICK WILLIAMS . I took the prisoner—I found at his lodging some seals with the same impression on it as is on this seal on this ticket—( read)—"Theatre Royal, Adelphi—two to the Pit any Tuesday or Thursday during the season.— HENRY BAKER."
GUILTY . Aged 39. Recommended to mercy by the Jury. — Judgment Respited. (There were two other charges against the prisoner.)

822. JOHN BOILEAU was indicted for a misdemeanor.
JAMES GRACE. I keep the Colosseum hotel, Portland-road. I have known the prisoner two years and a half or three years—he has used my house occasionally—on Saturday evening, the 5th of January, I saw him—he said he was in the army, in the 90th regiment of foot, and produced me an army order—I believed it to be genuine, and advance dhim, at his request, six sovereigns—he went away, leaving me in possession of this order—he called again, and wanted 3 l. or 4 l. more—I got an officer, and took him into custody—I parted with my money, believing the representation he made to be true.
SYDNEY CUMBERLAND. I am in the Army Pay-office. There is no
such person as John Boileau on the half-pay of the 90th regiment—there is a John Peter Boileau of that regiment—I am not acquainted with the signature of John Peter Boileau, and as many people do not use their second Christian name, it is possible this may be his—there was twelve months' pay due to John Peter Boileau, at 4 s. 6 d. a day—this is the usual order that is given—this order was presented to me—I would not pay it, as it was not filled up for John Peter Boileau—the person who presented it was not the officer.
Prisoner. Mr. Grace gave me the six sovereigns on the Saturday, and on Monday I was to call for some more—he told me to call the next day, and then I was taken—I have been on half-pay for many years, but not in the 90th regiment.
GUILTY . Aged 44.— Confined Twelve Months.
[trialAccount t18390204-823]
823. MARY COLLINS was indicted for a misdemeanor.
JOHN SHEARS . I am in the service of William Hilliar and another—they are linen-drapers. Mr. Thomas Dean, of Albion cottages, Islington, was a customer of theirs—our firm allowed that person to have goods on credit—on the 22nd of October the prisoner came and said, "I want twelve yards of black silk for a dress for Mrs. Dean"—I had known the prisoner three or four years—she said, "Mrs. Dean does not wish to give more than 3 s. or 3 s. 3 d. a yard"—she had twelve yards of black silk, which came to 1 l. 7 s.—I parted with it under the impression that her story was true, and that she came from Mrs. Dean—I did not discover it was not so till I called on Mrs. Dean.
MART ANN DEAN . I live in Albion cottages, Islington, and am the wife of Thomas Dean. I deal with Mr. Hilliar and another—I know the prisoner—I did not, on the 22nd of October, send her for any silk on my account, nor did I receive it.
JOHN NESBIT . I am an officer. I apprehended the prisoner on the 2nd of February—I asked her what she had done with the property—she said she had lost the duplicates, but it was pledged at certain pawnbrokers, which she named—I went and found the property, with the exception of one or two things, which had been taken out—this is the black silk—( producing it.)
JOHN SHEARS re-examined. This is the silk.
FRANCIS BEST . I am a pawnbroker. I took in this silk on the 23rd of October of a female, in the name of Ann Smith—I do not know who it was.
GUILTY . Aged 62.— Confined Two Years. (There were several other charges against the prisoner.)
