824. JAMES MESSENGER was indicted for stealing, on the 25th of January, part of a plough, called the ground-plate, value 3 s., and part of a plough called a mould, value 7 s., the goods of George Thomas Wanger —2nd COUNT, calling it 28lbs. of iron.
GEORGE THOMAS WANGER . I am a farmer, and live at Chingford, in Essex—I kept my plough under a shed. On the 28th of January, I
missed the ground-plate and mould-plate—I received information, and went to Walker's marine store shop on the 29th, and saw them—I do not know the prisoner—my house is about three miles from Walker's shop.
DINAH SAWKINS . I am servant to John Walker, of High-street, Tottenham. On the 28th of January, the prisoner came in between two and four o'clock—I purchased a quarter and 91bs. weight of iron of him for 1 s., at the rate of 3 s. a cwt., as cast-iron—he was in the habit of bringing bones and things there before—these two articles were among the iron.
NELSON SMITH , ( police-constable of Edmonton, No, 5.) I went with the prosecutor to Walker's shop, and found this mould and ground-plate there—I fitted them to the prosecutor's plough, and they agreed.
THOMAS WOODROFFE CRAIG . I am a policeman. I apprehended the
prisoner—I told him the charge—he said he found the iron in the marshes,
and knew nothing about stealing it.
Prisoner's Defence, I picked up these things in a ditch in the marshes—I gather old iron, bones, and things.
GUILTY . Aged 18.— Confined Nine Months.
[trialAccount t18390204-825]
825. WILLIAM HILL, alias James Hawkes, was indicted for stealing, on the 18th of January, 3 sheets, value 6 s.; 3 aprons, value 1 s. 6 d.;3 window blinds, value 1 s. 6 d.;3 handkerchiefs, value 1 s. 6 d.;1 shift, value 2 s.; 2 printed books, value 6 s.; 2 snuff-boxes, value 2 s.; 1 teacaddy, value 5 s.; and 1 work-box, value 4 s.; the goods of Richard Stroud.
RICHARD STROUD . I live at Ilford, in Essex. A man named Hawkes lodged in my house, but I do not know that it was the prisoner—I never saw him myself till he was at Worship-street—on the 19th of January, between five and six o'clock, I lost the articles stated, and saw them at Spitalfields station-house on the 21st.
GEORGE TREW ( police-constable H 125.) On the 19th of January, about four o'clock in the morning, I met the prisoner in Lamb-street, Spitalfields, with a bag—I asked what he had got—he said some linen and other articles—I asked where he brought them from—he said from Ilford, in Essex, and was going to sell them; that he was out of work and wanted victuals—I asked if the linen was marked—he said, "No"—I looked at the linen, and found it was marked "M. S." RICHARD STROUD re-examined. These articles are mine—"M S"are my wife's initials—her shift is marked "M S"—my sheets are marked.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. Where had yon left the things? A. In the sitting-room, in different places—I saw them safe about eight o'clock in the evening, when we went to bed—I cannot tell how they were taken—I believe the prisoner came home about twelve o'clock that night—his wife struck a light, and let him in—our room has no fastening to it—I came down just as he came in, and heard him talking to his wife, but I had never seen him myself—I had only been in the house five days—I know he was in distress, for when I went into their room, on missing the things, I saw nothing but a few turnips cut up, and there was no fire to cook them—the children had been crying for victuals for some days—I would employ him if the Court would allow him to go home.
(The prisoner received a good character.)
GUILTY . Aged 32.— Recommended to mercy.— Confined Fourteen Days.

827. JOHN SAWYER was indicted for stealing, on the 15th of January, 1 pair of boots, value 3 s. 6 d. the goods of John Esterford, his master.
JOHN ESTERFORD . I am a shoemaker, and live at Royal Hill, Greenwich. The prisoner was in my employ in the beginning of January—I missed boots and shoes; and one day I missed a pair while he was waiting for his work, and found them in his basket, as he was going out of the shop with them—I found another pair of my boots at the pawnbroker's.
Cross-examined by MR. HORRY. Q. Was any thing said about your overlooking it if he confessed? A. Yes, I said I would overlook about the shoes—the boots were taken first—I have given him work for a year and a half—he calls at the shop for it, and goes away with it—I am quite certain I did not give him these boots to make—I never gave him more than two pairs to make at once—these boots were all ready for sale—they did not want any thing done to them—I did not give them to him as a pattern to make others by—I may have done such a thing, but I never gave him these—I saw them hanging on the door-post before he came into the shop, and when he was gone I missed them—he promised to give me the duplicates of these boots—he gave me one, but I could not find the other.
JOHN LEVICK . I am shopman to Mr. Nash, a pawnbroker, at Greenwich. On the 15th of January the prisoner pawned these boots for 1 s.—he looked like a shoemaker, and they often pledge work.
( Property produced and sworn to.)
(The prisoner received a good character.)
GUILTY . Aged 22.— Confined Two Months.
(There were two other indictments against him.)
[trialAccount t18390204-828]
828. ELIZABETH JONES, alias Taylor, was indicted for stealing, on the 13th of January, 2 candlesticks, value 2 s.; 1 towel, value 2 d.; 1 tablecloth, value 2 d.;1 fork, value 1 d.; the goods of Joseph Wilson: and 1 handkerchief, value 1 s. 6 d., the goods of George Henry Wilson.
GEORGE HENRY WILSON . I am a labourer, and live at Woolwich with my parents. On Saturday night, the 12th of January, the prisoner asked me to give her a night's lodging—I took her home, and she slept with me down stairs—my father and mother slept up stairs—when I awoke the next morning the prisoner was gone, and I missed my handkerchief—I had not given it to her.
DANIEL RIORDAN ( police-constable R 207.) I met the prisoner in Greenwich market-place, on Sunday morning, the 13th of January, about nine o'clock—I observed a bundle under her arm, and asked if what she had belonged to herself—she said yes, it had belonged to her some time—I found she had two candlesticks, a tablecloth, a towel, and frock.
JANE WILSON . I am the wife of Joseph Wilson, a pensioner. These articles are his, except the handkerchief, which belongs to my son—the things were safe on Saturday night, at eleven o'clock.
Prisoners Defence. I was intoxicated when I met the policeman, and do not know what I said—I bought the things for 2 s. 6 d., of a man very much resembling the prosecutor, but he was dressed differently.
GEORGE HENRY WILSON re-examined, I never sold nor gave them to her—I am certain she is the woman I took home that night—my mother. did not know the prisoner was coming to the house.
GUILTY , Aged 29.— Confined Three Months.
[trialAccount t18390204-829]
829. THOMAS DYSON and GEORGE MORRIS were indicted for stealing, on the 26th of December, 1 till, value 1 s.: 2 weights, value 1 s.; 10 shillings, 12 pence, 24 halfpence, and 240 farthings; the goods and monies of John Shove.
JOHN SHOVE . I am a corn-dealer, and live at Lewisham. On the 26th of December, about five or six o'clock, I left my till safe in the shop, and went into the back parlour—my man-servant at the same time went out at the front door—I cannot say whether the till was in the shop at that precise moment, although I believe, at the time of the examination, I did recollect a circumstance which enabled me to say it was the case positively—a few minutes after leaving my shop I heard what I thought was the rattling of the scale against the desk—I went into the shop, but saw nobody there—I went to the street door, and looked round, but could not perceive any one—a customer came in in a few minutes, and I then missed my till—there had been about 5 s. in farthings, 2 s. in other copper money, and from 10 s. to 20 s. in silver, in it—the scale was directly over the till—I know the prisoner Morris by his coming to the shop for a person who was an occasional customer, and he says, and I believe it is correct, that I gave him 1 s. that afternoon as a Christmas-box—I might have given him that from the till, or from my pocket—the till is a drawer with divisions—the farthings were by themselves, the other coppers by themselves, and the silver by itself.
Cross-examined by MR. CHAMBERS. Q. What day was it you lost your till? A. The 26th, boxing-day—I recollect giving Morris 1 s. that day.
WILLIAM BEVINSTOCK . I am a labourer, and live at Lewishambridge. On the evening of the 26th of December, as I was going through the village, I saw the prisoner Dyson inside Mr. Shove's shop, leaning over the counter, and Morris looking in at the window—there was nobody else—I went on to Mr. Lindsay's, and on returning saw Dyson come outside with something under his arm—he called Morris by the name of George, and said, "Come on"—they walked together till they got into the road, and then went faster—I afterwards went to the Fox and Hounds, and saw both the prisoners down stairs in that house—Dyson asked me to fetch him a pot of ale, which I did, and he gave me twenty-four farthings to pay for it.
Dyson. I did not give him any money to fetch ale—I had some ale, and he drank with me. Witness. He asked me to drink, but I did not—I told him I had some up stairs.
Cross-examined. Q. What were you doing on this day? A. Going after some cheese for my mother—I have been in the service of Mr. Goodwin, a builder, at Lewisham—I was not charged with stealing lead—three or four of us were at work, and there was some lead missing, but Mr. Goodwin said he would employ me again—I have not been to him since—I left his service nearly a fortnight after the lead was missed—it was not as soon
as he found it out—the lead had been on the top of the house—Mr. Goodwin said nothing to me about it, only to the others—he never discharged me and told me it was for that—I will not swear he did not tell me so—he did not send me away on account of missing the lead—it was Mr. Bean, the plumber—he told Mr. Goodwin of it—he charged others besides me—I have never been in custody in my life—I was in the hands of Barham the officer once, a good bit ago—I cannot say what it was about—I cannot recollect whether I have been in the hands of any other officer.
FRANCIS DUBOIS . I keep the Fox and Hounds beer-shop at Lewisham, about two hundred yards from Mr. Shove's shop. The prisoners came there, on the 26th of December, between six and seven o'clock—I cannot say whether they came together, but they were in the room together—they had some ale—they paid for part, and other people in the room paid for part—Morris paid for his in halfpence and pence, and Dyson paid in farthings—Beviostock fetched one pot of ale from the bar, and paid me twenty-four farthings for it—Morris was in the tap-room at the time, but not within hearing.
Dyson. Q. I came into your shop about seven o'clock? A. Yes; and you were there till I shut up between ten and eleven—there were from eighteen to twenty persons in the room that night.
GEORGE BARHAM . I am a constable of Lewisham. In consequence of information I apprehended Dyson, and found on him 3 s. in silver and 4 d. in halfpence—I received a quantity of farthings from Dubois.
Cross-examined. Q. What day did you take Dyson? A. I believe it was the 1st of January—there was one examination when he only was in custody—Morris was taken the following day—I have known Morris six years, and always knew him to be an honest industrious young man.
GEORGE ABBOTT . I am a constable of Lewisham. On the 26th of December, about six o'clock in the evening or a little after, I was passing Mr. Shove's shop, and saw Dyson standing against the window, and an-other, who I cannot swear to, stood on the other side of him—there was nobody in the shop.
Cross-examined. Q. I believe you knew Morris before this transaction? A. Yes—I thought it was not Morris I saw, till I heard Bevinstock's evidence—I have known him from a child—he always bore a respectable character—I took another person before the Magistrate, who I thought it was.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18390204-830]
830. THOMAS DYSON was again indicted for stealing, on the 26th of December, 22 cigars, value 3 s. 8 d., the goods of William John Clements.
JOHN COWAN . I am apprentice to William John Clements, a hairdresser, at Lewisham, about 150 yards from Mr. Dubois. On the 26th of December, about half-past eight or near nine o'clock at night, I came down stairs, and caught the prisoner at the sash of my master's window with one hand up to his right hand pocket, and the other hand in the window up to the elbow—I asked him what he wanted—he said, nothing, but held his head down, walked to the door, and ran—I ran after him, calling "Stop thief"—he got away—I missed about half an ounce of tobacco out of a jar, and some cigars from the window, also a pair of scissors and some razors—I found some cigars on the floor—I had seen the scissors at two o'clock that afternoon—the prisoner was in a situation to have taken the things—he did not ask to buy any thing, but ran out.
Prisoner. I did not go into his shop at all—it was said that a person in a flannel jacket took the cigars—Mr. Dubois knows I was in his house all the evening. Witness. I swear it was him—I am not mistaken—he ran over Lewisham-bridge, in a contrary direction to Dubois—he had a flannel jacket on like he has now—I knew him before by sight, but not by name—I am sure it was not Bevinstock—I am positive the prisoner is the lad.
Prisone. I had on a blue jacket that night.
FRANCIS DUBOIS . My beer-shop is about 200 yards from Clement's shop—the prisoner was at my house on the evening of the 26th—I cannot say whether he went out in the course of the evening—he came between six and seven o'clock, and he was there when I shut up at a quarter to eleven o'clock—nobody could go out without passing my bar, but I should not notice a person going out—there were eighteen or twenty in the house—if he had gone out, I should not have missed him—if a person ran over Lewisham-bridge, he could not get to my house, without returning over the bridge again, without he went some miles round—I did not notice what jacket the prisoner had on—he has brothers very much like him in appearance.
GEORGE BARHAM . I apprehended the prisoner on the 1st of January, on this charge—I found on him three shillings in silver, and fourpence in half-pence—he was dressed in a flannel jacket—he was at work at the time I took him, as a bricklayer's labourer—I have seen him in different dresses—I have seen him in a dark jacket—he sent for one to put on at the time I put him in the cage.
GEORGE ABBOTT . I saw the prisoner on the night of the 26th, a little after six o'clock—I can hardly say how he was dressed, but I think he had a bluejacket on.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18390204-831]
831. CHARLOTTE BARTON was indicted for stealing, on the 13th of January, I sovereign, 2 half-crowns, 2 shillings, and 1 half-penny, the monies of James Thomas Francis, from his person.
JAMES THOMAS FRANCIS . I am a waterman. On the 13th of January, between nine and ten o'clock at night, I was walking up High-street, Deptford, in company with a friend, and went into the Centurion to have some refreshment—the prisoner came in while I was there, and called me by name—I took no notice of her, but came out, and went to the Lord Duncan—I came out of there, and walked down High-street—I got into conversation with her, and between Downing-street and Effingham-place, she put her hand into my waistcoat pocket, took out my money, and ran away with it—I ran after her, caught her, and took her into the Lord Duncan, where my friend was—I told him I had been robbed of my money—he asked her if she had any money about her—she said she had not a farthing—he saw she had something in her mouth, put his hand to her cheek, and found there was some money there—she directly ran out—I ran out after her, called a policeman, and gave her in charge—one half-crown, one shilling, and one half-penny were found in her mouth.
EDWARD CULMER . I was with the prosecutor on the 13th of January—in consequence of what he told me, I asked the prisoner if she had any money about her—she said she had not—I said, "If you have, you had better give it up to the young man"—she said she had no money at all about her—I said, "Don't say so, you have some in your mouth"—I put
my hand up to her cheek, and found money rattle in her mouth—she ran out—Francis followed, and gave her in charge.
Prisoner. They were intoxicated. Witness. We were not.
HENRY GUY ( police-constable R 159.) On Sunday night, the 13th of January, I was on duty in Broadway, Deptford, and saw Francis and the prisoner—he told me she had robbed him of 1 l. 7 s., and desired me to take her into custody—I asked her if she had done so—she denied having taken any money at all, or having any—I took her to the station-house, and asked her to give me what she had in her mouth—she said she had nothing—I then took her by the throat, fearing she might swallow it, and she spat out a half-crown, and one shilling and one half-penny—the prosecutor was perfectly sober.
Prisoner's Defence. I met the prosecutor on this evening while I was in company with a young woman—he gave me a quartern of rum to drink at the Centurion—I said I would not stop there, as it was so public—we then went to the Lord Duncan, and had something there—he said he wanted to speak to me, and we went out—he said, "Cannot I go home with you?"—I said, "No, you cannot"—he said, "Cannot you come up here with me?"—I went with him up High-street—he was so tipsy he could hardly walk, and was very dirty—he then said be had lost his money—we went to the Lord Duncan, and Culmer asked me if I had his money—I said no, I had none but my own—they began to pull me about, and fearing I should lose my money, I put it into my mouth.
J. T. FRANCIS re-examined. The prisoner came to me in the Centurion, and I gave her something to drink there—I gave her something at the Lord Duncan also—I have known her from quite a child—her father and mother are dead—they were very respectable—her father was a warrant-officer—I never had any connexion with her in my life, but I understand she gets her living by walking the streets.
GUILTY . Aged 22.— Recommended to mercy. — Confined One Month.
[trialAccount t18390204-832]
832. JOHN TAYLOR was indicted for stealing, on the 23rd of January, 4 handkerchiefs, value 17 s., the goods of William Whistler.
CHARLES STEWART WARDEN . I am a constable of Woolwich. I produce some silk handkerchiefs which I received on Wednesday evening, the 23rd of January, from the serjeant-major of the rifle brigade—in consequence of information, I went to Mr. Whistler's shop at Greenwich, and afterwards to the barracks, and took the prisoner—I told him the charge—he said he had received the handkerchiefs from a girl at Deptford.
JOSEPH WILKINSON . I am a sergeant-major in the rifle brigade, stationed at Woolwich. The prisoner was a private in the corps—on the morning of the 23rd of January, I sent for him, and asked him if three handkerchiefs given to me by Ford, belonged to him—he said they did, and they were given to him by a girl at Deptford—he had one round his neck of the same pattern—I desired him to take that off, and reported it to the commanding officer, who desired me to give them to the constable to make inquiry about them—there was room for a dozen handkerchiefs under his waistcoat.
WILLIAM FORD . I am a sergeant in the rifle brigade. On the morning of the 23rd of January, I found a piece of silk handkerchiefs in a spare bed adjoining the prisoner's, concealed inside the mattrass—the bed was almost knocked down by nobody sleeping on it, and I doubled it up again
—I went into the yard, and found the prisoner had one of the same pattern round his neck—I asked him if those handkerchiefs belonged to him—he said yes, and that a girl gave them to him at Deptford.
JOHN WHISTLER . I assist my father, William Whistler, a draper at Greenwich. These silk handkerchiefs belong to him—I missed them, to the best of my recollection, on Tuesday evening, the 22nd of January—I had seen two riflemen in the shop that afternoon—I cannot recognise the prisoner as being one of them.
JAMES GOODWIN . I am a private in the rifle brigade. On Tuesday afternoon, the 22nd of January, I went to the prosecutor's shop with the prisoner, and bought a pair of stockings—the prisoner was dressed in his undress clothing, as he is now—I went from there to Deptford, and got to the barracks about eight o'clock in the evening—the prisoner parted company with me for about three-quarters of an hour in Deptford—I did not notice the handkerchiefs in the shop.
Prisoner. Q. Was not I talking to you all the time I was in the shop? A. Yon were in the shop with me, and spoke to me about the stockings you wanted—you went across to the other side of the shop, and asked the price of another pair—the gentleman said they were 2 s. 8 d., and yon said it was too dear.
COURT. Q. Have the soldiers pockets in their clothes. A. Only watchpockets, unless we have them put ourselves—his waistcoat was buttoned up in the shop.
Prisoner. I had these clothes on, and could not conceal any thing.
GUILTY . Aged 22.— Transported for Seven Years.
Before Mr. Common Sergeant.

833. GEORGE SMITH, alias Batchelor, was indicted for stealing, on the 2nd of January, 1 coat, value 20 s., the goods of Samuel Burrows.
SAMCEL BURROWS . I keep a public-house in New-street, Deptford The prisoner came there on the 2nd of January) and bad a bed from Wednesday till Friday—he did not come on Friday night, but came again on Saturday, and about six o'clock that afternoon went out, faying be was going a little way, and should return that night—he did not come back, and I missed my coat five minutes after he was gone—he owed me about 4 s. 9 d.—he was taken on the Wednesday following—be belongs to the 1st rifle brigade, and told me he was on furlough—this is my—( looking at it.) SARAH W ——. My husband is a pawnbroker at Greenwich The prisoner pawned this coat for 12 s. on Saturday night, the 5th of January, about eight o'clock—I asked particularly if it was his own, and he said it was. [trialAccount t18390204-834]
834. WILLIAM WALLER was indicted for feloniously assaulting Henry Walker Weatherstone on the 29th of January, putting him in fear and taking from his person, and against his will, 4 keys, value 10 d.; 1 key ring, value 3 d.: 6 half-crowns; 4 shillings; and 6 sixpences; his goods and monies.
HENRY WALKER WEATHERSTONE . I am master of a merchant vessel. On the 29th of January I was going down to my ship, and went to the Golden Anclior at Woolwich, to get refreshment at noon—I met the prisoner
there, and treated him with a pot of ale—being intoxicated myself, I asked him to accompany me down to my ship—I was able to walk—he took me to a brothel in Woolwich—I do not know the name of the street—two girls came down stairs—I wished to retreat, and moved towards the door—the prisoner came and shoved me down on the floor—I received no bodily injury—when I was down, he put his hand into my pocket, and took 23 s. or 24 s. from my right hand breeches pocket—I am sure I had full that, and I might have had more—I made no resistance, further than taking his regimental cap off his head and I kept it—I called, "Police"—a woman appeared from an adjoining house, and called the prisoner by name, "Waller" as he was making his escape—she said something to him besides calling his name, but I cannot say what—I did not hear him make any reply—he went away with my money—I took his cap to the Arsenal the same night—this was between five and six o'clock—I had been in the prisoner's company all that time—I believe he was drunk too—be had some gin beside the ale I gave him—I was not drunk when I first met him—I am quite sure that the money was taken from me—I received no hurt—he bad three comrades with him at the Golden Anchor, but we left them there—I only gave them one pot of ale—I missed a bunch of keys, besides my money—I have not seen them since—I had got 9 l. in gold in
my fob, which was not taken.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. Did the prisoner take you to the house at your request? A. Not to my knowledge—I think not—I was intoxicated, and cannot say whether he asked me to go there, or I asked him—I could see he was a soldier, and knew where to find him—I cannot tell how far the house was from the Golden Anchor—there were two women, the prisoner, and myself, in the room—I did not know the sort of house I was going to—I was standing with my back to the door, when this took place—the two women were going down stairs—they made their escape up-stairs, when I was thrown down—I saw them in the lockup house next day—they were charged with pawning a Petersham great-coat, which I had left there when I went to the Arsenal—my silver was in my right pocket—I was thrown down on my back—I was not very drunk, but being a stranger I wished the prisoner to accompany me home—the women were on the opposite side of the room when I was thrown down—I did not see any body else in the room—it was a good large room—I was not struck—I think I was thrown backward by his arm—I saw his arm—I had not changed a sovereign or any thing at the public-house—I had changed a half-sovereign the night before—I had not counted my money at the public-house—I had not been to any other public-house.
ANN JOHNSTONE . I am a widow, and live at Woolwich. I let the house next door to me to a tenant—about six o'clock in the evening of the 29th of January, I was sitting in my own house, and heard a dreadful call of "Police" and "Murder" in the adjoining house—I beard a second call, and then I went and saw the prosecutor on the floor, and the prisoner on the top of him, but in what exact manner I cannot exactly tell, as the fire was low and there was no candle—I called out, "What in the name of goodness is there amiss?"—the prisoner jumped up, and I could see by his clothes that he was a gunner—the prosecutor lay still, as if confused or stunned—I called for a light, which was brought in a few moments, and two or three persons came—the prosecutor still lay on the floor, and then I could see the prisoner who was
standing on the floor by him—I knew him directly the candle came, and I said, "I know you, Waller, you good-for-nothing man"—he said, "No, you don't know me"—I said, "Yes, I do; and you will find that tomorrow"—when the prosecutor got up he said he had got the cap of the soldier who had ill-used him—a gunner came in after the candle was brought—I did not know him.
Cross-examined. Q. What sort of a house is this, is it not a brothel? A. Of course it is your business to say so, but I know nothing of the kind—I never heard it called a brothel—it is not one, I swear that—there are only two rooms, which are let to two soldiers and their wives, as far as I know—I do not know the two young women who came down stairs—I have a man and his wife lodging in my own house named Evans—they are creditable people.
JOHN WELFORD . I am a gunner in the Royal Artillery, the prisoner is also a gunner—he belonged to the same room as me at, the time of this transaction—I missed my cap on the Monday morning in question, and found the prisoner's cap on my bed—I had not seen him that morning—there were two beds in the room—the prisoner slept in the other—he did not sleep at home on Sunday night—I had seen my cap safe on Sunday—I was not in Johnstone's house on Monday evening, I was on duty—this I is the cap I lost.
Cross-examined. Q. Have you known the prisoner some time? A. Yes—he has borne an irreproachable character for honesty.
SAMUEL WATTS . I am a constable. On Tuesday evening about six o'clock, the prosecutor came to me at a public-house, where I was attending an inquest on a convict, and applied to me to take a soldier in custody, who had rubbed him—he said he did not know his name, but he had got his cap, and had taken it to the Arsenal—I went to the Arsenal, and this cap was delivered to me—I have had it ever since—the prosecutor was intoxicated then, but seemed to have a knowledge of what he was saying—I took the prisoner up that night, with three others, for being disorderly in the street—he was intoxicated—the prosecutor saw him next morning and recognised him—5 s. 2 d. was found on the prisoner.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18390204-835]
835. THOMAS SCOTT was indicted for stealing, on the 16th of June ' 3 watches, value 15 l., the goods of Samuel M'Clellan, in his dwelling-house.
SAMUEL M'CLELLAN . I am a watchmaker, and live in Broadway, Deptford. On the 19th of June last I missed two gold watches and one silver one—I had seen them on the 15th hanging in my shop, at the side of the shop—they were worth 15 l.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. You did not see any body take them? A. No—I have seen the prisoner in the Broadway.
DANIEL LAZARUS DAVIS . I live in High-street, Chatham. On the 17th of July I took a watch in pledge, in the name of Benjamin Bone—I do not know the person who pawned it—there was "B. Bone" scratched in the case when he brought it to me—I have never seen the prisoner that I remember.
JOHN BONIFACE . I am a pastry-cook, and live at Deptford. On the 17th of July I met the prisoner in the Borough market, and went with him to Chatham by steam, and just beyond Gravesend, on the Rochester canal, he offered me a silver watch for sale—I did not buy it—I looked at it—it had
the name of "B. Bone" scratched inside the case—he said he had got two gold watches also to sell, but did not produce them—when we got to Chatham I saw him go into Mr. Davis's house, and pawn the silver one—he told me when he came out that he had got 30 s. on it, and I saw that on the duplicate which he had—I did not ask where he got the watch—I know this to be the watch—( looking at it)—there were two B's in it when it was given to me—I do not find them there now—it was scratched in a similar manner to this inside, as if a name had been erased—I cannot swear this is the watch—I do not think this is the same watch, because the name was scratched deeply in.
DANIEL LAZARUS DAVIS re-examined. I did not make any alteration in the watch, and you will find the name there now—I can see it now, though the witness cannot.
MR. PHILLIPS to JOHN BONIFACE. Q. Can you read? A. Yes—the watch I saw had "B. Bone" in it—it is inside one of these cases now—I was not aware it opened with a double case, and I only looked in one case—I see it now in this inner case—I have not seen it since the 17th of July, last year—I have known the prisoner for years—I have not quarrelled with him latterly—I have not spoken two words with him by way of anger—he served his apprenticeship to my father—we have had no quarrel—he never told me he had exchanged his watch with another person for his—I did not get any of this mosey—he did not lend me 10 s. to buy an oven—he gave me change for a sovereign—I did not borrow any thing of him.
COURT. Q. Can yon now take on yourself to swear positively that is the watch he showed you? A. It is—he told me that he wrote the name "B. Bone" in it himself.
WILLIAM PHIPPS . I am a police inspector. I went with the prosecutor to look for his watch at Davis's, at Chatham—the watch now produced was shown to me there—I then went and apprehended the prisoner—he at first denied having been to Chatham to pawn the watch, but on the Sunday morning he sent word that he wanted to speak to me—I brought him into the charge room—he said he wished to speak the truth, that he pawned the watch, but he had got it from Michael Maddox—I took Maddox into custody afterwards—he was remanded for a week, and then discharged.
Cross-examined. Q. You found there was a Michael Maddox? A. Yes—the prisoner gave me a minute description of him—Maddox denied all knowledge of it—I had a knowledge of him before—he is a very bad character—he lived at Deptford, but at present lives at Camberwell.
SAMUEL M'CLELLAN re-examined, I hare the cap of the watch now, which was left behind on my premises—it fits this watch—I have not a doubt of the watch—I have not found the gold ones.
MR. PHILLIPS called WILLIAM BONIFACE. I am brother to the witness who has been examined, and live in Church-street, Woolwich. I have known the prisoner about eleven years—he lived with me about four years, and then was apprenticed to my father—he was with him five years—my father died, and then he returned to me, and remained about twelve or sixteen months—he always bore a very honest character—I remember his having a silver watch—I gave it to him myself—the one produced is not it—I have seen this watch before, while he was working for me, in June last—he took it out of his pocket, and gave me an account of how he came to have this watch, and not the one I had given him.
COURT. Q. Did you examine the watch particularly? A. No—I was very busy at the time, and did not pay any attention to it—I never saw him with any gold watch.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18390204-836]
836. JOHN FREDERICK was indicted for feloniously breaking and entering the shop of Henry Simmons, on the 13th of January, and stealing therein eight horse-shoes, value 4 s., his goods.
JAMES STARTUP . I am a blacksmith, in the employ of Henry Simmons, who has a little house near the cage, in the Greenwich-road. On Monday morning, about seven o'clock, when I went to work, I missed some horse-shoes off some pegs in the workshop, under a window, which I had seen safe on Saturday afternoon, when I left work—the wire-work which had been nailed over the window was torn away sufficiently to get a hand in, and the frame-work broken in—the glass had been broken before, and the wire was put to prevent a hand being put in.
WILLIAM PULLEY . I am a policeman. On Monday, the 14th of January, I was in the yard of the Horse and Groom, at Blackheath, when the prisoner offered some horse-shoes for sale—I asked him where he got them—he said he found them in Back-lane, Blackheath—I asked if any one was with him when he found them—he said his younger brother Was, and they were wrapped up in an old horse-cloth—I told him to go and fetch his brother, and the cloth they were in—I waited for him half an hour—he did not return, and I went in search of him—I found him on Blackheath-hill afterwards, and took him into custody.
Cross-examined by MR. CARDEN. Q. Were you in the yard when the prisoner came in? A. No, I was called in and found him there—I heard the smith ask what he wanted for them, and he said 1 s.—this was full half a mile from the prosecutor's—they are all new shoes.
JOHN CLINKER . I am a farrier, at Blackheath-hill. The prisoner came to ray shop, and offered these eight shoes for sale—I called Pulley's attention to it, who was standing outside the shop, and asked the prisoner what he wanted for them—he said 1 s.—I asked where he got them—he said he found them on Back-hill, in a sack—I said, if he brought the sack I might tell who they belonged to—he was away a good while, and the policeman went and took him—he said he did not steal them—they are worth 3 s. ( Property produced and sworn to) NOT GUILTY .
Before Mr. Recorder.

841. JOHN FREDERICK FRANCK was indicted for embezzlement.
LEIGH WEAVER . I am a linen-draper, and live at Greenwich. The prisoner was my cashier—the shopmen have each separate books, in which they enter what they receive, and take it to the cashier at his desk—they give him a small cheque—here are three of the cheques for the sums stated—the prisoner has entered in his own book the right amounts, and afterwards erased the "ten," and altered the young men's books as well to make them tally—in each case he has erased the "1" with a pen, so that he
did not account to me for the sums he received, but minus the ten shillings.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. The books are kept by the young men? A. Yes, and where there has been 14 s. 2 d. inserted in pencil, the "1" has been rubbed out—I have no knowledge of when it was done—the cashier accounts to me—I have all the books here.
ALFRED SELWOOD. I serve in the prosecutor's shop. I keep a book—this is it—it is not dated but to the 34th cheque I paid the cashier 14 s. 21/2 d., and to the 39th cheque 15 s. 51/2 d., which have been both altered, and the "1" erased.
HENRY BROOME . I am assistant to Mr. Weaver. I paid 11 s. 10 d. to the prisoner—the "1" has been erased in my book.
NOT GUILTY .

843. WILLIAM ROBBS was indicted for stealing, on the 7th of January, 2 hampers, value 2 s. 6 d. the goods of Thomas Moody.
OLIVIA MOODY . I am the wife of Thomas Moody, a basket-maker, at Greenwich. On the 7th of January, these hampers were safe at my door, at seven o'clock in the evening, at half-past seven o'clock I found them at the station-house—the small one I swear to—I lost one like the other, and believe it is mine.
WILLIAM MOODY . I know this large hamper—I made it myself.
GEORGE HARRIS ( police-constable R 158.) I fell in with the prisoner at a quarter-past seven o'clock that evening, two or three hundred yards from the prosecutor's—he had a military cloak on, and something under it—as he passed me he said, "My knapsack is d—d heavy"—I went after him and said, "What have you got?"—he said, "Baskets, to be sure"—I asked what he was going to do with them—he said he did not know, he was going to Woolwich, and he had got a pass—I said he must go with me—he went a little way, then threw off his cloak aid the hamper, and became very violent—he collared me, and I collared him—he tried to bite my face—he knocked my hat off—he bit my arm and drew blood—he had been drinking.
Cross-examined. Q. What did you find on him? A. 3 s. 6 d.
ALEXANDER MIDDLETON . I am a sergeant of the Scotch Fuasileer Guards. The prisoner belonged to that regiment—his conduct has been remarkably good, but he had a fracture on his head—I have seen him when he has been drinking, and then he does not know what he is doing.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18390204-844]
844. JOSEPH BRICE was indicted for stealing, on the 5th of December, 123/4 lbs. weight of mutton, value 6 s., the goods of Richard Beaumont Burnaby.
JAMBS MORRIS , I am a constable of Plumstead. Mr. Richard Beaumont Burnaby is a captain in the Royal Artillery, and lives at the academy, on Woolwich-common—I know the prisoner—his friends are very respectable
—his father is a pensioner of the Royal Artillery—on the 5th of December, about eight o'clock at night, I saw him with something under his arm at the corner of a plantation attached to the academy—I said, "What have you got there?"—he dropped it and ran—I said, "It is no use to run away, I know you"—I pursued him to Jackson's-lane, and there lost him—I returned to where he had dropped something, and found a leg and loin of mutton—I knew the prisoner, but I did not know where he lived—his jacket was afterwards found at his father's, in Jackson's-lane—it had a small piece of meat on it and some marks of fat.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. Are you in the police? A. No—the prisoner was afterwards found in bed—I was not told he had been in bed ever since a little after seven o'clock—I went to the house of one Collins that night—I did not tell him that it was a lad of the name of Smith—I have never said so—I have been accused of forging a Magistrate's name to an order for payment of money—I am not aware that any boys were ever taken up, and committed on my oath, and that the Magistrate afterwards discharged them on its being proved that I had sworn falsely—I do not know that there is a warrant now against me for forgery—I believe there is.
DAVID GRAHAM . I am in the service of Captain Burnaby. This mutton is his, and was taken from the safe—I have no mark on it, but he lost some like this.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18390204-845]
845. ROBERT GEE was indicted for stealing, on the 8th of January, 1 box, value 5 s. 6 d. the goods of Lewis Davis.
JOSEPH WEIGHT . I am a constable of Woolwich. On the 8th of January, about six o'clock in the evening, I saw the prisoner take a box out of Mr. Davis's shop, and run away—I pursued, and brought him back—he had to reach inside to get it.
JAMES LAMB . I am assistant to Mr. Lewis Davis, of Tower-street, Woolwich, a pawnbroker. This box is his—we had some like this standing inside the door—I could not swear whether it was sold or stolen.
JAMES HENRY WATTS . I am an ironmonger. I was with Weight, and saw the prisoner take the box from inside Mr. Davis's shop.
Prisoner. Q. Had I any thing with me? A. Yes, a bundle containing a gown.
Prisoner's Defence. I went to that shop on some business of my own—I cannot say how I got the box, but I must have paid for it—I had the fever very bad in Jamaica, and since that I sometimes do not know what I am doing.
GUILTY . Aged 30.— Recommended to mercy by the Jury. Confined Four Days.
Before Mr. Recorder.

846. WILLIAM SMITH . was indicted for stealing, on the 16th of January, 1 house-tie, value 2 s., and 1 weight, value 1 d., the goods of William Pershouse, his master.
GEORGE JAMES PULLEN . I am servant to Mr. William Pershouse, a coach-master and a farmer, who lives at Greenwich. Some pieces of iron were taken from a wall that was pulled down, and put into a loft of his—the
prisoner had worked there as a labourer—the policeman brought this piece of iron and weight to my master.
JOHN DENNIS . I made this house-tie for Mr. Pershouse, nearly two years ago—I made three more at the time, and it cost two shillings, or half-a-crown—as old iron, it is worth 6 d. or 8 d.
JOHN WALKER ( police-constable R 182.) I met the prisoner with a bag on his back, on Tuesday, the 15th of January—I asked him what he had got there—he said it was no business of mine—I took the bag from him—he ran away, about thirty yards—I then overtook him—he said he had just picked the bag up in the road—it was quite dry, but it was a very muddy night—it contained this 1/4 cwt., and this iron tie.
Prisoner. Q. Did I not say that a man threw it out of the loft for me to sell? A. You said, when yon returned from the Magistrate, that a man named Banks threw it out of the loft, and told you to sell it for old iron—Banks has since absconded.
GEORGE JAMES PULLEN . re-examined. We had a weight of this desoription, and I believe this is it.
Prisoner. Q. Was I ever in the loft? A. Yes, occasionally, I believe, I and Banks was in the loft.
Prisoner's Defence. I picked them op in the road—I had not worked at the place for some time.
GUILTY . Aged 70.— Confined One Month.
Before Mr. Sergeant Arabin.

847. ANN PHILLIPS was indicted for stealing, on the 22nd of November, 1 coat, value 10 s.; 2 shawls, value 10 s.; and 1 tippet, value 2 s. 6 d., the goods of Louisa Jackson.
LOUISA JACKSON . I live at Deptford, and am single. The prisoner lodged in my house—her husband is a whitesmith—they came there on the 4th of June—this property was in a trunk which was locked, and was in her room—on the 19th of January, I found the trunk broken open, and these things were gone—the prisoner had left then—I spoke to her about it—she said she had done wrong, and I was to give charge of her at the police-office—this is my property—( looking at it.)
JAMES BUCHANAN . I am in the service of a pawnbroker at Deptford—I have a coat and shawl, pawned in the name of Ann Phillips—I do not know who by, but the prisoner pawned a pair of boots in the same name.
HAMMOND SMITH . I took the prisoner into custody. The duplicates were found in the box which the property was taken from.
MART WARNER DALTON . On the 19th of January the prisoner brought
this tippet to me for sale, between nine and ten o'clock in the morning—I bought it of her.
Prisoner. I am sorry for what I have done, and throw myself on the
mercy of the Court.
GUILTY . Aged 64.— Confined Six Months.

848. HENRY JAMES GARDNER was indicted for stealing, on the 19th of January, 1 pair of spectacles, value 7 s., the goods of George King; and MARY GARDNER , for feloniously receiving the same, well knowing them to have been stolen; against the Statute, &c.
MARY KING. I am the wife of George King—we keep a cookshop in Butcher-row, Deptford. Henry James Gardner came on the 19th of January,
between three and four o'clock, for a basin of soup—I left him there while I went to warm the soup—after he was gone I missed my spectacles—I knew him before.
MARY WHITCOMBE . I live with my mother. Mrs. Gardner asked me to go on an errand for her—I said I would—it was to pawn a pair of spectacles in the name of "Johnson, No, 5, Rope-walk," for 2 s.—I pawned them, and gave her the money.
Mary Gardner. I did not tell you you were to pawn them in the name of Johnson, it was Henry told you so, and if they asked you where, to say, the Rope-walk. Witness. It was you told me so.
JAMES FANCHON . I am a pawnbroker. I took in these spectacles in the name of "Mary Johnson, Rope-walk."
BENJAMIN LOVELL . I am a police-sergeant. I got the duplicate from Mary Gardner, and found the spectacles.
( Property produced and sworn to.)
Henry James Gardner's Defence. I went for the soup, and on the form was the spectacles—I asked my mother to ask the little girl to go and pawn them for me—I told my mother that a chap owed me some money, and be gave me these to pawn.
HENRY JAMES GARDNER — GUILTY . Aged 17. Confined Three Months.
MARY GARDNER.— NOT GUILTY .

849. WILLIAM HOLLIS was indicted for stealing, on the 19th of January, 6 pairs of combs, value 2 s., the goods of Mary Ann Davies.
MARY ANN DAVIES . I keep a stationer's shop at Deptford—I sell combs. On the 19th of January the prisoner came into my shop to offer matches for sale—I did not want any—I had an opportunity of seeing him, and I believe he is the boy—soon after he was gone I missed six pairs of combs from the window—it was then a little after twelve o'clock—soon afterwards the prisoner was taken by the officer—I saw these six pairs of combs, which I believe are mine—they were all fastened together when I lost them, but they have been cut.
Cross-examined by MR. HORRY. Q. You have a good business? A. Not very good—I never saw the prisoner before—I was in the parlour when he came into the shop—there is a glass-door to the parlour—I was in the shop about three minutes before—there is a sliding-board at the back of the window, and that was open—I had looked into the window a very short time before, and had set the board to rights—I believe these combs are mine—they are very common—I merely speak to them from having seen them frequently, and from their size.
URIAH EDEN . ( police-constable R 81.) I took these combs from the prisoner at five o'clock that day—he was offering them for sale in Church-street, which is a mile from the prosecutrix's—he had another in a handkerchief—as they were common combs, I let him go—he was afterwards taken by another officer—I met him again about twenty minutes after.
GUILTY .** Aged 13.— Transported for Seven Years—To the Convict Ship.
SURREY CASES.
Before Mr. Recorder.
