850. CHRISTIANA SPICE was indicted for stealing, on the 4th of January, I tablecloth, value 10 s.; 1 petticoat, value 4 s.; 1 handkerchief, value 2 s. 6 d.;1 pillow, value 2 s.; 1 apron, value 1 s.; 1 shawl, value 1 s.; 1 pair of pattens, value 3 d.; and 1 pair of stockings, value 3 d.; the goods of Sarah Grubb.
SARAH GRUBB . I am a widow, and live in King-street, Blarkfriars-road. I employed the prisoner to look after my children in my absence, and paid her 6 d. a day—she found her own food—on Friday, the 4th of January, I left her in charge of the children, and on returning, about seven o'clock in the evening, she was gone—I missed the articles stated, which are worth more than 1 l.—the prisoner boarded and lodged in the house for three months—I found her again on the 6th of January.
WILLIAM WEST . I am shopman to Mr. Wharton, pawnbroker, Mount-street, Westminster-road. I produce a tablecloth, pawned by the prisoner on the 4th of January.
ROBERT OVENDEN . I am a policeman. I took the prisoner in charge. I found the shawl on her, the pattens on her feet, and the stockings on her legs—I found duplicates of the other property on her, which the pawnbrokers have given up. ( Property produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner's Defence, I am not guilty of taking any thing—the apron was lent me—the other things I certainly had.
GUILTY . Aged 17.— Confined Three Months.
Before Mr. Justice Vaughan.
[trialAccount t18390204-851]
851. JOHN PASSFIELD was indicted for killing and slaying John Edward Passfield.
JOHN BOULTER . I am a turner. I have known the prisoner five or six years—he is a plasterer, bricklayer, and carpenter; a handy sort of man, and got his living in that way—he has worked for me—he did three weeks' work for me about five weeks ago—he finished it on the 17th of November—the deceased died on the 29th of December—the prisoner had about five days and three quarters' work from me between those times—he had two children, one girl and one boy, (the deceased,) who was between six and seven years old—his wife has been dead about two years—he had a guinea a week when he worked for me—he would have the means of supporting his family, when in employment—I believe he sometimes got more from other people—during part of the time he worked for me, he lived in Robert-street, or Ann-street—he never rented the place in Counter alley—he merely lived there on sufferance—on the 28th of December, the day before his child died, I went there with the policeman—the door was open, and we walked into the room, on the ground-floor—the girl and boy were both lying in one corner of the room on some shavings—the policeman asked the boy whether he had had any victuals that day—he said "Yes," he had had some bread—he did not say who had given it to him—there was nothing in the room but a few empty baskets—no kind of furniture at all, nor any fire—I asked the child where his father was, he said be did not know—in consequence of what I saw, I immediately went to the beadle
—he was not at home—I left my card, requesting he would attend to the case early in the morning—the beadle did not come down next morning, and in the after part of the day I sent my boy, who brought word that the child was dead—I went again to the beadle, who was not at home, and left word that the child was dead—the child had had the hoopingcough—it coughed while I was there—and I understand he had the measles, but I do not know—I have known the prisoner six or seven years—he might have done better for his children, if disposed—there was neglect, I think—I always found him very willing to work when I sent for him—I know he could not always find employment, because he applied to me for work.
JURY. Q. DO you consider him in full possession of his intellect? A. I know nothing to the contrary—I never observed any deficiency at all—he was very handy in what he undertook to do—the whole employment he had from me since the 17th of November, was five days and three quarters—I believe he had a little work elsewhere, but he wanted to work for me, I had some which I said I could not have done till after Christmas—I really think, at the time, that he was in great need of the means of providing for himself and children—he had three quarters of a day's work on the 30th of November.
MARGARET PASSFIELD . I am the prisoner's daughter, and am thirteen years old. I think my brother was between six and seven years—I do not know the day of the month he died—he had been ill about three weeks or a month before he died—he complained of his head being bad—he had the measles, I believe, and he used to cough—I never heard him hoop—he had had a cough a good while before he died—he died in Counter-alley—I remember Mr. Boulter coming there the day before he died—we had nothing but a few shavings there, no bed or bed-clothes—we had something to eat that day and the day before—my father gave us all he could—he was not in employment at the time my brother died—when my father had food he gave it to us as well as took it himself—I never knew him take food himself and not give us any—he did not go and ask for medical assistance when my brother was ill—I had had some bread to eat the day Mr. Boulter came, and my brother too—my father had not long been gone out when Mr. Boulter came—he was gone to see and get us some victuals—he slept in the same room with us, on the shavings—he always gave us part of what he had—he was pretty kind to us—we had been living there about a month, I believe—my brother had a very bad cough—he coughed at night—my father would sometimes spend his money somewhere else—he used to get us in some victuals first, and then he used to go out, leaving me and my brother in the house by ourselves—he sometimes came home drunk, not very often, and not when we were without food—we have not been a day, I think, without something or other—we sometimes got bread and dripping, and sometimes bread and potatoes—sometimes we had bread given to us—sometimes people gave us victuals, and sometimes they would not—my brother had a coat given to him, and a pair of trowsers my father gave him, and a shirt my father bought him—I know Mrs. Bantlin—my father slept at her place once on the floor—my brother was well then—it was before he was taken bad, not long before—we had only some old pieces of baize and carpets over us—my brother had no doctor—he was very bad for about three weeks or a month, and about a day or two before he died he could not stand up on his legs—he died on Saturday—the
night before he died he had some gruel and bread, and ate very heartily—he had as much as he wished—we were not in great want of food while we were in Counter-alley—we were never without some supply of food—my father did not give my brother the gruel and bread—Mr. Boulter sent the gruel—when my father was in employment he supplied us with food and firing—we came from the workhouse about two years or a year and a half ago, at Nessing, in Essex—we had not enough victuals there, and my father took us out—he was in the workhouse also—I did not tell him we had not enough food there—he knew it himself—he knew how much we had—he used to give my brother part of the food given to him, because he was in the same ward—he was kind to my brother.
JURY. Q. Did your brother's legs swell much before his death? A. No; my father got drunk when he got work, but he would get us in some victuals first, and then go out—he would get us about two quartern loaves, or a quartern and a half, and coals and potatoes, and a bit of dripping—he would then go to the public-house, and come home about eight or nine o'clock, sometimes.
FRANCES BANTLIN . I am the wife of William Bantlin, a traveller. I have known the prisoner ten years—we rent an apartment in the house, in Counter-alley, Newington—we moved there on the 19th of November, and the prisoner came to help us move—he then lived in Ann-street—the prisoner asked me, as it was so late that sight, to let him stop there, and he made shift on the floor—the children were afterwards brought there, while I was away, not to the same house as me, but the next house—I believe he gave his children part of what he had himself, as far as laid in his power—I believe he was out of work about the time of the child's death—I do not think he neglected them through getting liquor, but one and another person, who knew him in his trade, I believe, gave him liquor—I do not think it was at his own expense—I do not think, while he was there, he had sufficient to support the children—I have seen him give them food—they never complained to me that he neglected them—I understand that the children's mother, on her death-bed, requested he would not put them in the workhouse—I saw the child the evening before he died, and he looked very bad—(I went in with Mr. Boulter's boy)—I had not seen him for two or three days before—he used to come up, and sit by my fire-side, when the father had not power to get a fire—he had not the hooping-cough, but he had a consumptive cough, and he had the measles—I think he caught cold, for the girl brought him out the day after he had the measles out on him, and I think they struck in—as far as I saw, the prisoner brought them bread and meat, or whatever he could get, and gave them the same as himself—I never saw any neglect—he was particularly kind to the boy—I believe he was out of employ a fortnight or three weeks before the boy's death—I know he had nothing to do in the Christmas week, for my husband made him come and dine with us, and the child also—I advised him to get an order for the parish doctor, who would give him a note to go to the Board for relief—I cannot recollect exactly what he said at that moment, but he said at times that all the doctors in the world could not save him, for he had had a complaint from his infancy—the child had had the small-pox about four months before—I am not at all aware of any intentional neglect.
EDWARD ILIFF . I am a beadle of Trinity Church, Newington. In consequence of information, I went to Counter-alley on Sunday, the 30th
of December, the day after the boy's death—I found every thing in as bad a state as possible—I went immediately on the commencement of the morning service—I found the prisoner there, lying in one corner of the room, on some wood shavings—there was no furniture whatever in the room—there was some fire—the prisoner appeared in a state of stupor—I could make nothing of him at first—I found the child dead, lying on a log of wood, in an opposite corner of the room, covered over with a sort of rug—the girl Was sitting by the fire—there was nothing whatever in the house in the shape of food—I asked the girl, "Have you had any thing to eat to-day?"—she said at first, "No"—whether she understood me I do not know—I said, "You are sure you have had nothing to eat to-day?" and the prisoner answered, "No"—the girl said, "I have had a little bread"—I asked her who supplied what she had—she said, "Mr. Boulter, it was part of what was left the night before"—I asked the prisoner if he was hungry—he said, "Yes"—I fetched them some bread and bacon—they did not eat it in the way I have seen poor people eat sometimes—they cooked it first—when I found he had filled his belly, I said, "I am a constable, and it is necessary you should give me some information respecting the death of this child, or I must take you into custody—there is a report that this child has been very much neglected"—(for, while they were preparing their refreshment, I had been out to make inquiry in the neighbourhood)—he said, "We have been short"—I asked what was the cause of it—he said he had not employment, and no means of getting it for them—I said, "Have you made any application for parochial assistance in your extremities, or medical advice for the child?"—he said no, he had not, in that instance, and the reason he gave was, because he had before that time been passed on to his parish, which was Nessing, in Essex, which he had left, thinking he could do better by coming to town; and I think the girl said, they left because they had not victuals enough there—I do not think the prisoner made that observation—I asked him if he had neglected the children in consequence of cohabiting with some woman—he said, the children and himself had certainly been very short, but he could certainly acquit himself of any thing like neglect—he said nothing about the woman—he made the same observation afterwards, on the way to the station-house.
WILLIAM BARNARD BODY . I am a surgeon. I did not see the child before death—I saw it on Sunday, the 30th of December, but did not open the body till the following day—it appeared very much emaciated—I could see nothing to account for death—there was no appearance of external marks—on opening the body I found a disease of the lungs, adhesion of the pleura, and a large abscess, which had broken—it died from consumption, which must have been going on for some time—supposing it to have had small-pox, then measles and hooping-cough, that might produce consumption, but not necessarily so—all the viscera of the stomach was in a state of health—the stomach was quite empty, but it appeared that during the night the child had a very severe diarrhcea—I have no means of judging whether disease was produced by want of food—even supposing there had been neglect, it was impossible to ascertain it decidedly in that state—I cannot say the child died from neglect of being supplied with food—whether the emaciation was the result of consumption or want of food I cannot state—there is hectic fever with consumption—that would produce very great emaciation—there is no distinction between emaciation produced by disease and shortness of food.
MR. BOULTER re-examined. I always found the prisoner ready to come to work—I never knew him harsh or severe to his children.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18390204-852]
852. WILLIAM LAMBERT, JOHN DONOVAN , and JOHN FOSTER , were indicted for burglariously breaking and entering the dwelling-house of John Pillow, at St. John, Southwark, about the hour of one in the night of the 24th of January, with intent to steal, and stealing therein, 1 veil, value 10 s.; 1 spoon, value 5 s.; 2 buttons, value 1 s.; 1 pair of braces, value 3 d.;1 cloak, value 4 l.;3 coats, value 5 l. 10 s.; I tooth-pick, value 1 s. 6 d.;1 knife, value 3 d.;1 rupee, value 2 s.; 1 half-franc, value 5 d.;5 crowns, 4 half-crowns, and 10 shillings; his property; and that Lambert had been before convicted of felony.
MR. PAYNE conducted the Prosecution. JOHN PILLOW. I am a printer, and live in Horsleydown-lane. On Thursday night, the 24th of January, I went to bed about twelve o'clock—I got up about half-past six o'clock next morning, and went into the kitchen—I found the kitchen window wide open, and a pane of glass partly broken—I had not noticed the window the night before—I found my writing-desk on the kitchen table—it had been brought from the parlour—it was closed, but I found it was broken open—it had contained various papers and a bag of money—I found the papers scattered about the room—there were five crown-pieces, four or five new half-crowns, a few shillings and sixpences, and two or three fourpenny-pieces, in the bag—I had preserved them, being new, not meaning to circulate them, except the crown-pieces, which were not quite new—I found a tin box in the kitchen, that had been in a drawer in the parlour the night before—I did not see it the night before, and cannot say when I saw it last—the parlour is in the front of the house, and the kitchen quite at the back—there are five or six steps from the kitchen to the parlour—there had been a guinea, a halfguinea, and a few new shillings and sixpences, in the tin box—a small wooden box was left in the parlour, which had contained a little Indian rupee, some copper coin, a pair of shirt-buttons with "E P" engraved on them, a few cents, a half-franc, and a small plated copper coin—I have seen them since—I have missed a great deal of wearing-apparel—there was a blue cloth cloak with a lynx-fur collar, a great-coat, a suit of black, a claret dresscoat, and another olive green great-coat, which I had thrown off in thekitchen the night before—there was a silver tooth-pick in the pocket of the black
waistcoat—a veil was also missing, and a silver caddy-spoon, engraved "J A P," which were my first wife's initials, that had been in the teacaddy in the parlour—a penknife was also missing from the parlour—the lock of the parlour door was shot when I went to bed the night before, but the key was left in it—the kitchen window opens into the yard, which is enclosed by a wall seven or eight feet high—on the other side of the wall is a public lane—a person could get over—a hand could be got in where the window was broken, to open it—it was broken close to the fastening—I found half of the square of glass next morning laid carefully against the wash-house window—the window is not more than three or four feet from the ground—a person could get in without assistance—it is a paved yard.
PETER LOGAN ( police-sergeant N 12.) I was sent for to the prosecutor's on Friday morning, the 25th, about half-past seven o'clock—the house is in the parish of St. John, Horsleydown—I picked up this duplicate in the
back-yard—it was afterwards shown to the prisoner Donovan at the station-house, and he claimed it as his own—it has the name of John Donovan on it—I had sent Sergeant Woodhouse to take him on finding it.
JOHN BRIDOELAND ( police-constable N 123.) On Thursday night, or Friday morning, the 25th of January, at a quarter before one o'clock, I saw the prisoners Lambert and Donovan together in Tooley-street, about two hundred yards from the prosecutor's house—they went down Unicorn-square—Donovan had a blue cloak on, with a dark fur collar—they were going in a direction from the prosecutor's—I went round my beat, and about one o'clock I saw them both coming back in a direction towards the prosecutor's—Donovan then had on a black frock coat, and no cloak—they were going towards the prosecutor's the last time.
WILLIAM FETHERTON ( police-sergeant N 17.) On Friday morning, the 25th of January, at half-past four o'clock, I saw the three prisoners in the Crown public-house, High-street, near St. George's Church—it is a market house, and is always open about a quarter past four o'clock—they remained there till about twenty minutes before six o'clock—they then came out with two or three others, and went towards London-bridge—that was all I saw of them at that time.
Foster. The statement is perfectly correct—I spoke to him, and asked him to have a cup of coffee. Witness. He did speak to me outside the house, MARY HARRISON. I am sister to Mrs. Pillow. On the night of the robbery I fastened the kitchen window, between seven and eight o'clock—I went to bed at eleven o'clock—I saw every thing safe before I went to bed—I looked at the fastening at eleven o'clock, before I went to bed, and it was all secure—there was no pane of glass broken then—the doors were quite fast—Mr. Pillow fastened the back door—he went to bed before me—the desk was in the parlour—there was no desk on the kitchen table then.
JOHN WOODHOUSE ( police-constable N 13.) In consequence of information I received I went with another constable to Unicorn-square, on Friday morning, about a quarter past eight o'clock, and saw the three prisoners soners in company together in the square, near Lambert's sister's house—I laid hold of Lambert and Donovan, and told them what I wanted them for—they both said they would not go—they made great resistance, and Foster said to Lambert, "Bill, you had better go quietly if you have not done any thing"—he still continued to resist, and threw himself down on the ground, but I succeeded in taking him to the station-house—in going there I observed Lambert, who was then in Conlan's custody, take something from his coat pocket—I was behind him, with Donovan—I laid hold of Lambert's hand, and took this veil from it—I asked him where he got it—he said it was his sister's—I told Conlan to search him—he then became very violent again in the street, and both he and Donovan threw themselves down—I did not see Conlan search Lambert—I searched Donovan, and found a sovereign in his fob pocket, and a crown-piece and five half-crowns in his right-hand trowsers pocket, three of which were new—I had a great deal of trouble to get them from him—Foster walked with us till we began to search Donovan and Lambert, and then he ran away—he had on a close-bodied claret-coloured coat, and a great-coat over his arm, which I think he has on now—he afterwards came to the station-house, about half-past eight o'clock, dressed as he is now—I do not know what had become of the claret coat—he had not got it on—I commenced searching him, and then he took his money out, and gave me—it was one crownpiece, a sixpence, three fourpenny-pieces, and 6 d. In copper—there were no
shillings—I was at the station-house when a duplicate was shown to Donovan—he said it was his—I asked him what it was for—he said for a pair of boots, that they were his own boots, he had pledged them himself.
JURY. Q. Why not take Foster into custody at first? A. I had not sufficient strength—we had enough to do with the others.
EDWARD CONLAN ( police-constable M 131.) I was in company with Woodhouse on Friday morning, the 25th of January—I searched Lambert, and found on him a pair of braces, a silver caddy spoon, four pieces of foreign coin—a pair of silver shirt-sleeve buttons, two five shilling pieces, two half-crowns, one quite new, a sovereign, some shillings, sixpences, and fourpenny pieces, and 1 s. 51/2 d. In copper—while we were taking Lambert and Donovan along, Foster said he would go on quietly, but as soon as I commenced searching Lambert, he went away—I cannot say whether he walked or ran away—he had a close-bodied claret coloured coat on—he came to the station-house about a quarter of an hour afterwards with a different coat on—I think he had the same great coat on his arm that he has on now.
Foster. I have sent for the coat I had on, it was not a claret—I know I changed my dress, but not in the way they speak of—what colour is claret? Witness. A dark brownish colour—I am sure it was a dark claret coat he had on, and he had on a different dress when he came to the station-house.
SOPHIA PILLOW . I am the prosecutor's wife. I was called up by my husband between six and seven o'clock on Friday morning, the 25th of January—I went into the kitchen, and saw the window wide open, and a pane of glass broken—I know this lace veil to be mine—I have had it many years—I have a white one which my sister worked from it of the same pattern—I have often worn it—it is rather a rusty colour—I had teen it within a week of the robbery—I kept it in the parlour drawer—I have not the least doubt it is mine from the pattern and colour.
JOHN PILLOW re-examined. This caddy spoon is mine, it has the initials of my first wife on it, "J A P"—this knife belongs to Mary Harrison—these sleeve buttons are mine—they are engraved with my father's initials "E P," I have had them about two years—the braces I know are mine—I have not recovered any of the wearing apparel—there is property t o the amount of 2 l. Not recovered—here is a silver rupee which I know perfectly well—there is no mark on it, but I had one and missed it—here is a small French coin, a Napoleon, which I have had for years, and a George the Second halfpenny—they are all mine.
EDWARD CONLAN re-examined. The George the Second halfpenny, and the foreign coins were found on Lambert.
Lambert's Defence. I was going home about half past twelve o'clock at night, the policeman met me at the corner of a court by a public-house—he said, "Well, Bill, are you going home?"—I said, "Yes, and going to bed"—I went into the public-house first to have a pint of half-and-half, and when I came out, he said, "You might as well have given me half of it"—I took him in, and he had a glass of the best rum along with me—he then said, "You had better go home and go to bed"—I did so and he saw me in doors—I got up at half past six o'clock next morning, and going underneath the rail-road, towards the Borough, I saw a lot of things underneath the rail-road in different places, separate from each other—there was
a veil, a pair of braces, and a lot of coins—I picked them up, and put them in my pocket—I then came up as far as St. George's church in the Borough, and went into a public-house to have half a pint of coffee—I there saw Donovan—I sat down and got rather intoxicated—he wanted to see me home as I could not take myself home, and about nine o'clock, he took me into a cab, and I was taken by the policemen.
Donovan's Defence. I was coming home from my aunt's at Deptford at past twelve o'clock—I met Lambert, and said, I was very dry—he went into the public-house with me to have some half-and-half—the policeman was inside, and he said we might have given him some of it—we treated him with a glass of rum, and went home—when I got up in the morning, I was going for an errand to Whitechapel for my father, and met Lambert and Foster in a public-house—he treated me with some coffee there—we all three of us stopped there, got rather intoxicated, and I took a cab to see Lambert home, and two constables came and took us into custody.
Foster's Defence. On Thursday evening, about ten o'clock, I went into the King's Arms public-house in Kent-street, kept by Mr. Clark—I remained there till half-past three o'clock in the morning—I then came out, and went to the public-house where sergeant Featherton says he saw me at four o'clock—the doors were not open till a few minutes after four o'clock—there were some scavengers and several working men waiting there to get something to drink—I went in and staid there till half-past five o'clock, when Lambert and Donovan came in, and asked me to have something to drink—we had something, and soon after Lambert asked me if I would get him a cab to see him home—I got him one, and we rode as far as Unicorn-square, where Lambert's sister lives—he went in there, and came out again while Donovan and I were waiting outside—he said, "I shall not stop at home"—I told him he had better, as he was very fresh, and he would only get drinking, and if he had a shilling or two to take care of them—nothing would persuade him, and while we were talking, the policeman came up, and said they were his prisoners—they did not offer to take me, but took the other two—I walked four or five hundred yards along with them, and while they were searching Lambert and Donovan, I went into a public-house and had a glass of gin—I then went home to my landlady—I told her that Lambert and Jones were taken up while I was talking to them at their door, and I thought they would come and take me, so I would save them the trouble, and go and give myself up—I had a cup of coffee, and pulled off my coat, for I had been to the station-house several times before, and they knew I was rather a roughish customer—I went and gave myself up—I have written several times to the landlord of the public-house in which I was until half-past three o'clock, but he will not come, because he is afraid of being fined for having his house open—if you will give me time to subpoena him, I could make him come and speak the truth—I do not mean to say I was not in the prisoner's company, but I am as innocent of the charge as a child unborn—I wish to ask Woodhouse whether he did not go and inquire where I was at the time the robbery was committed—he promised me he would.
JOHN WOODHOUSE re-examined. I did go, and the publican where he says he had been until three o'clock, told me he left the house at twelve o'clock, and did not return at all.
ROBERT LIVERSEGE . I am a policeman. I produce a certificate of Lambert's former conviction, which I got from the office of Mr. Lawson, Clerk of the Peace for Surrey—( read)—I was present at his trial—Lambert is the man who was then convicted in the name of Thomas Smith. LAMBERT — GUILTY . Aged 23.— Transported for Life. DONOVAN — GUILTY . Aged 18.— FOSTER — GUILTY . Aged 22.—
Transported for Fifteen Years.
[trialAccount t18390204-853]
853. JOSEPH HUDSON was indicted for unlawfully, maliciously, and feloniously assaulting Thomas Winkley, on the 10th of January, and cutting and wounding him upon his head, with intent to maim and disable him.—2nd COUNT, stating his intent to be to do him some grievous bodily harm.
MR. CLARKSON conducted the Prosecution.
THOMAS WINKLEY . I am a horse slaughterer, and live in Green-street, Borough. The prisoner has been in my service—I had occasion to discharge him, and afterwards preferred a charge of breach of the peace against him for threatening my life, but his wife interceded for him, and I did not prosecute—that is about three months ago—he lived in one of my little houses—I had taken proceedings to get possession of it, but had offered him no offence whatever up to the 10th of January—about a quarter to five o'clock on that evening, I was coming from my house to the opposite premises, and saw the prisoner coming up the street—he hallooed out, "Hoy, stop, I want you"—I was opening my gate, and he came up and knocked my hat off with a stick, and said, "How do you like that, where is Mr. Pople?"—(the prisoner had been to my house in the early part of the day and ill-used me and Mr. Pople, who was in the house at the time, had rescued me from him)—I was in the act of stooping to pick my hat up, and he struck me across the head with this stick ( produced) several times, as hard as he could—it broke on my head, and while doing so he said, "I have got a little sharper instrument that that for you"—I think it has been a prop—Brady, one of my men, came to my assistance, and led me out of the yard—the blows cut my head—I was almost drowned in blood—Mr. Newth attended me and is doing so still—I am forty-nine years old.
JOHN BRADY . I was at work in the prosecutor's yard on Thursday the 10th of January—I saw the prisoner come—he brandished this stick over his head, and requested Mr. Winkley to fetch Mr. Pople to him, that he was then prepared with a stick—he wished to fight the prosecutor, and said he was prepared with a sharper instrument if necessary—he knocked the prosecutor's hat off and said, "D—your eyes, what will you do now?"—while the prosecutor was in the act of picking up his hat, the prisoner struck him on the head with the stick, with all his might, three times—I do not know whether the blows all fell on his head, but some did, for I saw the blood flow in torrents from his head—I assisted him across the yard.
GEORGE ELISHA NEWTH . I am a surgeon, and live in Great Suffolk-street. I was sent for to the prosecutor, on Thursday evening, at five o'clock—I examined his head and found an extensive contused wound, about three inches long, bleeding very profusely on the left-side of the scalp near the temple—it appeared to be produced by a round blunt instrument, like that produced, it would produce just such a wound—he lost a considerable quantity of blood—in my judgment I should consider it a very serious
wound—he has hardly recovered the effects of it now—he has been under my care ever since—the skull was laid bare, it was a ragged braised wound. WILLIAM TAYLOR ( police-constable M 14.) I was called to take the prisoner into custody—he was running up the street at the time, and went into a public-house—I followed him in, and as soon as he saw me he took up this stick to make a blow at me—I immediately took him by the collar and took the stick from him—I took him to the station-house, and found this clasp-knife on him.
Prisoner's Defence. I was going down Green-street that day, and saw Mr. Pople and Mr. Winkley standing together—I was rather in liquorone of them, I do not know which, said, "Here comes this vagabond"—I asked them what made them call me a vagabond—the prosecutor said, "You scoundrel, get out of my sight"—I wanted to know the reason of his calling me that name, and he said to Mr. Pople, "Knock him on the head with your stick"—Mr. Pople hit me a blow on my head, and I staggered and fell, being drunk—I went away about ten yards and got a stick—I then returned and said, "Now I am ready"—I made a strike at Mr. Pople, and accidentally hit Mr. Winkley, but not intentionally—I made sure to hit Mr. Pople, and had no intention at all to strike Mr. Winkley.
THOMAS WINKLEY re-examined. What he has stated is false—he came to me while I was in-doors—I went out to him, and he said, "I want to know about this 4 l., and counting-house crack "—I had had my counting-house broken open about a week before, but bad never charged him with it—I said, "I do not want any thing to say to you"—he caught hold of my neck-cloth, and dragged me out into the street—Mr. Pople come out and said, "Do you want to murder the man?"—the prisoner got up in a fighting attitude to Pople, and made a hit at him—Pople warded it off and struck him, but did not knock him down—he came up again to Pople in a fighting attitude, and Pople struck him on the side of the head with a stick—it was twenty minutes after that that he came with this stick and did what I have described—we had had our tea in the meantime—his house is about fifty yards from mine—Mr. Pople was not present when my head was cut—the prisoner could not have hit me by mistake.
RICHARD POPLE . I was at the prosecutor's house on the day in question—Mr. Winkley called me out, and I found the prisoner had got the prosecutor on his back on the pavement, and had his hand at his throat—I rescued him—the prisoner then got up and began to fight with me, and I struck him—he then went away and I also—I was not present when the prosecutor was struck afterwards—he came to me with his head covered with blood.
GUILTY . Aged 25.— Transported for Fifteen Years.
[trialAccount t18390204-854]
854. JOHN FOLEY and DANIEL HOLLAND were indicted for stealing, on the 1st of January, 1 leaden pump, value 2 l., the goods of the London and Southampton Railway Company, the masters of the said John Foley.
MICHAEL JONES . I work for Mr. Francis, of Nine Elms, Battersea, and occupy a house in his garden, within a few yards of the London and Southampton railway. On the 1st of January I saw, in a field belonging to the Company, and adjoining the railroad, a pump, two hods, a sack, an axe, and a piece of pipe—I returned and told the foreman, then went back, and saw the two prisoners, one with a quantity of lead in the hod, and the
other putting some into a sack—the pump was then cut up into small pieces—I had seen it whole twenty minutes before—I saw Foley put the lead into the sack—he was endeavouring to put the remainder into his hod, when I asked what he had got—he said he had nothing—I saw a chopper lying between the prisoners—I took it up, and told them, as I knew what they had got, the first man that moved I would cut his legs off—they wished to go up the meadow, but I ordered them to put the lead down, and gave them both into custody—Foley ran away when we got into the main road, but I pursued and took him—Holland walked gently before me—I took them both into the yard, and delivered them to Pye.
CHARLES PTE . I am a constable of the rail-road. Foley works on the railway—they were both given into my custody—I went to the place, and found the pump cut in pieces—I knew it to be the Company's pump—I had seen it within a week—Holland works for the contractor.
JAMES KENNERLY . I am a constable of the Southampton railway. The prisoners were brought into the station-house—I found a hammer at Holland's lodging, in Vauxhall-road—Foley gave his address in South Lambeth-buildings—I could find no such place, but I found he lived in Alpha-court—I there found an axe and hammer—when I came back I asked him why he gave me a wrong address—he said he did not know the name of the place—I asked if it was in Alpha-court—he said he believed it was.
JAMES WALKER . I work on the Southampton railway, and am a carpenter. This is my chopper. I left it safe in the basket on the works, on the 1st of January, in a building which I was roofing.
WILLIAM HAMMERSTON . I am a carpenter on the Southampton railway. This is my axe—the last time I saw it was on the 30th of October—I had met with an accident, and did not go to work after that for five weeks.
CHARLES CONSTABLE . I am a carpenter on the railway. This hammer is mine—I left it on the works—I was away for a month.
GEORGE WILLIAM EDGECOMB . My father is a carpenter on the railway. This small hammer is mine—I left it in my father's counting-house.
(The prisoner Foley put in a written defence, stating that he had found the things in the field.)
Holland's Defence. As I was coming home, at four o'clock, I picked up the hammer on the road—I did not know who it belonged to—I thought it not worth advertising, and left it at my house—I know nothing at all about the pump.
(The prisoners received good characters.)
FOLEY — GUILTY . Aged 40.
HOLLAND — GUILTY . Aged 39.
Confined Six Months.
Before Mr. Recorder.
[trialAccount t18390204-855]
855. HENRY GEORGE GIBBONS was indicted for stealing, on the 6th of January, 1 watch, value 1 l., the goods of Jonathan White.
ELIZA WHITE . I am laundry-maid at Foster-house, Egham, which is a lunatic asylum. On the 8th of December the prisoner visited Ann Collins, a fellow-servant of mine there—he also spent New Year's-day there, and slept there—he came again on the 6th of January, and went out with Collins, to whom he appeared to be paying his addresses—I had a trunk in the laundry, containing wearing apparel, and some silver—about six o'clock I missed a
watch belonging to my brother Jonathan White from the laundry, and found the things in my box tumbled—I missed 28 s. In silver—I missed the watch about six o'clock—I should know it again—there was a small jeweller's gold ring fastened to the handle—the number of the watch is 6608, and the maker's name is Lee.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. When had you seen it last? A. Between twelve and one o'clock on the 6th of January—I saw the prisoner there between two and three o'clock that afternoon—he remained there about an hour and a half—there was no one else there but the domestics.
HENRY BRITTON . I am servant to Mr. Watts, a pawnbroker, in Angelterrace, King-street, Hammersmith—this watch was pawned by Mary Langston, who is not here, in the name of "Ann Gibson."
ANN COLLINS . I am a servant at the Asylum, I know the prisoner—on the 6th of January I saw him there in the kitchen—I left him alone in the laundry for about an hour—I never saw the watch in his hands.
Cross-examined. Q. Did not he keep company with you when he came there? A. Yes; he went into the laundry, out of the rain—the box was in the laundry—there was no one else there that I know of.
RICHARD GOLDING . I am a policeman. I received information on the 28th of January, and a bag was given to me, at the George-inn, Hammersmith, by Mr. Battersbee, who is not here—on examining it I found the duplicate of a watch in it—Langston is in prison for illegally pawning.
HENRY BRITTON re-examined. That is the counterpart of the duplicate—I saw the prisoner at my master's premises a few days after, but not on this occasion.
RICHARD BROWN . I know the prisoner had that bag when he lodged with me at No. 2, Little College-street, Westminster—I never saw the bag at Egham.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18390204-856]
856. HENRY GEORGE GIBBONS was again indicted for stealing, on the 16th of January, 1 cloak, value 8 s., the goods of Vincent Prosper.
ROBERT WEARE . I am keeper at Dr. George Burrows's establishment at Clapham. About four o'clock on the evening of the 15th of January, I saw two cloaks safe at the house—one belonged to Mr. Vincent Prosper—I did not notice it again that day—I missed it about seven o'clock next morning—this is it—( looking at it)—the prisoner had lived in the establishment as a keeper, but had left on the 8th of November—he had come on the 22nd of October—the police were sent for, and examined some footmarks—they were those of a person who wore straps—I gave directions to the policeman, and the prisoner was taken.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. Who is Mr. Vincent Prosper? A. A patient there—the cloak was behind the door in the hall when I last saw it—I saw it on the 15th, as the patient had it on that day—I did not see it hung up—I saw it after the patient had it, about four o'clock in the afternoon—the prisoner's boots and straps were compared with the footmarks, two days after, when he was taken, and they exactly corresponded—there had been a board placed over the marks—I found that still there—the boot was first placed on one side, and then in the mark—there was no other mark made.
RICHARD GOLDING . I am an officer. I was sent for, and received a communication from Were—I went into the garden, and observed footmarks near the house, which appeared to have been made by a person wearing straps to their trowsers—I made inquiry, and from information,
suspected the prisoner—on the 18th I saw him in custody at Queen-square office, with this cloak on—I took it from him, and these boots and straps were taken from him—I went with my brother constable, and examined them with the marks, and from the circumstance of their being overtrod here, I put it in, but not to make any pressure—they exactly corresponded—I put it to the side first—I did not make another impression.
Cross-examined. Q. Did he account for where he got the cloak? A. Not at that time, he said, at the next examination, that he had bought it.
ROBERT WEARE re-examined. I have examined the cloak carefully—it is Vincent Prosper's, I have not the least doubt of it.
MR. PAYNE. Q. What do you know it by? A. have seen it for five or six months—I have been in the habit of putting it on the gentleman myself—I brought the gentleman from on board a ship, and here are marks I know it by, at the time I brought him from the vessel—there is a button which is broken at the bottom, and it is stained, as the gentleman was rather dirty, and is in the habit of blowing his nose on it, and wetting it, and there are marks of it—I have not the least doubt it is his.
GUILTY . Aged 32.— Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18390204-857]
857. ELIZABETH SMITH was indicted for stealing, on the 6th of February, 175 yards of flannel, value 14 l. 10 s., the goods of Martin Atkinson.
ALFRED BRADLAND . I am shopman to Martin Atkinson, of Bridge-road, Lambeth. On the night of the 6th of February, rather before seven o'clock, I missed this flannel from about four feet within the door—I saw the prisoner with it, and took her about three yards from the door—she said it was not he that took it—it was partly covered with her cloak.
WILLIAM DAVEY . I am a policeman. I took the prisoner in charge—she said a young man, in company with two females, gave it to her to carry, and promised to pay her.
Prisoner. I had no thought it was stolen—two females gave it to me, who I had seen about the street before—I had not got it under my cloak. Witness. She had carried the flannel a very little way, it was very heavy.
MR. BRADLAND re-examined. She had both arms round it, and it was partly covered with her cloak—I saw several persons about—she said she would point out the person who gave it her, but finding it on her, I kept her.
WILLIAM DAVEY re-examined. I have reason to suspect the other persons, they are thieves—I got their names from the prisoner—I have made inquiries about her, and have heard nothing against her before—she gets her living by selling dolls about in a basket.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18390204-858]
858. ELLEN SAGGER was indicted for stealing, on the 14th of January, 1 purse, value 2 d.;1 sovereign, 1 shilling, and 1 £5 Bank note, the goods and monies of Frances Maria Kitchener, in the dwelling-house of Charles Ernest Rack witz.
MR. PAYNE conducted the Prosecution,
FRANCES MARIA KITCHENER . I am a widow of an officer in the navy. I have a pension of 40 l. a-year, and live in Westminster Bridge-road, Lambeth,
in the house of Charles Ernest Rackwitz, a baker. I have known the prisoner eighteen or twenty years—we were in the habit of visiting each other—her husband holds a respectable situation in Doctor's-commons—on Monday, the 14th of January, I changed a cheque at Barclay's bank—my landlady was with me, and she took the money, all but 5 s., which she gave me—the money was a £10 note, three £5 notes, and a sovereign—after I got home, I paid my landlady the £10 note, and two of the £5 notes—I put the sovereign into the other £5 note, and put them together into my purse, which I put into my pocket—I afterwards put it into my table-drawer in my front room—the drawer was not locked—that was between four and five o'clock in the afternoon—the prisoner was in my room at the time—she came about four o'clock, and drank tea with me—I gave her 2 s. To fetch a loaf from below, and we had a little gin and water together about seven o'clock I said to her, "I am very tired, and if you will excuse me, I will go to bed"—I was fatigued with the walk in the morning—she said, "I will go up stairs with you"—she did so, and assisted me to bed she wished me good night, stooped over the bed and kissed me, and went away—I heard the private door slam—I did not go to sleep, but about ten minutes after, I got out of bed, put on my shoes and cloak, and went down stairs to get my purse out of the table-drawer, but it was gone—I am quite sure I had put it into the drawer.
Cross-examined by MR. CLABKSON. Q. Had you and the prisoner met together a good deal lately? A. No; she came whenever she found it convenient—we were not in the habit of writing to each other—I never intrusted her with my money to take care of—we were intimate—I lodged with her mother three years—I took nothing after tea on this night—we had sixpenny-worth of gin and water between us before tea, just after the prisoner came, which was four o'clock—it was two glasses, I believe, it was not nearly half a pint—I frequently have a little gin and water after dinner, not always—I had taken a little porter at my dinner—I took nothing that did me any harm, I was perfectly myself—I do not usually go to bed so early as seven o'clock, except from fatigue or illness—I had no punch-bowl in the house that night—I did not ask the prisoner to go up stairs with me, she offered to do so—I did not put the £5 note into any punch-bowl, or any basin—I put it into my purse, and in the table-drawer—I did not put it into the slop-basin at tea—the prisoner went away about half-past seven o'clock, and I got out of bed about ten minutes after—I went down, as I fancied I had mislaid my money, and wished to be sure—I searched in the closet, under the sofa, and in the boxes, and every where I could think of, I was full an hour searching—I then went to bed again—I did not call up my landlady, but she came up to me.
WILLIAM VICKRESS . I keep the White Hart, in the Walworth-road, about half or three quarters of a mile from the prosecutrix. On the 14th of January, between seven and ten o'clock in the evening, I gave the prisoner change for this note—I knew her before, and have not a doubt of her being the person—I knew her name, and put it on it myself—I have written "Sagger, Manor-place, 14 January, 39"—I afterwards paid the note to the Excise.
Cross-examined. Q. How long have you known her? A. From twelve to eighteen months, she is the wife of a very respectable man—I was standing at the desk when she came in, and asked for change—I am not aware that
I changed any other £5 note that day, but I cannot be certain, I only surmise the time, I fancy it was after tea—my attention was not called to this for nine days—I had forgotten the transaction then, except by the parties referring to some money I had paid to the Excise—I had paid sixteen guineas to the Excise—I think it was three £5 notes—I paid it, I think, the Wednesday after I took the note—I could not have written the name and address on either of the other notes, for I wrote it when the prisoner changed it—I did not notice whether I wrote names on the other notes—I have written Mr., not Mrs. Sagger, on this note—I knew her to be a married woman—it was written in her presence—I said,."Your name is Mrs. Sagger, I believe," and she said, "Yes"—I was certain of her person, but not exactly of her name at the time—I had sent goods to her house, and knew her as a customer.
WILLIAM SILK . I am clerk to Barclay and Co. I have not my books here—I have brought the numbers of the notes which I paid the progecutrix—I made the entry in the book.
MARIA CATHERINE RACKWITZ . I went with the prosecutrix to the Bank, on Monday, the 14th of January. She received a £10 note, three £5 notes, a sovereign, and five shillings there—I took charge of the money—when I got home, I gave it all up to her, except five shillings, which she had at first—she paid me £10 and two £5 notes—she took the sovereign, and rolled it up in the other £5 note—I have the numbers of the notes, which I got from the banker's.
NOT GUILTY .

860. SUSANNAH WELLER, alias Sarah Miller, was indicted for stealing, on the 31st of December, at St. Giles, Camber well, 1 pair of ear-rings, value 7 l.;1 necklace, value 5 s.; 1 purse, value 3 d.;1 collar, value 2 s.; 1 flounce, value 2 s.; 1 handkerchief, value 1 s.; 1 waistband, value 1 s.; 1 shop, value 1 s.; and 1 pocket-book, value 6 d.; the goods of William Small, her master, in his dwelling-house.
JOHN BOURN . I am foreman to Messrs. Mullens, pawnbrokers, of Staverton-row, Newington. On the 31st of December the prisoner came and offered me a pair of diamond ear-rings to pawn, and a gilt snap—I saw what the ear-rings were, and asked what she wanted for them—she said 4 s.—I asked how she came by them—she said she bought them in Wiltonler, shire, and gave 3 s. for them—I asked what her name was—she said Wel and she lived in Cold-harbour-lane—I felt satisfied they were not hers, and gave, her into custody—the ear-rings are worth 7 l.
CELIA ROUBEL SMALL . I am the wife of William Small. We live in Hill-terrace, Grove-lane, in the parish of St. Giles, Camberwell—the prisoner was my nursery-maid for about seven weeks. These are my ear-rings—I missed them on the 27th of December, four days before she offered them to pawn, but I did not mention it to her, as I had no suspicion of her—they
were taken from a box in a glass-drawer in my bed-room—these other things are mine—this purse was taken from ray work-box, and this collar from one of the children's drawers; this flounce from a closet, and the other things from different drawers—they were all found on the prisoner.
THOMAS HOOKWAY ( police-constable P 13.) I was sent for, and took the prisoner—I found these things on her, which the prosecutrix has identified.
Prisoner. I did not take the ear-rings from my mistress's drawer.
GUILTY . Aged 17— Recommended to mercy by the Prosecutrix. Transported for Ten Years.

867. JOHN WAGSTAFF was indicted for stealing, on the 24th of January, 2 bottles, value 4 d., and I quart of wine, value 4 s., the goods of William Sandom —2nd COUNT, stating it to belong to Frank Thomas.
JOHN BEZANT . Mr. William Sandom is a captain in the navy—I am servant to Frank Thomas, a lighterman—this wine was in my master's warehouse. On the 24th of January the prisoner came there rather drunk—he came to take the rope in, and he said to me, "Will you take this rope in, as I am afraid I shall fall?"—he then said, "I am going up stairs"—I said, "What for?"—he said, "I must go up"—he went, and I went after him to No. 3—I there heard him hustling among the wine—I went down to the foreman, and when I went up again he had got to No. 5, near to a bag of hops—he came out, and I saw a bottle of wine in his pocket—the foreman asked him what he had got there—he said he had picked it up from behind the bag of hops—he gave it to the foreman.
SAMUEL WILMOTT . I am foreman to Mr. Thomas. I went up stairs with Bezant—the prisoner had a bottle of wine in his pocket—he was drunk—he was in the service of Wood, Field, and Wood, hop-merchants—we warehouse hops for them, and he had come that day with some hops—I found another bottle of wine behind the bags of hops, one he gave me out of his pocket, and two more were found at his lodging—I know these bottles by the marks on the corks—my master had the care of them.
JOHN GASKIN . I am an officer. I was called to take the prisoner—I went to his house, and found some wine there.
FRANK THOMAS . I am a lighterman. I was sent for, and gave the prisoner in charge—this is the wine, it was left in my care.
Prisoner's Defence. I saw the bottle standing by the bag of hops—I took it up, but did not think about thieving. GUILTY . Aged 45.— Recommended to mercy. — Confined Six Weeks.

868. HENRY HINCKLEY was indicted for stealing, on the 28th of December, 2 half-crowns, the monies of George Webb, his master.
MR. CLARKSON conducted the Prosecution.
GEORGE WEBB . I am a cheesemonger, and live at Lambeth-walk. The prisoner was in my employ for about eight months—on the 28th of December I marked three half-crowns—(I have one till for gold, and three for silver and copper)—I put the three half-crowns into a till on the cheese and bacon side, about half-past twelve o'clock—the prisoner had access to the whole
of my tills—I went to it again about three o'clock in the afternoon, and the three half-crowns were gone—on the 7th of January I marked seven sovereigns and four half-sovereigns—I gave them to George Seymour, to put through the slide into the gold till, which is generally kept locked, hut on that occasion I left it unlocked on purpose—it is under the butter counter, and the shopman put the gold into it, through the slide, about twelve o'clock—I had previously seen Mr. Wheeler, a coal-merchant, and gave him instructions, and at half-past one o'clock I saw Mr. Wheeler's servant come to the shop—I had given Seymour orders to go to dinner at that time, and I left the shop, leaving the prisoner in it—I said to him, "Mind the shop; I am going into the Borough"—I went out, and returned in about half an hour—the prisoner was just then going down to dinner, and the others were just coming up from dinner—I went to a silver till, and missed about 1 l. worth of silver, which I had left in it—I then went to the gold till, and found the same number of sovereigns I had left in it, with no addition—I sent for a policeman, and went down, and said to, the prisoner, "You have robbed me of a sovereign"—he said, "I have not"—I said, "You have taken one, and it is not in the till"—I then said, "Policeman, you must take him; I am sure he has got it"—the prisoner said he had not—I sent for the gold till, and there were only seven sovereigns and four half-sovereigns in it—I sent for Mr. Wheeler and the servant—I said, "Mr. Wheeler, I believe you sent a sovereign over for some bacon?"—he said, "I did, by my servant"—the servant said, "I paid it to this young man"—the policeman then searched the prisoner, but found no sovereign—I then said, "Give up the sovereign, you vagabond, and go about your business"—he persisted that he had not got it—I said, "I am satisfied you have got it, I do not care whether you produce it or not"—after some time he said, "You say if I give you up the sovereign I am to go about my business"—I made no answer, but said to the policeman, "Take him away and lock him up"—he took him up stairs—he went behind the counter as I thought for his hat, but he threw down the sovereign and said, "There is your sovereign"—I said, "This is not the first sovereign you have robbed me of, I am satisfied you have robbed me to a great extent"—he then pulled out five shillings, and declared that was all the money he had got—I said, "Previously to your being locked up I shall see your box"—he went up stairs and then said he had not got the key of his box—I told the policeman to break it open, but I believe the prisoner opened it with a key—the policeman found ten sovereigns in the breast pocket of a coat in the hot—the prisoner said, "That is mine"—I said, "Take possession of it, policeman"—I then said to the prisoner, "Have you any more?"—he said, "No"—the policeman searched further and found thirteen packages of silver, and amongst it I found two of the half-crowns which I had marked on the 28th of December, and one that I had marked previously—on the way to the station-house I said to the prisoner, "Don't you think you are a pretty fellow to rob me?"—he said, "I am not the only one that is robbing you"—I have six other persons in my employ.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Are you certain you are correct in that phrase? A. Yes.
WILLIAM WHEELER . I am a coal-merchant, and live in Lambeth-walk. I marked a sovereign and called my servant out of the kitchen and told her to take particular notice of it—my sister told her to go and purchase something
—she went out and brought back lbs. 10 d., and a piece of bacon—I was sent for to Mr. Webb's and saw the same sovereign—the prisoner picked it up from under the counter, and threw it on the counter.
ROBERT CREASY ( police-constable L 172.) I went to Mr. Webb's on the 7th of January, and saw the prisoner sitting at dinner down stairs. Mr. Webb charged him with stealing a sovereign—he said he had not—in consequence of the conversation which took place, I went up to the shop—I then saw the prisoner feeling under the counter, and he produced a sovereign—I went to his box, and found ten sovereigns and thirteen packages of silver—Mr. Webb said he had marked some half-crowns and missed them, and if they were there he could identify them—he picked out these marked ones.
MR. WEBB. These are two of those I marked on the 28th, and this is one I marked before—I can swear to them.
(Michael Delaney, a tailor, gave the prisoner a good character.)
GUILTY . Aged 22.— Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18390204-869]
869. ELIZABETH WEST was indicted for stealing, on 17th of January, 2 handkerchiefs, value 3 s., the goods of Henry Wharton and John Wharton; and that she had been before convicted of felony.
GEORGE TRAVELL . I am shopman to Henry and John Wharton, pawnbrokers, in Bermondsey-street. On the 17th of January the prisoner pledged a gown for half-a-crown—as she was going out my attention was drawn to her—Bennett fetched her back, and two handkerchiefs dropped from her—the officer was passing at the time—the prisoner was very violent, and in dragging her into the shop a gown-piece fell from her—I delivered the handkerchiefs to the officer—these are them—they are my masters'—they had been hanging two yards and a half inside the shop when the prisoner came in.
Cross-examined by MR. DOANE. Q. When had you seen them? A. About five minutes before—there is no mark on them.
ROBERT BENNETT . I am shopman to Messrs. Wharton. I saw the prisoner in their shop on the 17th, and followed her out—I brought her back, and on the way she droped this black silk handkerchief—the boy picked it up, and brought it to her—she said it was not hers—this other handkerchief I took from under her cloak—she struck me on the head—I dropped the handkerchiefs in the scuffle, and they were taken up by another person.
JOHN KELLY ( police-constable M 85.) I took charge of the prisoner and the articles.
HENRY TRITON ( police-constable M 175.) I know the prisoner, and was present at Newington, in 1836, when she was tried—I have the certificate of her conviction from the Clerk of the Peace's office—( read.) GUILTY .* Aged 21.— Transported for Seven Years.
Before Mr. Sergeant Arabin.
