870. JAMES BAILEY was indicted for bigamy.
MR. BALLANTINE conducted the Prosecution.
MARY SHREEVE . I am a widow, and live in Seymour-place, Marylebone. I am the mother of Elizabeth Bailey—I remember her being married to the prisoner at the parish church of St. Mary Magdalen, Bermondsey, on the 5th of January, 1816—I was present, and witnessed it—I saw my daughter yesterday.
Cross-examined by MR. DOANE. Did they continue to live together? A. Yes, but not in my house—I heard of his second wife going to live with them, but I never saw her there—I know nothing of the second marriage.
DEBORAH BRADFIELD . I was married to the prisoner on the 12th of June last, at the parish church of St. Mary, Newington.
Cross-examined. Q. You were married by banns? A. Yes—I had been living at the prisoner's two months before—his wife lived in the same house—I knew he had a wife—I was in the family-way by him, and it was proposed that we should marry, that the child should be legitimate, and that I might get a situation as wet nurse if I could show my marriageregister—his wife knew of it—I had no wish to prosecute him.
GUILTY . Aged 46.— Confined Three Months.
[trialAccount t18390204-871]
871. JOHN TERRY was indicted for stealing, on the 1st of January, 3 spoons, value 4 l., the goods of James Paul, his master.
JAMES PAUL . I live in Bermondsey, and am a surgeon—the prisoner was my errand-boy for about five weeks. On the 1st of January, I had a party, and the next morning I missed three spoons—I had the prisoner apprehended on the 9th, and told him if he would tell me where they were I would let him go home, as I wanted to make my set complete—I have never found them, but I have traced where they went to, and he confessed to having stolen them.
NOT GUILTY .
Before Mr. Justice Williams.
[trialAccount t18390204-872]
872. EDWARD PEARCE was indicted for feloniously assaulting Richard Hodgson, on the 21st of January, and stabbing and wounding him on his left arm with intent to maim and disable him.—2nd COUNT, stating his intent to be, to do him some grievous bodily harm.
RICHARD HODGSON . I am a broker's assistant. On the 21st of January I was employed by Mr. Turner, of Friar-street, Blackfriars-road, to make a distress on the prisoner's premises, No. 2, Hebden-court, Pearl-row. Blackfriars-road—I went there about half-past two o'clock in the afternoon—I met Prettyman the landlord at the door—the prisoner was only a tenant—I sent the landlord up first, and then went in myself—I went up to the prisoner's room, and found him sitting on a stool at work, conversing with the landlord—he is a shoemaker—the landlord was saying to him, "I wish you to settle this little thing without putting me to the trouble of putting a distress on your goods—I wish you to do it"—the prisoner said he had no money to settle it—I then said to the prisoner that a duty would devolve on me which I was very reluctant to perform on a man in his situation, but without he and the landlord could settle it, I was there with a warrant to distrain his goods—I held the warrant in my hand, and showed it to him—on my doing so, he said, "You are not going to rob my poor children of my poor bits of things?"—I said, I was very sorry, but I had a duty to perform which I could not help—he immediately turned round to the left side of him, and snatched a knife from the seat on which he was at work—it was such a knife as shoemakers use—he rose up from his seat, swore a tremendous oath, and requested me to leave the room, holding the knife up in an attitude to plunge it at me—I said, "My good man, do lay such a dangerous instrument as that down, I have not come here to do you any harm"—I requested him two or three times to lay it down—he then turned
to his seat again, and snatched up a hammer—in the interim I looked round to get something—I saw a child's cradle, and just as he was coming forward to strike me with the knife, I got it up and put it between us—he got hold of the other side of the cradle, lifted it up, and instantaneously sprang under the cradle, and made a plunge at me—I put my arm down to keep the knife from my side, and received it just above my elbow—it was a job under the cradle—I did not know the knife had entered my arm at the moment, till I perceived the blood flow—I then immediately felt a pricking pain—I turned the cradle round, and said—"You have committed a very serious act upon me,"—he made no reply—I moved the cradle round, went and seized him by the shoulder and the back of his trowsers, shoved him out of the room, and shut the door against him—I did not observe what he did with the knife—he came back again after I had closed the door, and requested to have his coat—I opened the door a little way, and perceiving he had not the knife or hammer, I allowed him to take his coat—he was in the act of going—I said he had no occasion to go, I should certainly give him into custody—he went away—I followed him, and he made a sudden rush into a house to escape—I went after him—a lad went for a policeman, who came, and I saw the prisoner taken in a watercloset, about twenty minutes after I had received the stab—I did not hear precisely what he said to the policeman, as the person in the house rescued him from me, but he said it was a very bad job, or something like it—Mr. Staddon, the doctor, has attended me—I was obliged to have my arm laid open—I am not well now, but am getting better.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Have you done every thing the doctor desired you? A. Yes—I have not been drinking spirits—the prisoner said to the policeman, when he took him, that he had done a very bad act, and he did not know how he came to do it—I did not bear him say that the knife touched my arm in the scuffle, and he did not intend to do it; but I was in such a situation that I might not hear what be said—it was not while I was struggling with him about the cradle that I felt the pain, it was just after—( looking at his deposition)—this is my handwriting.
Q. ( Reading from his deposition)—"I did not feel it for the moment, but in struggling with him after, for the cradle, I felt pain." A. That it not true—it was after I dropped the cradle that I felt pain—I did not struggle with him for the cradle—I kept him off some time, and he was trying to get the cradle from me, to strike at me—I was distraining on him for 10 s. 6 d.—I did not notice how many children there were—I think I saw two, but I would not swear it—I do not think there were four—I was so flurried while I was there, that I do not know whether they were crying or not—I put my hand on a chair when I went in, to distrain upon it, but nothing else—there was a woman in the room—I think the prisoner had a shoe on his knee—he threw it down when he rose up from his seat.
COURT. Q. Might this have been done by his using his hand to struggle for the cradle, or did you see a distinct push made at you? A. He sprang under the cradle and did it—I saw it coming, which made me put my elbow down and receive it there instead of my side.
JAMES PRETTYMAN . I am a shoemaker; the prisoner occupied one room in my house—I employed Hodgson to distrain—I was in the prisoner's room when Hodgson came in—he told the prisoner that he was very
sorry, he had got a very unpleasant office to do, but as regarded him he must perform it—the prisoner was sitting on the seat he works at—the prisoner said, "You don't mean to deprive my wife and family of my goods?"—Hodgton said he had got that job to do, and he must do it—the prisoner then said to me, "Do you mean to take my goods?"—I said, "I don't wish to take your goods, but I have given you warning to leave my house, and I must have my money"—he said his wife was gone out for some money—I said, "You have told me so often, I can't help that, you must leave the house to-day"—the prisoner got the knife to cut at Hodgson, and told him to leave the room—Hodgson told him to put such an instrument as that down—he still kept it in his hand—I flew to the door, and saw him take up the hammer, and at the same time Hodson picked up the cradle—I saw the prisoner stoop under the cradle, with the knife in his hand—I saw the knife was in his hand, as he was going under the cradle, and his arm was bent—Hodson was standing with the cradle in his hand—the knife was towards his side—I never saw the knife afterwards, but most shoemakers use a sharp-pointed knife—it was one he was at work with—I ran down stairs, and went for a policeman—I saw Hodgson again in about a quarter of an hour—his coat was then off, and his shirt was all covered with blood—the prisoner had lodged with me three months, at 2 s. 3 d. a week—10 s. 6 d. was due—he is a journeyman—I did not see Hodgson strike him at all.
JAMES CUDDY ( police-constable L 20.) I took the prisoner into custody, between three and four o'clock, on the 21st of January, in a privy—I Galled Hodgson, and asked if he was the person who committed the assault—he said, "Yes"—the prisoner said nothing—as we were going to the station-house, I said he was a foolish man to get himself into that trouble—he said it was a foolish thing, but in the irritation of the moment, seeing his children crying round him, and he having not a mouthful of victuals to eat, it so irritated him, that he did not think for the moment that the knife touched the man's arm, and it must have been in the scuffle.
JOHN STADDON . I am a surgeon. I was requested by Mr. Jeremy the Magistrate to examine Hodgson's arm, between four and five o'clock on the afternoon of the 21st of January—I discovered a wound near the elbowjoint of the left arm, produced by some sharp instrument—it appeared to have been a stab or thrust—I did not examine the depth of it then, but after it became necessary to lay the wound opts, it was discovered to be three inches deep, or more—it was above the elbow, and had passed between the integuments up towards the shoulder—I think it was such a wound as might have been made with a shoemaker's knife—it is getting well now.
MR. PHILLIPS called
ELLEN SULLIVAN . I am the wife of a carver and gilder. I was in the prisoner's room when Hodgson came in—the prisoner was at work at his bench at the time—directly Hodgson came in he seized the cradle—the prisoner jumped up at the moment, with the knife he was at work with in one hand, and a boot in the other, and seised hold of the cradle—I did not observe whether he dropped the boot—I saw them struggle for the cradle—I think, from what I saw, that the blow was quite accidental—the prisoner has four children—there were three there—they were crying—I have known the prisoner eight or nine years—he has always borne the character of an honest, peaceable, unoffending man—he has been a long time out of work.
COURT. Q. Did you see the thing happen? A. Yes—they had a struggle, and both fell on the bed—I did not see the man's arm bleeding, for as Mr. Pretty man left the room I left also—I did not know he had been hurt—I did not notice whether the prisoner kept the knife in his hand all the time they were struggling for the cradle—I did not see him stoop down under the cradle, or hold the knife up—I am sure he did not hold the knife up at all—I cannot tell how this injury was done to Hodgson, or at what time of the struggle.
(The prisoner received a good character.)
GUILTY . Aged 42.— Recommended to mercy. — Confined Twelve Months.
[trialAccount t18390204-873]
873. RICHARD HITCHCOCK was indicted for stealing, on the 7th of February, 1 gelding, price 25 l.;1 cart, value 8 l.;1 set of harness, value 2 l.;20 sacks, value 1 l. 10 s.;4 horse-cloths, value 4 s.;1 bag, value 1 s.; and 56 bushels of oats, value 9 l. 9 s.; the goods and property of James Muggeridge.
JAMES WHOWALL ( police-constable M 166.) I was in Tennis-court, King-street, Southwark, on Thursday, the 7th of February, between seven and eight o'clock in the evening—I saw a horse and cart standing still it was a very bye place, my curiosity was excited, and I hid myself where I could see the cart, but nobody could see me—in about ten minutes I saw the prisoner coming down a turning called Bowling-green-lane, and he look the mare by the reins—there was another man with him—the prisoner then took the cart down Bowling-green-lane into King-street, and on to Maze-pond—I followed them—the other man saw me, and he went away—when the prisoner got to Maze-pond, he looked round and saw me—I then went and asked where he was going—he said, "Home"—I said, "What, are you the carman?"—he said, "Yes"—I said, "Where is your whip?"—he said, "Up in the cart"—but there was no whip there—I said I should take him to the station-house—he said it was very hard, as he saw the horse and cart standing, and he was going to take it home; but he was going in the wrong direction—I asked him what was the direction on the cart—he could not tell me, but I found on it the name of Mr. Muggeridge, Tottenham Court-road—the cart was then three or four miles from there, and the prisoner was going in a contrary direction—I took the mare and cart, and my brother officer took the prisoner.
Prisoner. He asked me if I was the carman—I told him "No, but I saw the horse and cart coming down King-street,"—I knew the owner, and was going to take it home. Witness. I am sure he said he was the carman.
JOHN ALLEN ( police-constable M 87.) I was with Whowall—I took the prisoner—there was another person with him, who got away—I asked the prisoner where he found the cart—he said, "In the Borough," and shortly afterwards he said he was coming through Mermaid-court, and saw it standing, and was going to take it home—Tennis-court leads into Mermaid-court.
HENRY BRAMBLE . I am carman to Mr. James Muggeridge, a cornchandler, in Tottenham Court-road. I was out with his cart on the 7th of February—there were fourteen sacks of oats in it, worth nine guineas—I left it in Earl-street while I went into a public-house to get some refreshment—I put the nose-bags on the horses—when I came out the horse and cart and oats was gone—the oats had come from Mr. Smith's, a wharfinger, at the end of Earl-street—my master has his oats landed there, and I went
to take them home—it was about half-past six o'clock when I left it—I saw the cart and oats again in the green-yard, in| the Southwark Bridge-road, at half-past ten o'clock the same night—it was my master's—I saw the mare in the stable.
JAMES WHOWALL re-examined. I took the cart to the green-yard—I did not see any other horse and cart there—I left it about eight o'clock—the horse was put into the stable.
GUILTY . Aged.— Confined Six Months.
Before Mr. Recorder.
[trialAccount t18390204-874]
874. JOHN CARSTLE was indicted for stealing, on the 28th of January, 1 pair of boots, value 16 s., the goods of Jonathan Hall.—2nd COUNT, stating them to be the goods of William Smith.
WILLIAM SMITH . I live at Fisher's-rents, Tanner's-hill, Deptford, and work for Jonathan Hall, the landlord of the Ship and Whale, at Rotherhithe. These boots belong to me—they were made for me—my master delivered them into my hands while I was at work, and if I lost them I was to make them good—I left them on Saturday evening, the 26th of January, in my master's tool-house, and on the Monday they were gone—the prisoner had worked there, but was discharged on the Saturday.
RICHARD JAMES MJORE . I live in Grove-lane, Deptford. I saw the prisoner at a beer-shop on the 28th of January—he said he had been at tide work, but did not mean to go any more, that he had pawned his boots for 5 s., and he wanted to sell the duplicate, as he was going to tramp —I bought the duplicate, and sent a lad to get the boots out.
Prisoner. When I sold the duplicate I said I should have them again. Witness. I took them that he should not lose his work, but might have them again.
JOSEPH PHILLIPS . I live at Mr. Wilson's beer-shop—I got the duplicate from Moore, and got the boots out.
THOMAS SIMMONS . I am assistant to Mr. Greenleaf, a pawnbroker, at Deptford. The prisoner pawned these boots on the 26th of January, in the name of W. Knight, Tanner's-hill.
BENJAMIN LOVELL ( police-sergeant R 15.) I found the prisoner at a lodging-house at Tanner's-hill—he said he had pawned the boots to get more beer, but he was drunk at the time.
Prisoner. They belonged to any man that worked in the yard; as much to me as to any one.
WILLIAM SMITH re-examined. I was measured for them, when I was not using them, other men might use them, but there was only one man in the yard, besides me, who could wear them—the prisoner could not get them on.
GUILTY . Aged 28.— Confined Six Weeks.
Before Mr. Sergeant Arabin.
[trialAccount t18390204-875]
875. JAMES VINCENT MILLER was indicted for embezzlement.
PETER ALEXANDER KING KENT . I am in partnership with my brother, we are patten-makers, and live in Brook-street, Lambeth. The prisoner was in our employ as commission-agent, and to receive orders—we paid him as he obtained the orders—he was to receive money on our account—it was his duty to bring back the whole money to me, and to receive the commission—on the 25th of January I packed up some clogs to go to. Mr. Pearce, in the Walworth-road—the prisoner was to take them, and to receive 17 s.—he should have brought that to me when he returned, but he.
never returned—I did not see him again till I gave him in charge, about a fortnight after.
WILLIAM PEARCE . The prisoner solicited an order from me for some clogs. I gave him an order for a dozen pairs—he brought them on the 25th, and I paid him 17 s. on account of his master.
JAMES PEPPER ( police-constable £ 51.) I took the prisoner an the 5th of February, in Mint-street, Borough. I told him I wanted him for absconding with his master's money—he said, "I know I took the money, but it was for want of bread—my master can't make a felony of it, it is a breach of trust."
Prisoner's Defence. I took home these clogs—previous to going, I asked for sixpence to get me something to eat, which was refused—I then said, "Lend me a penny," which was refused.
MR. KENT re-examined. This is partly true, but he had been paid the commission on these clogs previous to the order going in, because he pleaded distress—on the morning he speaks of, he wanted some more, and I would not let him have it.
GUILTY . Aged 18.— Recommended to mercy. — Confined Six Weeks.
[trialAccount t18390204-876]
876. JOSEPH STOWER was indicted for stealing, on the 4th of February, 2 scarfs, value 1 l. 12 s., the goods of William Kingston and another, his masters.
MR. CLARKSON conducted the Protecution
JOSEPH HARVEY . I am in partnership with Mr. William Kingston, we are linen-drapers, and live in Westminster Bridge-road. The prisoner was our porter—be came on the 1st of December, and, as I understood, he was in very bad circumstances—we received information which lad us to suspect him, and on the evening of Monday, the 4th of February, I placed myself at a skylight from which I could have a view of that part of the shop where he and the other porter slept together—I went about eleven o'clock at night, and when I had been there some the minutes, I saw the other porter leave his bed-side, and go down stairs—I then saw the prisoner go to a distant part of the warehouse to the shawl department—he returned with something under his arm—I saw the black fringe on his white shirt, and it appeared to me to be shawls which he had—he stooped down, folded up the shawls, and seemed to have great difficulty in getting them in somewhere, I fancy it was into a pocket—I went to the station-house, and requested the assistance of an officer, and at seven o'clock the next morning the officer came to our shop, and took him into custodyMayor, our clerk, went down, and fetched up the prisoner's coat—I asked the prisoner if it was his—he said "Yes," and if he had had it that morning—he said "Yes"—the policeman took these two shawls out of the pocket of that coat.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. IS it possible you could have made any mistake about the person who did this? A. No; they are scarfs, or long shawls.
SAMUEL MAYOR . I am clerk to the prosecutor. On the morning of the 5th of February, when the prisoner was taken, I saw the officer find these scarfs in his coat-pocket.
ROBERT BRODIE ( police-constable P 132.) I went to the prosecutor's, and the prisoner was given into my custody—I took him up stain—a coat was produced, which be said was his, and these shawls were found in
it—I took him to the station-bouse, and there he began to cry, and laid he wished he had got into bed, and not taken the shawls, which were the first he had ever taken.
GUILTY . Aged 27.— Transported for Seven Years.
ADJOURNED TO MONDAY, MARCH 4, 1839.
